Near Linear Time Approximation Schemes for Clustering of Partially Doubling Metrics
Anne Driemel, Jan Höckendorff, Ioannis Psarros, Christian Sohler, Di Yue

Abstract
In the metric k k -median problem we are given a finite metric space ( X ∪ Y , 𝐝 ) (X\cup Y,\mathbf{d}) and the objective is to compute a set of k k centers C ⊆ Y C\subseteq Y that minimizes ∑ p ∈ X min c ∈ C ⁡ 𝐝 ⁡ ( p , c ) \sum_{p\in X}\min_{c\in C}\mathbf{d}(p,c) . In general metric spaces, the best polynomial time algorithm, which is due to Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson [ 19 ] , computes a ( 2 + ε ) (2+\varepsilon) -approximation for arbitrary constant ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . However, if the metric space has bounded doubling dimension, a near linear time ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximation algorithm is known due to the work of Cohen-Addad, Feldmann, and Saulpic [ 18 ] . In this paper, we show that the ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximation algorithm can be generalized to the case when either X X or Y Y has bounded doubling dimension (but the other set not). The case when X X has bounded doubling dimension is motivated by the assumption that even though X X is part of a high-dimensional space, it may be that it is close to a low-dimensional structure. The case when Y Y has bounded doubling dimension is perhaps more natural. It is motivated by specific clustering problems where the centers are low-dimensional. Specifically, our work in this setting implies the first near linear time approximation algorithm for the ( k , ℓ ) (k,\ell) -median problem under discrete Fréchet distance when ℓ \ell is constant. The latter problem is a version of the k k -median problem under Fréchet distance when the input consists of time series of z z reals and where the centers are time series of ℓ \ell reals [ 22 ] . Previously, for this problem no ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximation algorithm with running time polynomial in k k was known. We also introduce a novel complexity reduction for time series of real values that leads to a similar result for the case of discrete Fréchet distance. In order to solve the case when Y Y has a bounded doubling dimension, we introduce a form of dimension reduction that replaces points from X X by sets of points in Y Y . To solve the case when X X has a bounded doubling dimension, we generalize Talwar’s decomposition [ 44 ] of doubling metrics to our setting. The running time of our algorithms is 2 2 t ​ O ~ ​ ( n + m ) 2^{2^{t}}\tilde{O}(n+m) where t = O ⁡ ( ddim ​ log ⁡ ddim ε ) t=O(\mathrm{ddim}\log\frac{\mathrm{ddim}}{\varepsilon}) and where ddim \mathrm{ddim} is the doubling dimension of X X (resp. Y Y ). The results also extend to the metric (uncapacitated) facility location problem. We believe that our techniques are likely applicable to other problems.



1
Introduction

Partitioning of data sets according to data characteristics is one of the most fundamental problems
in data analysis and optimization. Depending on the underlying problem there are
many different variants of partitioning problems. In this paper,
we consider the metric
k
-median problem and the (closely related) metric facility location problem. The former problem
belongs to the area of unsupervised learning and is formulated as follows. We are given a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, where the set
X
consists of the data points and the set
Y
of allowed center locations. The goal is to choose a set
C
of
k
centers from
Y
such that the
\cost_{k}(X,C)=\sum_{x\in X}\min_{c\in C}\dist(x,c)
is minimized. The set
C
induces a partition of
X
into
k
sets by assigning each point to its nearest center. The resulting partitioning of
X
is also called a
clustering
. The facility location problem is closely related even though it originates from a different setting. In facility location we have a set of clients
X
and a set of possible facilities
Y
both from a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
and with each
f\in Y
there is an opening cost
\ocost(f)
associated. Every client has to be assigned to an open facility and will pay the distance to the facility as connection cost.
The objective is to find a set of facilities
F\subset Y
such that the sum of opening and connection costs is minimized, that is, we want to minimize
\sum_{x\in X}\min_{f\in F}\dist(x,f)+\sum_{f\in F}\ocost(f)
. Thus, the main difference between the two problems is, that in
k
-median clustering
the number of centers is restricted to
k
, while in facility location we may open as many centers as we want, but we need to pay for it. Otherwise, both objective functions minimize the sum of distances of points from
X
to their nearest centers.

Neither problem admits
a polynomial time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm
for arbitrary small constant
\varepsilon>0

under standard complexity theoretical assumptions. In fact, the
k
-median problem cannot be approximated better than
1+2/e

[
34
]
and the facility location problem not better than
1.463

[
27
,
34
]
.
At the same time, both problems can be fairly well approximated. For the
k
-median problem the best possible approximation algorithm achieves a factor
2+\varepsilon
approximation
[
19
]
and for the facility location problem, the best known approximation factor is 1.488
[
38
]
. Interestingly,
both problems can be approximated up to a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
, if the underlying metric space has bounded doubling dimension
[
18
]
.

In this paper, we raise the question whether a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation can also be achieved, if only one of the sets has bounded doubling dimension, but the other set is high-dimensional. This setting has been studied before in the context of nearest neighbor search
[
33
,
30
]
and Euclidean facility location
[
32
]
. In this paper, we give the first near-linear time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithms for facility location and
k
-median in these settings.

We then observe that other known clustering problems have centers coming from a space that is low-dimensional: An example is the

(k,\ell)
-median clustering problem
[
22
]
of time series data
under the discrete Fréchet distance. The Fréchet distance is a standard distance measure for polygonal curves.
These curves can be represented as a time series of points. The Fréchet distance is especially suited for comparing series of different complexity (number of points in the time series). We will present in Section
9
how one can solve the above problem using our algorithm for doubling metrics. In the
(k,\ell)
-median problem the length of the center time series is restricted to a constant
\ell
, which essentially means that the space of center time series is doubling (albeit it does not imply that the space of clients is doubling).

We then apply our algorithm to get the first
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for this problem with a near linear running time (for constant
\ell
) while all previous algorithms were exponential
in
k

[
8
,
42
]
.
For the case of one-dimensional ambient space we also give a more direct algorithm that is based on a new complexity reduction method for the discrete Fréchet distance
that reduces the problem to the case of bounded doubling dimension.
We further investigate the reverse setting when the data points are low-dimensional and the candidate center set is high dimensional.
Such a setting may arise when high-dimensional data is located on or near a low-dimensional structure, which is
a common assumption, for example, in the field of manifold learning. We show that also in the reverse setting we obtain an almost linear time approximation scheme.

This paper is a direct follow up to the work of Driemel et al.
[
21
]
that introduced a complexity reduction for time series and presented a near linear time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for
(k,\ell)
-median for time series. This paper strictly improves both results by giving an explicit bound to the complexity reduction and generalizing the
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to polygonal curves of arbitrary ambient dimension.

1.1
Problem Definitions

In this section we introduce the problems we consider.
We start by defining the metric facility location problem.
Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
be a metric space.
Input to the facility location problem is a set of
n
data points (or clients)
X
and a set of
m
candidate facilities
Y
. Every facility
f\in Y
is assigned with an opening cost
\ocost(f)>0
.
The goal is to output a set of facilities
F\subseteq Y
, such that

\floc(X,F):=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)+\sum_{f\in F}\ocost(f)

is minimized, where
\dist(x,F):=\min_{f\in F}\dist(x,f)
.
The optimal facility location value is denoted by
\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y):=\min_{F\subseteq Y}\floc(X,F)
.

In the metric
k
-median problem, our input is also a set of
n
data points
X
and a set of
m
candidate centers
Y
. The objective is to find a subset
F\subseteq Y
of centers such that

\cost_{k}(X,F):=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)

is minimized over all sets
F\subseteq Y

of cardinality
k
.
The optimal
k
-median value is denoted by
\opt_{k}(X,Y):=\min_{F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k}\cost_{k}(X,F)
.

Throughout this paper, unless stated otherwise, we assume that the distances in
X\cup Y
can be computed in constant time.
We remark that in a setting where this is not the case and we require time
T
to compute a distance between two points in
X\cup Y
then we get an additional factor of
T
in the running time.
It is known that
if the metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
has bounded doubling dimension, we have a near linear time approximation algorithm
[
18
]
.
In this paper we study the problem variant, where just one of the two sets
X
and
Y
has bounded doubling dimension. Perhaps surprisingly, we show that in these cases we can still obtain a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm.
Since one can
O(1)
-approximate the doubling dimension of a metric in
2^{O(\ddim)}n\log n
time
[
31
]
, we
will assume that our algorithms are given an upper bound
\ddim
on the doubling dimension.

1.1.1
Application: The
(k,\ell)
-Median Problem under Discrete Fréchet Distance

We would like to apply our results to a geometric variant of
k
-median clustering that we will define in the following.
We denote a polygonal curve
\pi
with vertices
p_{1},\ldots,p_{z}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\pi=\langle p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{z}\rangle
. The
complexity
of a polygonal curve is the number of points of the sequence defining it. The set of all polygonal curves of complexity
z
with vertices in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
.
A
traversal

T
between a polygonal curve of complexity
z
and a polygonal curve of complexity
\ell
is a sequence of index pairs

T=(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}),\dots,(i_{t},j_{t})

such that the following conditions hold:

i)

(i_{1},j_{1})=(1,1)
,

ii)

(i_{t},j_{t})=(z,\ell)
, and for each
1\leq r<t
,

iii)

i_{r+1}-i_{r}\in\{0,1\}
,

iv)

j_{r+1}-j_{r}\in\{0,1\}
,

v)

(i_{r+1}-i_{r})+(j_{r+1}-j_{r})\geq 1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{z,\ell}
be the set of all traversals between polygonal curves of complexity
z
and polygonal curves of complexity
\ell
.

Definition 1.1
.

The
discrete Fréchet distance
between
\pi=\langle p_{1},\ldots,p_{z}\rangle
and
\tau=\langle q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
is defined as:

\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)=\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{z,\ell}}\max_{(i,j)\in T}\|p_{i}-q_{j}\|_{2}.

We consider the
(k,\ell)
-median problem for clustering under the discrete Fréchet distance that has been introduced in
[
22
]
in the context of the continuous Fréchet distance.
The problem is a variant of the
k
-median problem under Fréchet distance, where the complexity of the center polygonal curves is restricted to be at most
\ell
.

Definition 1.2

((
k,\ell)
-median clustering problem)
.

Given a set of polygonal curves
\Pi\subset\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
and parameters
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, compute a set
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
,
|\mathcal{C}|=k
,
that minimizes

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}}\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau).

We remark that one may invariantly define the centers to have complexity at most
\ell
, instead of exactly
\ell
. This does not change the problem, since every polygonal curve with complexity fewer than
\ell
can be extended to a polygonal curve with
\ell
vertices by repeating the first element of the polygonal curve without affecting the discrete Fréchet distance.

1.2
Our Results

In the following section, we present our results. We first show that for the
k
-median and the facility location
problem there are near-linear time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithms when the set of facilities
Y
has bounded doubling dimension. This extends previous results that were restricted to the case that
X\cup Y
has bounded doubling dimension.
The theorem below summarizes Theorems
5.1
and
7.1
and is proven in the corresponding sections.

Theorem 1.3
.

There are randomized algorithms that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m,\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
, compute a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of the
k
-median and facility location problem in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

We then show a similar result for the case when the set of clients
X
has bounded doubling dimension. We keep the theorems separated since the underlying techniques are different. The following theorem summarizes Theorems
6.1
and
8.1
and is proven in the corresponding sections.

Theorem 1.4
.

There are randomized algorithms that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m,\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, compute a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of the
k
-median and facility location problem in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

We apply our result to the
(k,\ell)
-median problem under discrete Fréchet distance that is the first near linear time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for this problem when
\ell
and
d
(the dimension of the ambient space) are constant. All prior algorithms were exponential in
k
.

Theorem 1.5
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,z,k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
P\subset\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
with
|P|=n

computes a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution to the
(k,\ell)
-median problem in time
2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(d\ell nz)
with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(d\ell\log\frac{d\ell}{\varepsilon}\right).

1.3
Technical Overview

In the following we give an overview of the main ideas of our results.
There are two conceptual ideas, one related to the case of low-dimensional centers, the other one related to low-dimensional clients.
In the setting of low-dimensional centers, we develop a form of dimension reduction which represents every high-dimensional client by a set of points in the low-dimensional space.
In the setting of low-dimensional clients, our main contribution is a new hierarchical decomposition that generalizes Talwar’s decomposition
[
44
]
to partially doubling metric spaces.
Both ideas are independent of the concrete problems we study and we therefore believe that there is high potential to apply them to other problems. Once we have established these main ideas, there is the technical challenge to integrate them into the dynamic programming approach from
[
18
]
. This requires overcoming several technical problems to deal with our setting.

We start by describing the case of low-dimensional centers. We illustrate our ideas on the facility location problem.
For simplicity, we will assume uniform opening costs.
The approach to
k
-median is similar.
Finally, we discuss an application to the
(k,\ell)
-median clustering problem under discrete Fréchet distance.

1.3.1
Low-dimensional Centers

We consider the case where the point set
X
is in an arbitrary metric space and
Y
has bounded doubling dimension
\ddim
. A similar setting has been considered before in
[
26
]
where the authors develop approximation algorithms for finding the smallest set to be removed to get a set of bounded doubling dimension and where they show that one can compute an approximate minimum spanning tree and other proximity structures when
O(\sqrt{n})
points are not doubling.
A simple illustrative example is to have a point set
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with Euclidean distance and a fixed
2
-dimensional plane
Y
that is supposed to contain the centers.
In this example, the set
Y
will be unbounded (one can usually discretize such a space to obtain a set of candidates that contains a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation).

Dimension reduction.

A simple idea to solve this special case is to project all points from
X
to the plane
Y
and solve the resulting low-dimensional problem. Such an approach will result in a constant approximation as the distance to
Y
as argued in the following. For the analysis we can think of the projection as moving all points to
Y
. By the triangle inequality, this will change the cost of any solution by at most the sum of distances the points have been moved. Since this sum is also a lower bound on the connection cost of any solution to the high-dimensional input, we get that any solution on the projection is a constant approximation.
For similar reasons approximating the distances from
x
to
y\in Y
by
\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))+\dist(\pi_{Y}(x),y)
, where
\pi_{Y}(x)
denotes the point from
Y
closest to
x
, only gives a constant approximation. Instead of replacing
x
by a single point, our new idea is to replace
x
by a set of points
N_{x}
(which we call
proxies
), which is an
\varepsilon\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))
-net of a ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))/\varepsilon)\subseteq Y
around
\pi_{Y}(x)
of radius
\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))/\varepsilon
.
(Formally, a
\rho
-net of a set
B
is a subset
N\subseteq B
, such that 1) the interpoint distances in
N
are at least
\rho
, and
2) every point in
B
has a nearby point in
N
within distance
\rho
.
)
Taking
\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)
for
y\in Y
we approximate all distances within a factor of

(1+\varepsilon)
as summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. For every
x\in X
let

N_{x}
be an
\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,Y)
-net of
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
with
\pi_{Y}(x)\in N_{x}
.
For all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
define

\widehat{\dist}(x,y)=\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y).

(1)

Then we have

\dist(x,y)\leq\widehat{\dist}(x,y)\leq(1+4\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,y).

We combine the above dimension reduction idea with
the algorithm of
[
18
]
.
We start from a standard hierarchical decomposition for doubling metrics by Talwar
[
44
]
, which is an analogue to the randomly-shifted quadtree in doubling metrics.
The decomposition
\mathcal{H}
is constructed on top of
Y
, and has
L=O(\log m)
levels.
For each
0\leq\ell\leq L
, level
\ell
of
\mathcal{H}
, denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, is a partition of
Y
into clusters of diameter at most
2^{\ell}
,
and
\mathcal{H}_{\ell-1}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
.

\mathcal{H}
can be represented as a tree, where each node corresponds to a cluster and has
2^{O(\ddim)}
children.
In
[
18
]
, a dynamic program is run on
\mathcal{H}
to compute a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for facility location.
In our setting, one immediate issue is that clients in
X
are not directly defined on
\mathcal{H}
, but are replaced by a proxy set
N_{x}\subseteq Y
.
Therefore, it is not immediately clear that one can follow a similar approach as
[
18
]
.

For the sake of presentation,
let us first have a brief review of
the argument of
[
18
]
, for the case
X=Y
.
They use the notion of
portals
, which originates from
[
4
]
.
Roughly speaking, the portal set
P_{C}
for a cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
is an
\varepsilon 2^{\ell}
-net of
C
.
The level
\ell
portals are the union of
P_{C}
over
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
.
For a pair of points
x,y\in Y
, the actual distance between
x
and
y
will be replaced by the
portal-respecting distance

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)
, which is the length of the
portal-respecting path
between
x
and
y
.
Specifically, let
\ell
be the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is
cut
w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
, i.e., the highest level where
x
and
y
fall into different clusters.
The portal-respecting path between
x
and
y
starts from
x
(a portal at level
0
), each step connecting the current portal to the closest portal at one level up, until reaching a portal
p
at level
\ell
.
Portal
p
is then connected to another portal
q
at the same level, and the path goes all the way down from
q
to
y
.
It can be shown that the portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)
is upper bounded by
\dist(x,y)+O(\varepsilon)2^{\ell}
.
A number of techniques are developed in
[
18
]
to bound
\ell
, the level where
\{x,y\}
is
cut
.
Finally, a dynamic program is run on
\mathcal{H}
w.r.t. portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
, where each entry of the DP table is encoded by a cluster
C
of
\mathcal{H}
, and a configuration indicating how
C
interacts with other clusters via its portals
P_{C}
.

Integrating the dimension reduction with portal-respecting distance.

In our setting,
if we would like to combine our approach with the algorithm of
[
18
]
,
the first step is to combine our dimension reduction idea with the portal-respecting distance.
This is done in a rather straightforward way, by modifying (
1
) to

\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)=\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,y).

Now, a main challenge would be bounding the error incurred by the portal-respecting distance, namely,
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)
.
In
[
18
]
, this can be done by bounding the highest cutting level for a single pair of points
\{x,y\}
.
However, since
x
is now represented by
N_{x}
, we have to bound the cutting level of
\{u,y\}
for every
u\in N_{x}
, which becomes more involved.
Nevertheless, we argue that the above error can still be effectively bounded, by only considering the cutting level for two sets
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
and
\{\pi_{Y}(x),y\}
.
Specifically, if
j
is the highest level where
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
is cut, and
\ell
is the highest level where
\{\pi_{Y}(x),y\}
is cut, then we show that

\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)\leq O(\varepsilon)2^{\max\{j,\ell\}}.

Dynamic program.

We further integrate the dimension reduction with the dynamic program framework in
[
18
]
.
The algorithm is run on
\mathcal{H}
.
Whenever we compute the connection cost from a client
x\in X
to some set of facilities
F\subseteq Y
, we replace
\dist(x,F)
with
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
.
Two challenges come with such a replacement.
First, recall that
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
depends on
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
for all proxies
u\in N_{x}
;
therefore, to compute it we have to enumerate all
u\in N_{x}
, which can be done only when
N_{x}
is entirely contained in some cluster
C
.
This is different from
[
18
]
, where the connection cost of
x
can be trivially computed at the leaf node
\{x\}
.
Second, even if we successfully define such a cluster
C
which contains
N_{x}
, it is unclear how we access the distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
.

To resolve the first issue, we find for every
x\in X
a suitable cluster
C(x)
that entirely contains
N_{x}
in the preprocessing stage, and “defer” the computation of
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
to cluster
C(x)
.
More concretely, for a client
x\in X
and a cluster
C
on
\mathcal{H}
, we say
x
is
revealed
in
C
if
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)\subseteq C
, and say
x
is
newly revealed
in
C
, denoted
C(x)=C
, if
C
is the lowest level cluster where
x
is revealed.
Then each DP table entry
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C})
is defined to be the minimum
revealed facility location cost
of
C
, given the configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}
, i.e.,

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C}):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\colon\text{$F$ is consistent}\\
\text{with }\mathbf{a}_{C}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{x\in X\colon x\text{ is revealed in }C}\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)+\ocost(F\cap C)\right\}.

Since all clients are revealed in the largest cluster (root node)
Y
, the root node stores the optimal facility location cost.

For the second issue,
let us consider how the DP table is updated.
The revealed cost of
C
can be decomposed into two parts —
(1) the total revealed cost of
C
’s child clusters, and
(2) the
newly revealed cost
of
C
, i.e.,
\sum_{x\colon C(x)=C}\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
.
The first part can be obtained from the corresponding table entries of
C
’s children.
For the second part, the key observation is that if
x
is newly revealed in
C
(
C(x)=C
), then
the proxy set of
x
is entirely contained in the portal set of
C
, i.e.,
N_{x}\subseteq P_{C}
.
Therefore, for each
u\in N_{x}
,
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
can be directly obtained from the configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}
which has the information of the connection of
P_{C}
, and thus
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
can be effectively computed.

Our final algorithm has a running time of

2^{2^{t}}\tilde{O}(n+m)
where
t=O(\mathrm{ddim}\log\frac{\mathrm{ddim}}{\varepsilon})
.
We remark that this is faster than the algorithm of
[
18
]
, which has
t^{2}
instead of
t
in the (double) exponent. This improvement comes from the fact that one can improve the analysis of their hierarchical composition
[
18
]
.
The fact, that such an improvement is possible had been observed in
[
12
]
referring to the paper
[
1
]
.
In the present paper, we provide a self-contained proof of this fact in Lemma
2.5
.

1.3.2
Low-dimensional Clients

Next we consider the case when
X
has bounded doubling dimension
\ddim
and
Y
not.
One may wonder if the same
approach for the case when
Y
is low-dimensional applies here.
However, due to the asymmetric nature of the problem it is unclear how we could benefit from the dimension reduction in this case. Intuitively, this can be seen by the fact that “moving” a facility/center can be much more costly than moving a client.
Indeed, if we move a point in
X
by a distance
z
we change the cost of any solution by at most
z
. Moving a point in
Y
can change the cost of a solution by as much as
|X|\cdot z
, since we could potentially assign every point in
X
to the same point in
Y
.

Thus, it is unclear how to apply the previous approach.

Our new decomposition.

For the reason above, we extend Talwar’s hierarchical decomposition to the case that only a subset of the metric space is doubling.
We start from constructing Talwar’s decomposition on top of
X
; denote the resulting decomposition by
\mathcal{H}
.
It then remains to decide how to add the points in
Y
to
\mathcal{H}
.
Intuitively, we want that every point
y\in Y
always lies in the same cluster as
\pi_{X}(y)
, the nearest neighbor of
y
in
X
.
More concretely, our plan is to assign to every
y\in Y
a suitable level
0\leq h(y)\leq L
, and add
y
to
\mathcal{H}
in such a way that

(a)

\{y\}
is a leaf node at level
h(y)
; and

(b)

y
and
\pi_{X}(y)
are in the same cluster at levels higher than (or equal to)
h(y)+1
.

We call such cluster
\{y\}
an
ornament
at level
h(y)
.
After adding all
y\in Y
to
\mathcal{H}
, we obtain a hierarchical decomposition for
X\cup Y
, denoted by
\mathcal{P}
.

It could be tricky to define for every ornament
\{y\}
the level
h(y)
to which it should be attached as a leaf.
On the one hand, we want
h(y)
to be sufficiently large, so that
y
can be covered by some level
h(y)
portal (i.e.,
\dist(y,P_{C})\leq\varepsilon 2^{h(y)}
for the cluster
C\ni y
).
Therefore, we can extend the definition of portal-respecting path to
\mathcal{P}
, and define portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
on
\mathcal{P}
the same way as
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)
.
On the other hand, we want
h(y)
to be sufficiently small, so that the error incurred by
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
, namely
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)=O(\varepsilon)2^{h(y)}
, is negligible.

As a first attempt, consider choosing
h(y)=\log(\dist(y,X)/\varepsilon)
.
Then we have
\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))\leq\varepsilon 2^{h(y)}
,
and we can further argue that
y
is within distance
\varepsilon 2^{h(y)+1}
to some level
h(y)+1
portal.
Therefore, the portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
can be defined the same way as
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)
.
However, this choice of
h(y)
becomes problematic in terms of the error
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)
.
Consider an arbitrary point
x\in X
, and assume
h(y)
is the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut.
Then the error is
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)=O(\varepsilon)2^{h(y)}=O(\dist(y,X))
, which is too large to afford.

To resolve the issue, we choose
h(y)
as a slightly smaller value
\log(\dist(y,X)/\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.
For this choice, the error becomes
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)=O(\varepsilon)2^{h(y)}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\dist(y,X)
,
which is at most
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\dist(x,y)
and thus can be charged to
\dist(x,y)
.
The tradeoff is that
y
is no longer
\varepsilon 2^{h(y)+1}
-covered by the level
h(y)+1
portal set.
Instead, the covering radius becomes
\sqrt{\varepsilon}2^{h(y)+1}
.
Nonetheless, we can still define
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
similarly under this weaker covering property, and it does not change the
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\dist(x,y)
error bound above when the cutting level is exactly
h(y)
.
We obtain a weaker error bound of
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})2^{\ell}
only if the
cutting level
\ell
is strictly greater than
h(y)
.
In this case, the question reduces to finding the highest cutting level of
\{x,\pi_{X}(y)\}
, which can be answered fairly well using techniques in
[
18
]
and the previous section, since both points are in
X
.

Dynamic program.

Our new hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{P}
and the portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
can then be combined with the dynamic program of Cohen-Addad et al.
[
18
]
to get our result in the case when the clients are low-dimensional.
This seems difficult at first, because each cluster on
\mathcal{P}
now has an unbounded number of child clusters, mainly due to the newly added ornaments.
A naive enumeration of the configurations of these ornament children would blow up the time complexity of the DP.

Perhaps surprisingly, we show that we can avoid doing the enumeration for ornaments, and it suffices to only enumerate the configurations of non-ornament children, the number of which is bounded by
2^{O(\ddim)}
.
Our key observation is that ornaments must be candidate facilities, and thus only the opening cost needs to be computed.
Therefore, we can obtain from portals of non-ornament children the information which ornaments are potentially required to be opened as a facility.
Once we have this information, we simply select the smallest set of ornaments that serve all the unserved portals.
This can be done via solving a set cover problem with a bounded universe.

1.3.3
Applications to the
(k,\ell)
-Median Problem under Discrete Fréchet Distance

We next discuss how to apply our results to the
(k,\ell)
-median problem under discrete Fréchet distance.
It is a folklore result that the doubling dimension of the metric space
(\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d},\dist_{dF})
, i.e.
the space of polygonal curves of
z
points in
d
-dimensional space equipped with the discrete Fréchet distance
1
1

1

Technically, we consider equivalence classes of curves of pairwise discrete Fréchet distance
0
to obtain a proper metric space.
, is
\Theta(dz)
. This implies that for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem the space of center candidates has bounded doubling dimension (for constant
\ell
and
d
). However, it also has an infinite number of points. Thus, we have to compute a discrete subset of that space that contains a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation and is not too large. In order to do so we make use of a result by Filtser et al.
[
24
]
from the context of nearest neighbor search. Applying
Theorem

1.3
, we obtain the following result.

See
1.5

Complexity reduction.

For the case of the ambient space being one-dimensional we develop a new complexity reduction for the discrete Fréchet distance that can be summarized as follows.
This provides an alternative approach that allows to apply the work of Cohen-Addad et al.
[
18
]
in a more direct way for this special case.

We believe that this complexity reduction is of independent interest. For example, it allows us to get an improved coreset construction for clustering under the discrete Fréchet distance.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be constants. Given an input time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
Algorithm
6
computes in time
O(z\ell\log^{2}z)+O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{2z^{\prime}}
with
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})

a time series
z\in\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}})
s.t. for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,

(1-\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)\leq\dist_{dF}(z,y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y).

Our idea for the complexity reduction can be described as follows.
As a first (simple) step, we show that one can reduce the number of distinct values appearing in a time series of complexity
z
to
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
while maintaining the distance to any time series of complexity
\ell
up to a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
.

Then we observe that the discrete Fréchet distance between two time series
x
and
y
, each of fixed complexity, can be written as a minimum over all traversals. Our goal is to describe the function minimizing over all traversals with a function minimizing over a much smaller set.
During each traversal, every value
y_{i}
of
y
is matched to a subsequence of
x
. In order to determine the Fréchet distance, it suffices to know the minimum and maximum value of
x
matched to
y_{i}
.
As it turns out, each traversal can equivalently (but not uniquely!) be described by remembering a sequence of constraints that consist of the minimum and maximum value matched to each
y_{i}
. Furthermore, the function minimizing over the set of all possible traversals can likewise be described by minimizing over all possible ordered constraint sets.
Since we have reduced the number of different values of the time series to
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
, the number of different constraint sets is small.
The set of constraints
will be called an
\ell
-profile (see Figure
2
for an example).
It is important to note that the set of all
\ell
-profiles
of a time series
x
with values from a fixed set
X

completely determines the Fréchet distance
to any time series of complexity
\ell
.
Since there are only a constant number of different sets of
\ell
-profiles (where the constant depends on
\ell
and
\varepsilon
) for any time series
x
with
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
distinct values, we can replace
x
by the shortest time series
x^{\prime}
over the same set of values that has the same set of
\ell
-profiles.
The length of the shortest such time series is a constant that depends on the set of profiles and the number of distinct values of the time series.
Since the number of profiles is also a constant depending on
\varepsilon
and
\ell
, the maximum length of these shortest time series is constant as well. We can compute such a time series using a dynamic programming approach. The resulting time series have length
O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})
.

1.4
Further Related Work

The
k
-median problem in metric spaces is known to be hard to approximate with a factor better than
1+2/e
unless set cover can be approximated within a factor
c\ln n
for
c<1

[
34
]
.
A number of different constant factor polynomial time approximation algorithms are known
[
13
,
35
,
5
,
29
,
20
,
41
,
15
,
45
]
, and the currently best approximation ratio is
(2+\varepsilon)

[
19
]
.
In the Euclidean plane, the problem is NP-hard
[
40
]
.
The first polynomial time approximation scheme for
k
-median in the Euclidean plane has been developed by Arora et al.
[
3
]
and later improved to near-linear time in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
when
d
is constant
[
36
]
. This result has been generalized to metric spaces of bounded doubling dimension
[
45
]

and later to a near-linear approximation scheme
[
18
]
.

The metric (uncapacitated) facility location problem can be approximated within a constant factor
[
43
,
14
,
35
,
41
,
45
,
39
,
11
]
, and the currently best polynomial time approximation algorithm achieves an approximation guarantee of 1.488
[
38
]
. At the same time, there is a conditional lower bound of
1.463
on the best possible approximation
[
27
,
34
]
.
In the constant-dimensional Euclidean setting, there are similar results as for the
k
-median problem
[
36
,
44
,
18
]
.

Driemel et al.
[
22
]
defined the
(k,\ell)
-clustering problem for time series as follows: Given a set
P
of
n
time series of complexity
m
and parameters
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
find
k
center time series of complexity
\ell
, such that (a) the maximum distance of an element in
P
to its closest center time series or (b) the sum of these distances is minimized. Variant (a) is referred to as
(k,\ell)
-center and (b) as
(k,\ell)
-median. Under the continuous Fréchet distance, they developed near-linear time
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithms for both clustering variants, assuming
\varepsilon,k
, and
\ell
are constants. They complement these algorithmic results with hardness results, showing that both
(k,\ell)
-median and
(k,\ell)
-center are NP-hard under continuous Fréchet distance. Approximating
(k,\ell)
-median for polygonal curves in arbitrary dimensions was recently studied in
[
9
]
. Cheng and Huang give the first
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for
(k,\ell)
-median under continuous Fréchet distance in
d>1

[
16
]
. Both clustering problems are also NP-hard under the discrete Fréchet distance and even for the case
k=1

[
7
]

[
8
]
.
Buchin et al. developed the first
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for
(k,\ell)
-median under discrete Fréchet distance, which runs in
\tilde{O}((1/\varepsilon k\ell)^{k\ell}\cdot k\ell nm^{(k\ell+1)})
time
[
8
]
. Nath and Taylor
[
42
]
improved this to
\tilde{O}(nm2^{O(k/\varepsilon\log(k/\varepsilon))}\cdot(\ell/\varepsilon^{2})^{O(kl)})
.
Buchin and Rohde
[
10
]
designed the first coreset construction for
(k,\ell)
-median under both variants of the Fréchet distance, where the size of the coreset has logarithmic dependence on the number of input curves.
Recently, Cohen-Addad et al. introduced a coreset construction for
(k,\ell)
-median under discrete Fréchet distance that has size independent of the number of input curves
[
17
]
.

Related to our dimension reduction are some data structures for approximate nearest neighbor search under discrete Fréchet distance
[
23
,
25
,
24
]
. In the asymmetric setting where the query time series has complexity
\ell
, the data structures cited above replace each input time series by a set of lower dimensional time series. This is fundamentally different from our dimension reduction, which replaces each time series with exactly one lower dimensional time series.

2
Preliminaries

Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
.
For a point
x\in X\cup Y
and
r>0
, define the
ball
centered at
x
with radius
r
to be
B(x,r):=\{y\in X\cup Y\colon\dist(x,y)\leq r\}
.
The
r
-neighborhood
of a subset
C\subseteq X\cup Y
is defined as
B(C,r):=\bigcup_{x\in C}B(x,r)
.
For a subset
S\subseteq X\cup Y
, denote
B_{S}(x,r):=B(x,r)\cap S
.
Denote the
diameter
of
S
to be
\diam(S):=\max_{x,y\in S}\dist(x,y)
.
The
aspect ratio
of
S
is defined as the ratio between the largest and smallest inter-point distances of
S
, denoted as
\Delta(S):=\frac{\max_{x,y\in S}\dist(x,y)}{\min_{x,y\in S}\dist(x,y)}
.
For a set
S\subseteq X\cup Y
and a point
x\in X\cup Y
let
\pi_{S}(x)
be a point in
S
that is closest to
x
, i.e.
\pi_{S}(x)\in\argmin_{y\in S}\dist(x,y)
.
Denote
\dist(x,S):=\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))
as the
distance
from
x
to point set
S
.

Definition 2.1

(Doubling dimension
[
28
]
)
.

The
doubling dimension
of a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)

is the smallest
t\geq 0
such that every metric ball can be covered by at most
2^{t}
balls of half the radius.
The doubling dimension of a point set
S\subseteq X\cup Y
is
the doubling dimension of the metric space
(S,\dist)
,
and is denoted
\ddim(S)
.

Definition 2.2

(Packing, covering and net)
.

Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
and a subset
S\subseteq X\cup Y
.
For
\rho>0
,
S
is
\rho
-packing
if
\forall u,v\in S
,
\dist(u,v)\geq\rho
.

S
is
\rho
-covering for
X\cup Y
if for every
x\in X\cup Y
, there exists
u\in S
such that
\dist(x,u)\leq\rho
.

S
is called a
\rho
-net of
X\cup Y
if it is both
\rho
-packing and
\rho
-covering for
X\cup Y
.

Lemma 2.3

(Packing Property
[
28
]
)
.

If
S
is
\rho
-packing, then
|S|\leq(\diam(S)/\rho)^{O(\ddim(S))}
.

2.1
Hierarchical Decomposition of Doubling Metrics

In this section, we review the hierarchical decomposition for doubling metrics,
first introduced by
[
44
]
.
Let
(X,\dist)
be a metric space with doubling dimension
\ddim
.
Without loss of generality, assume the minimum interpoint distance of
X
is
1
and that the diameter of
X
is
\Delta
.
Let
L=\lceil\log\Delta\rceil+4=O(\log\Delta)
.
Construct a sequence of
nested nets
on
X
:

X=N_{0}\supseteq N_{1}\dots\supseteq N_{L},

such that for
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
N_{\ell}
is a
2^{\ell-2}
-net of
X
.
Specifically,
N_{0}=X
and
N_{L}
contains only one point in
X
.
The hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
is constructed in
Algorithm

1
.

Input:

finite metric space
(X,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, parameter
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{2})

construct the nested net
X=N_{0}\supseteq N_{1}\dots\supseteq N_{L}
, such that for
0\leq\ell\leq L
,

N_{\ell}
is a
2^{\ell-2}
-net of
X

1

sample
\alpha\sim U(\frac{1}{2},1)

2

sample a random permutation
\sigma
over
X

3

let
\mathcal{H}_{L}\leftarrow\{X\}

4

for

\ell=L-1,L-2,\dots,0

do

5

let
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\leftarrow\emptyset

6

for

C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell+1}
with
|C|\geq 1

do

7

for

u\in N_{\ell}

do

8

let
C_{u}:=(C\cap B_{X}(u,\alpha 2^{\ell}))\setminus\bigcup_{v\in N_{\ell}\colon\sigma(v)<\sigma(u)}B_{X}(v,\alpha 2^{\ell})

be a child cluster of
C

9

let
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\cup\{C_{u}\}

10

for

0\leq\ell\leq L

do

11

for

C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}

do

12

construct a
\rho 2^{\ell}
-net
P_{C}
as the portal set of
C\cap X

13

return

\mathcal{H}:=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{L}\}

Algorithm 1

Hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}

[
44
]

Definition 2.4
.

For a set
T\subseteq X
,
say
T
is
cut
at level
\ell
w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
, if there
exists a cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, such that
T\cap C\neq\emptyset
and
T\setminus C\neq\emptyset
.

We summarize the properties of
\mathcal{H}
below.

Lemma 2.5

(Hierarchical decomposition
[
44
,
18
]
)
.

Consider a metric space
(X,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
.
Let
\mathcal{H}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{L}\}
be the hierarchical decomposition returned by
Algorithm

1
when given as input
X
.
Then
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

For
0\leq\ell\leq L
and
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
,
\diam(C)\leq 2^{\ell+1}
.

(2)

Every cluster
C
has at most
2^{O(\ddim)}
child clusters.

(3)

There exists a universal constant
c>0
, such that for every point set
T\subseteq X
,

\Pr[T\text{ is cut at level }\ell\text{ w.r.t. }\mathcal{H}]\leq\frac{c\cdot\ddim\cdot\diam(T)}{2^{\ell}}.

(2)

(4)

Portals: For
0\leq\ell\leq L
, every cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
comes with a portal set
P_{C}\subseteq X
, which satisfies

(a)

Bounded size:
|P_{C}|\leq\rho^{-O(\ddim)}
.

(b)

Covering: Every
x\in C
has
\dist(x,P_{C})\leq\rho 2^{\ell}
.

(c)

Nested: If
p\in P_{C^{\prime}}\cap C
for some
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1}
, then
p\in P_{C}
.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{H}
can be computed in time
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n)\log\Delta
.

Remark
.

In
[
44
]
, the cutting probability bound is proposed for a pair of points.
Indeed, it can be generalized to arbitrary subsets of
X
as
2
.
Similar results can also be found in e.g.
[
1
]
.

[
18
]
also considered the cutting probability for arbitrary subsets, but
their bound in
[
18
, Lemma 9]
has an exponential dependence in
\ddim
.
We note that this dependence can be improved to linear.
This will slightly improve the running time of the final algorithm.

In our final algorithm, we will set parameter
\rho
to be
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.

Proof.

Properties
(1)
,
(2)
and
(4)
are stated in
[
44
, Section 3]
, and the time complexity is given in
[
18
, Lemma 9]
,
thus we omit the proof here.

For property
(3)
, let us first consider the probability that
T
is cut at level
\ell
but not at level
\ell+1
.
If
\diam(T)>2^{\ell+2}
, then by property
(1)
,
T
must be cut at level
\ell+1
, and this probability is
0
.
We assume
\diam(T)\leq 2^{\ell+2}
below.

Say
T
is cut by
v\in N_{\ell}
, if among all net points with
\min_{x\in T}\dist(u,x)\leq\alpha 2^{\ell}
and
\max_{y\in T}\dist(u,y)>\alpha 2^{\ell}
,
v
is the one with the minimum index
\sigma(v)
.
Sort all points in
N_{\ell}\cap B_{X}(T,2^{\ell})
as
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{s}
, such that
\dist(u_{1},T)\leq\dist(u_{2},T)\leq\dots\leq\dist(u_{s},T)
.
Note that for
j\in[s]
,

T
is cut by
u_{j}
only if
\min_{x\in T}\dist(u,x)\leq\alpha 2^{\ell}
and
\max_{y\in T}\dist(u,y)>\alpha 2^{\ell}
, and
\forall i<j,\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)
.
Since
\alpha
and
\sigma
are independent, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[T\text{ is cut by }u_{j}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\min_{x\in T}\dist(u,x)\leq\alpha 2^{\ell}\text{ and }\max_{y\in T}\dist(u,y)>\alpha 2^{\ell}\right]\cdot\Pr[\sigma(i)>\sigma(j),\ \forall i<j]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\frac{\min_{x\in T}\dist(u,x)}{2^{\ell}}\leq\alpha<\frac{\max_{y\in T}\dist(u,y)}{2^{\ell}}\right]\cdot\Pr[\sigma(i)>\sigma(j),\ \forall i<j]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{y\in T}\dist(u,y)-\min_{x\in T}\dist(u,x)}{2^{\ell-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\diam(T)}{2^{\ell-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{j}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\qquad\Pr[T\text{ is cut at level }\ell\text{ but not at level }\ell+1]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\Pr[T\text{ is cut by }u_{j}]\leq\frac{\diam(T)}{2^{\ell-1}}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{s}\frac{1}{j}\leq\frac{O(\log s)\cdot\diam(T)}{2^{\ell}}\leq\frac{O(\ddim)\cdot\diam(T)}{2^{\ell}},

where the last inequality is because
\diam(T)\leq 2^{\ell+2}
and thus
s=|N_{\ell}\cap B_{X}(T,2^{\ell})|\leq 2^{O(\ddim)}
.
Finally, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[T\text{ is cut at level }\ell]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=\ell}^{L-1}\Pr[T\text{ is cut at level }\ell\text{ but not at level }\ell+1]

\displaystyle\leq O(\ddim)\cdot\diam(T)\sum_{i=\ell}^{L-1}2^{-i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{O(\ddim)\cdot\diam(T)}{2^{\ell}}.

∎

2.1.1
Portal-respecting Paths

Following
[
18
]
, we use the notion of
portal-respecting paths
.
For a pair of points
x,y\in X
, the portal-respecting path between
x
and
y
is a collection of segments, each of which connects some level
i
portal to its closest level
i+1
portal.
Specifically, let
\ell
be the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.
Consider a sequence of clusters
C_{0}(x),\dots,C_{\ell}(x)
and a sequence of portals
p_{0}(x),\dots,p_{\ell}(x)
, where
C_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{H}_{i}
is the level
i
cluster containing
x
, and
p_{i}(x)\in P_{C_{i}(x)}
is the portal closest to
p_{i-1}(x)
.
In particular,
p_{0}(x)=x
.
Define
C_{i}(y)
and
p_{i}(y)
analogously.
The portal-respecting path between
x
and
y
is then the sequence of portals
(p_{0}(x),p_{1}(x),\dots,p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y),\dots,p_{0}(y))
.
The portal-respecting distance between
x
and
y
is defined as the length of the path:

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y):=\dist(p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y)).

Lemma 2.6
.

For
x,y\in X
, the portal-respecting distance between
x
and
y
satisfies

\dist(x,y)\leq\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\rho 2^{\ell}),

where
\ell
is the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

The lower bound for
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)
is straightforward.
For the upper bound, by definition we have

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\dist(p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+2\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+2\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+4\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}O(\rho)2^{i}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\rho 2^{\ell}).

∎

2.2
Nearest Neighbor Search in Partially Doubling Metrics

In this section we give known results on approximate nearest neighbor search in partially doubling metrics.
Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, where
X
is a dataset of size
n
, and the query points come from
Y
.
In
Section

2.2.1
, we consider the setting where only
X
is doubling.
In
Section

2.2.2
, we consider the setting where only
Y
is doubling.

2.2.1
Low-dimensional Data, High-dimensional Queries

The first lemma is due to Krauthgamer and Lee
[
37
]
and gives a result for nearest neighbor search when
X
is doubling. We state it slightly differently from the original paper so that it fits better to our setting.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and

(X\cup Y,\dist)
be a metric space with
|X|=n
and
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
and let the aspect ratio of
X
be
\Delta
. Then there is an algorithm that
builds in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(n\log\Delta)
time a data structure that,
given a query point
y\in Y
, returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN of
y
in
X
.
The query time is
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(\log\Delta)
.

2.2.2
High-dimensional Data, Low-dimensional Queries

For the case where query points come from a subset
Y
with bounded doubling dimension
\ddim
, we adapt the following ANN data structure proposed by
[
30
]
, which assumes that one can compute an exact nearest neighbor that lies in sets of bounded doubling dimension in constant time.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
30
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Given a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n
and
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
and assume that for
x\in X
it nearest neighbor in
Y
can be computed in constant time. Then there exists an algorithm that
builds in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}{O}(n\log n)
time a data structure that,
given a query point
y\in Y
, returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN of
y
in
X
.
The query time is
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(\log n)
.

We slightly modify Lemma
2.8

by using Lemma
2.7
to compute an
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate nearest neighbor in a doubling space instead of an exact one. This only requires minor adjustments in the proof and introduces an additional factor
O(\log\Delta)
to the construction and query time, which is stated in the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

There exists an algorithm that,
given a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n
and
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
,
builds in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}{O}(n\log n\log\Delta)
time a data structure that,
given a query point
y\in Y
, returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN of
y
in
X
.
The query time is
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(\log n\log\Delta)
, where
\Delta
is the aspect ratio of
Y
.

3
Dimension Reduction Using Proxy Sets

In this section, we develop our dimension reduction techniques for the setting where the candidate center set
Y
has bounded doubling dimension.
We first explain
a simple form of dimension reduction that replaces a point in
X
by a constant-size set
N_{x}\subseteq Y
called
proxies
of
x
, such that the distance
\dist(x,y)
from
x
to any
y\in Y
is approximated by
\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)
. We later argue how this idea can be modified so that it can be incorporated in the algorithm of Cohen-Addad et al.
[
18
]
.
The set of proxies
N_{x}
will be defined as
an
\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,Y)
-net of a ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
, where
\pi_{Y}(x)
is the nearest neighbor of
x
in
Y
. For simplicity of exposition we assume here that

\pi_{Y}(x)\in N_{x}
. We later present a more general statement that also does not require this condition.

See
1.6

Proof.

By the triangle inequality we have
\dist(x,y)\leq\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)
for every
u\in Y
. Thus, it follows that
\dist(x,y)\leq\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)=\widehat{\dist}(x,y)
.
Now consider the case that
y\in B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
.
Define
\hat{u}\in N_{x}
to be the closest point in
N_{x}
to
y
.
In this case,
\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,Y)
and so

\widehat{\dist}(x,y)=\min_{u\in N_{x}}d(x,u)+d(u,y)\leq\dist(x,\hat{u})+\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq\dist(x,y)+\dist(\hat{u},y)+\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,y).

If
y\notin B_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(x),\dist(x,Y)/\varepsilon)
then we have

\dist(x,y)\geq\dist(\pi_{Y}(x),y)-\dist(\pi_{Y}(x),x)\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\dist(x,Y)

and so
\dist(x,Y)\leq\varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,y)
.
It follows that

\widehat{\dist}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))+\dist(\pi_{Y}(x),y)\leq 2\dist(x,\pi_{Y}(x))+\dist(x,y)=2\cdot\dist(x,Y)+\dist(x,y)\leq\frac{1+2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\cdot\dist(x,y).

The lemma follows with
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
∎

We would like to incorporate the idea of Lemma
1.6
into the algorithm of Cohen-Addad et al
[
18
]
. For this purpose, we need a more flexible version of Lemma
1.6
that allows us to replace
x\in X
by a net around the nearest points from an arbitrary set
S\subseteq Y
. While this will potentially result in larger errors, it allows us to charge the error to a constant approximation of the facility location problem. Note that
we do not require
\pi_{S}(x)
to be in
N_{x}
in the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
S\subseteq Y
. Let

N_{x}
be an
\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,S)
-net of
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
, for
x\in X
.
For all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y)=\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y).

For a set
F\subseteq Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,F)=\min_{y\in F}\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y).

For sets
C\subseteq X
and
F\subseteq Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{S}(C,F)=\sum_{x\in C}\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,F)
.

We remark that, strictly speaking,
\widehat{\dist}_{S}
depends on the choice of
S
and the
N_{x}
. However, the following arguments – unless stated otherwise – work for arbitrary choice of
N_{x}
. Therefore and for readability we ignore this dependence.
We will generalize our previous lemma to the setting of the above definition.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
S\subseteq Y
.
Then we have

\dist(x,y)\leq\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y)\leq(1+4\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,S).

Proof.

Our proof follows the ideas and structure of the proof of Lemma
1.6
.
By the triangle inequality we have
\dist(x,y)\leq\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)
for every
u\in Y
. Thus, it follows that
\dist(x,y)\leq\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist(u,y)=\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y)
.
Now consider the case that
y\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Let us define
\hat{u}\in N_{x}
to be the
closest point of
N_{x}
to
y
.
In this case,
\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq\varepsilon\cdot\dist(x,S)
and so

\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,\hat{u})+\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq\dist(x,y)+\dist(\hat{u},y)+\dist(\hat{u},y)\leq\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S).

If
y\notin B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
then
we have

\dist(x,y)\geq\dist(\pi_{S}(x),y)-\dist(\pi_{S}(x),x)\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\dist(x,S)

and so
\dist(x,S)\leq\varepsilon/(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,y)
.
Now let
\bar{\pi}_{S}(x)
be the closest point in
N_{x}
to
\pi_{S}(x)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dist(x,\bar{\pi}_{S}(x))+\dist(\bar{\pi}_{S}(x),y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+2\cdot\dist(\bar{\pi}_{S}(x),\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\dist(x,S)+\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon})\cdot\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S).

The lemma follows with
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
∎

To see how we can use Lemma
3.2
for the clustering problems we consider, let
S\subseteq Y
be a constant approximation for the facility location problem.
Then we get the following corollary.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and let
S\subseteq Y
be a
c
-approximation to the facility location problem on instance
(X\cup Y,d)
. Then
for any set
F\subseteq Y
we have

\widehat{\dist}_{S}(X,F)+\sum_{f\in F}\ocost(f)\leq(1+6c\varepsilon)\cdot\floc(X,F).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\widehat{\dist}_{S}(X,F)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\min_{f\in F}\widehat{\dist}_{S}(x,f)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\min_{f\in F}(1+4\varepsilon)\cdot\dist(x,f)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,S)+(1+4\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{x\in X}\min_{f\in F}\dist(x,f)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2c\varepsilon\floc(X,F)+(1+4\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+6c\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)+2c\varepsilon\floc(X,F).

Now the corollary follows from the fact that
\floc(X,F)=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)+\sum_{x\in F}\ocost(f)
.
∎

3.1
Integrating Proxy Sets with Portal-respecting Paths

Next, we show how to integrate our dimension reduction techniques with portal-respecting distance introduced in
Section

2.1.1
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the hierarchical decomposition of
Y
defined in
Lemma

2.5
.
Recall that
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
is the portal-respecting distance w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.
We have the following definition that combines
Definition

3.1
with
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
S\subseteq Y
.
For all
x\in X

Let
N_{x}
be an
\varepsilon\dist(x,S)
-net of

B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),d(x,S)/\varepsilon)
. For
y\in Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y):=\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,y).

For a set
F\subseteq Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)=\min_{y\in F}\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y).

For sets
C\subseteq X
and
F\subseteq Y
we define

\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(C,F)=\sum_{x\in C}\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
.

Strictly speaking,
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
also depends on a subset
S\subseteq Y
.
Since
S
is already clear in the context, we omit it and directly write
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
instead of
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H},S}
for ease of notation.

The following lemma bounds
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
.
For
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, assume that
\ell
is the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(x),y\}
is cut,
and that
j
is the highest level where

B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut, then

\displaystyle\dist(x,y)\leq\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)\leq(1+10\varepsilon)\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j}).

Proof.

Consider the following two cases.

Case 1:
y\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.

Then there exists
u\in N_{x}
, such that
\dist(u,y)\leq\varepsilon\dist(x,S)
.
Since
u,y\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
,

\{u,y\}
is cut at level at most
j
.
By
Lemma

2.6
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,y)\leq\dist(u,y)+O(\rho)\cdot 2^{j}\leq\varepsilon\dist(x,S)+O(\rho)\cdot 2^{j}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,y)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+\dist(y,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,y)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+2\varepsilon\dist(x,S)+O(\rho)\cdot 2^{j}.

Case 2:
y\notin B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.

There exists
v\in N_{x}
such that
\dist(v,\pi_{S}(x))\leq\varepsilon\dist(x,S)
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,v)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(v,y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist(x,S)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(v,y).

To upper bound
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(v,y)
, we consider the level where
\{v,y\}
is cut.

\{v,\pi_{S}(x)\}
is cut at level at most
j
, since
v,\pi_{S}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand,
\{\pi_{S}(x),y\}
is cut at level at most
\ell
.
Therefore,
\{v,y\}
is cut at level at most
\max\{j,\ell\}
.
By
Lemma

2.6
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(v,y)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(v,y)+O(\rho)2^{\max\{j,\ell\}}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(v,y)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),v)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+(1+\varepsilon)\dist(x,S)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j})

Therefore,

\displaystyle\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,y)+(2+2\varepsilon)\dist(x,S)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j}).

Finally, recall that

\displaystyle\dist(x,S)\leq\varepsilon\dist(y,\pi_{S}(x))\leq\varepsilon\dist(x,S)+\varepsilon\dist(x,y).

Thus,
\dist(x,S)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\dist(x,y)
.
We have

\displaystyle\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,y)\leq\left(1+\varepsilon\frac{2+2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)\dist(x,y)+O(\rho)(2^{\ell}+2^{j}).

Combining the two cases completes the proof.
∎

4
A New Metric Decomposition

In this section, we turn to the other setting where the client set
X
has bounded doubling dimension while the facility set
Y
does not.
At first, it may be tempting to apply the dimension reduction techniques in
Section

3
to
Y
, and define a proxy set of every facility
y\in Y
.
Unfortunately, this idea does not work in this setting, mainly due to the asymmetric nature between
X
and
Y
.
Algorithmically, the solution (facility set)
F\subseteq Y
is unknown to us in advance.
Thus it is difficult to decide the “active” proxy sets which should be used to compute the connection cost.
Moreover, even if we are able to maintain such active proxy sets, it can be tricky to integrate proxy sets for facilities with portal-respecting paths.
Therefore, some new ideas are needed.

We use a different approach in this setting.
Instead of first reducing
Y
to
X
and then applying Talwar’s metric decomposition on
X
,
we directly construct a hierarchical decomposition for the entire metric space
X\cup Y
.
Compared to Talwar’s decomposition for doubling metrics
[
44
]
, our decomposition has the new feature of handling points in the high-dimensional ambient space
Y
.
We first summarize its key properties in
Lemma

4.1
, and show how to efficiently construct it based on Talwar’s decomposition.
In
Section

4.1
,
we further discuss how to define portal-respecting paths and portal-respecting distances w.r.t. our new decomposition.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given a metric
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
and parameter
\rho\in(0,1/2)
, one can compute in
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
time a random hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{L=O(\log\Delta)}\}
for
X\cup Y
, satisfying

(1)

Bounded diameter: For
0\leq\ell\leq L
, every level
\ell
cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
has diameter
O(2^{\ell})
.

(2)

Ornaments:
\{y\}
is a leaf node at level
\min\{L-1,\lceil\log(\dist(y,X)/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil\}
, for every point
y\in Y\setminus X
.
Moreover,
\pi_{X}(y)
is contained in one of the siblings of
\{y\}
.
Cluster
\{y\}
is called an
ornament
.

(3)

Nested: For every cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
,
let
\Schild(C)
and
\Nchild(C)
be the set of
C
’s ornament child clusters and non-ornament child clusters, respectively.
Then

C=\bigcup_{D\in\Nchild(C)}D\cup\bigcup_{\{y\}\in\Schild(C)}\{y\},

i.e.,
C
is the union of its child clusters.
Furthermore,
|\Nchild(C)|\leq 2^{O(\ddim)}
.

(4)

Cutting probability: There exists a universal constant
c>0
s.t. for every subset
T\subseteq X
,

\Pr[T\text{ is cut at level }\ell]\leq\frac{c\cdot\ddim\cdot\diam(T)}{2^{\ell}}.

(5)

Portals: For
0\leq\ell\leq L
, every non-ornament cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
comes with a portal set
P_{C}\subseteq X
, which satisfies

(a)

Bounded size:
|P_{C}|\leq\rho^{-O(\ddim)}
.

(b)

Covering: When
\ell\leq L-1
, every
x\in C\cap X
has
\dist(x,P_{C})\leq\rho 2^{\ell}
; every
y\in C\setminus X
has
\dist(y,P_{C})\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}
.

(c)

Nested: If
p\in P_{C^{\prime}}\cap C
for some
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1}
, then
p\in P_{C}
.

Every cluster
C
on our new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
is a subset of
X\cup Y
.
Properties
(1)
,
(3)
and
(4)
are inherited from Talwar’s original decomposition (
Lemma

2.5
).
Property
(2)
is related to points in the high-dimensional ambient space
Y
, and becomes the key feature of our decomposition.
Specifically, every point
y\in Y\setminus X
will be attached to the tree as a leaf node at a level that depends on
\dist(y,X)
.
This is different from Talwar’s decomposition, where every point in
X
becomes a leaf node at level
0
.
Denote
h(y)
as the level where
y
is a leaf node on
\mathcal{P}
.
Then

\displaystyle\forall y\in X\cup Y,\qquad h(y)=\min\{L-1,\lceil\log(\dist(y,X)/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil\}.

(3)

Note that
h(y)=0
when
y\in X
, which is consistent with Talwar’s decomposition.

Property
(5)
defines portals for each
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, which are useful for us to further define portal-respecting paths on
\mathcal{P}
.
Roughly speaking, the portal set
P_{C}
mimics the
\rho 2^{\ell}
-net on
C
.
It is
\rho 2^{\ell}
-packing for
C
,
\rho 2^{\ell}
-covering for
C\cap X
, but only
2\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}
-covering for
C\setminus X
.
This weaker covering property turns out sufficient for bounding the error of portal-respecting distances in
Section

4.1
.
See
Figure

1
for an illustration of our decomposition.

Figure 1:
An illustration of the new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
.

C
is a level
\ell
cluster on
\mathcal{P}
, which has non-ornament child clusters
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},D_{4}
and an ornament child
\{y\}
, where
y\in Y\setminus X
.
The left figure shows how
C
is partitioned.
Black points are portals of
C
, and
\dist(y,P_{C})\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}
.
The right figure shows part of the decomposition
\mathcal{P}
, where circles are non-ornament nodes, and squares are ornaments.

Construction of
\mathcal{P}
.

Assume wlog the minimum interpoint distance of
X\cup Y
is
1
and that
\diam(X\cup Y)=\Delta
.
The construction of our new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
is formally given in
Algorithm

2
.

Input:

finite metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
,
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m
and
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
,
and
a parameter
\rho\in(0,1/2)

run
Algorithm

1
on
(X,\dist)
to obtain the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{L}\}

1

let
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\leftarrow\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
for
0\leq\ell\leq L

2

for

y\in Y\setminus X

do

3

compute

\pi_{X}(y)\in X

4

let
h(y)\leftarrow\min\{L-1,\lceil\log(\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil\}

5

\mathcal{P}_{h(y)}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{h(y)}\cup\{\{y\}\}

6

find the level
h(y)
cluster
C
that contains
\pi_{X}(y)
, let
\{y\}
be a sibling of
C
,
and add
y
to the ancestors of
C
(excluding
C
)

7

for

0\leq\ell\leq L

do

8

for

C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}

do

9

C
inherits the portal set
P_{C}
from
\mathcal{H}

10

return

\mathcal{P}:=\{\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{L}\}

Algorithm 2

New decomposition
\mathcal{P}

Algorithm

2
has three stages.
In stage 1 (Lines
2
-
2
), it runs
Algorithm

1
to compute the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
for
X
, and initializes
\mathcal{P}
as a copy of
\mathcal{H}
.
In stage 2 (Lines
2
-
2
), it adds points in the ambient space
Y\setminus X
to the decomposition.
Specifically, for every
y\in Y\setminus X
, it first finds the point
\pi_{X}(y)\in X
closest to
y
(Line
2
),
and decides the level
h(y)
where the ornament
\{y\}
should be a leaf node, based on the distance
\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))
(Line
2
).
Ornament
\{y\}
is then attached to level
h(y)
as a sibling of the cluster that contains
\pi_{X}(y)
(Lines
2
-
2
).
Finally, it adds
y
to all ancestors (Line
2
), which guarantees that the new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
is still nested.
In stage 3 (Lines
2
-
2
), our algorithm constructs the portal set for each cluster
C
by directly inheriting
P_{C}
from
\mathcal{H}
.
By construction,
\mathcal{P}
is a random decomposition, whose randomness solely comes from
\mathcal{H}
.

Remark
.

Line
2
computes the nearest neighbor of
y
in
X
, which requires
O(n)
time for each
y
.
In our implementation, the time complexity can be reduced via
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN search.
Moreover, replacing
\pi_{X}(y)
with a
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN in
Lemmas

4.1
and
2
will not affect the correctness of our subsequent analysis,
and only introduces an extra
(1+\varepsilon)
factor to our final approximation ratio.

The notion of being cut (
Definition

2.4
) can be adapted to
\mathcal{P}
as well.
Formally, say a set
T\subseteq X\cup Y
is
cut
at level
\ell
w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
, if there
exists a cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, such that
T\cap C\neq\emptyset
and
T\setminus C\neq\emptyset
.
In fact, we will mainly use this notion of cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
for
T\subseteq X
in our analysis.

We next show that the decomposition
\mathcal{P}
computed by
Algorithm

2
satisfies the properties in
Lemma

4.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

Properties
(2)
,
(3)
and
(4)
follow from the construction and
Lemma

2.5
.
We focus on properties
(1)
and
(5)
below.

Property
(1)
: bounded diameter.

The bound is trivial for ornaments.
Consider a non-ornament cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
.
By construction, there exists a corresponding cluster
\widehat{C}\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, such that
C\supseteq\widehat{C}
and
C\setminus\widehat{C}\subseteq Y\setminus X
.
For every
y\in C\setminus\widehat{C}
, by Line
2
of
Algorithm

2
we have
\ell>h(y)=\min\{L-1,\lceil\log(\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil\}
.

If
\ell>L-1
then
\ell=L
.
We have
\diam(C)=\diam(X\cup Y)\leq 2^{L}
.

If
\ell>\lceil\log(\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil
, then
\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))\leq\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}
.
By Line
2
, we have
\pi_{X}(y)\in C
as well.
Hence, for every
y,y^{\prime}\in C
,

\displaystyle\dist(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))+\dist(\pi_{X}(y),\pi_{X}(y^{\prime}))+\dist(y^{\prime},\pi_{X}(y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}+\diam(\widehat{C})+\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}

Since
\pi_{X}(y),\pi_{X}(y^{\prime})\in\widehat{C}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell+1}+O(2^{\ell})

By
Lemma

2.5

\displaystyle\leq O(2^{\ell}).

Therefore,
\diam(C)\leq O(2^{\ell})
.

Property
(5)
: portals.

By Line
2
of
Algorithm

2
,
P_{C}
is inherited from
\mathcal{H}
.
Therefore, the size bound and nested property go through.
We focus on the covering property below.

For
x\in C\cap X
, the bound follows from the standard property of nets.
For
y\in C\setminus X
, by construction we have

L-1\geq\ell>h(y)=\min\{L-1,\lceil\log(\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))/\sqrt{\rho})\rceil\}.

Thus,
\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))\leq\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}
.
Furthermore,
\pi_{X}(y)\in C\cap X
.
Therefore,
\dist(\pi_{X}(y),P_{C})\leq\rho 2^{\ell}
.
We thus have

\dist(y,P_{C})\leq\dist(y,\pi_{X}(y))+\dist(\pi_{X}(y),P_{C})\leq\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}+\rho 2^{\ell}\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}2^{\ell}.

Time complexity.

For stage 1 of
Algorithm

2
, by
Lemma

2.5
, the decomposition
\mathcal{H}
can be computed in
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n\log\Delta)
time on top of
X
.

For stage 2, as discussed above,
\pi_{X}(y)
will be replaced by a
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN of
y
in
X
(Line
2
), which can be found in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(\log\Delta)
time by
Lemma

2.7
.
The algorithm then calculates
h(y)
and attaches
\{y\}
to level
h(y)
, both of which can be done in constant time.
To add
y
to its ancestor clusters on
\mathcal{P}
,
note that
\{y\}
has at most
L=O(\log\Delta)
ancestors, so this step can be done in
O(\log\Delta)
time.
Therefore, stage 2 has complexity
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(\log\Delta)
for a single
y\in Y\setminus X
, and a total of
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(m\log\Delta)
.

For stage 3, the portal set
P_{C}
for each cluster
C
is inherited from
H
.
Since
|P_{C}|\leq\rho^{-O(\ddim)}
,
the total complexity of stage 3 is
\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}}\rho^{-O(\ddim)}=\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n\log\Delta)
.

In conclusion, the time complexity of
Algorithm

2
is
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
.

∎

4.1
Portal-respecting Paths on
\mathcal{P}

Consider metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the decomposition computed by
Algorithm

2
, with parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.

Consider a pair of points
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
It will be helpful to think of
x
as a client and
y
as the facility to which
x
is assigned in a certain solution.
Let
0\leq\ell\leq L-1
be the highest level where the set
\{x,y\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
Consider a sequence of clusters
C_{0}(x),\dots,C_{\ell}(x)
and a sequence of portals
p_{0}(x),p_{1}(x),\dots,p_{\ell}(x)
, such that
C_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the level
i
cluster containing
x
, and
p_{i}(x)\in P_{C_{i}(x)}
is the portal closest to
p_{i-1}(x)
.
In particular,
p_{0}(x)=x
.
The clusters and portals for
y
are defined in an analogous but slightly different way.
Let
j=h(y)\leq\ell
be the level where
y
is a leaf node.
(If
y\in X
, then
j=0
.)
Let
D_{j+1}(y),\dots,D_{\ell}(y)
be a sequence of clusters and
p_{j+1}(y),\dots,p_{\ell}(y)
be a sequence of portals, such that

D_{i}(y)\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the level
i
cluster containing
y
, and
p_{i}(y)\in P_{D_{i}(y)}
is the portal closest to
p_{i-1}(y)
.
Define
D_{j}(y)=\{y\}
and
p_{j}(y)=y
.
The portal-respecting path between
x
and
y
is thus defined as

\Big(x=p_{0}(x),p_{1}(x),\dots,p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y),\dots,p_{j}(y)=y\Big),

and the portal-respecting distance between
x
and
y
is defined as the length of the path:

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y):=\dist(p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+\sum_{i=j}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y)).

We note that above definition of portal-respecting path is analogous to that in
Section

2.1.1
and
[
18
]
, with the only difference that the path does not necessarily start from the
0
-th level.
This misalignment mainly comes from the construction of our modified decomposition
\mathcal{P}
, where a point
y
in the ambient space can be a leaf node at a high level
j>0
.
Also, all points except one endpoint of the path are net points (portals) in
X
.
We have the following bound on
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
, which depends on both the highest level where
\{x,y\}

is cut and the level where
y
is a leaf node.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
the portal-respecting distance between
x
and
y
satisfies

\dist(x,y)\leq\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)\leq\begin{cases}\dist(x,y)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell},&\text{if }h(y)<\ell;\\
\dist(x,y)+O(\rho)2^{\ell},&\text{if }h(y)=\ell,\end{cases}

where
0\leq\ell\leq L-1
is the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
,
and
h(y)
is defined in (
3
).

We note that
Lemma

4.2
is similar but weaker than
Lemma

2.6
.
If
h(y)=\ell
, then the bound is the same as
Lemma

2.6
.
If
h(y)<\ell
, we lose a
\sqrt{\rho}
factor in the additive error term.
This is mainly due to the weaker covering property in
(5)
of
Lemma

4.1
.
More concretely, it is only guaranteed that
y
can be connected to a nearby portal within distance
O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{h(y)+1}
rather than
O(\rho)2^{h(y)+1}
.
We give the proof below.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

The lower bound for
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)
is straightforward.
For the upper bound, by definition we have

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\dist(p_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(y))+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+\sum_{i=h(y)}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+2\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(x),p_{i+1}(x))+2\sum_{i=h(y)}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y)).

It is easy to show that the first summation is at most
O(\rho)2^{\ell}
.
One should be more careful about the second summation.
If
h(y)=\ell
, then the second summation does not exist, and we have
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\rho)2^{\ell}
.

If
h(y)<\ell
, then
h(y)<L-1
.
By property
(5)
of
Lemma

4.1
, we have

\dist(y,p_{h(y)+1}(y))\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}2^{h(y)+1}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=h(y)}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y))

\displaystyle=\dist(y,p_{h(y)+1}(y))+\sum_{i=h(y)+1}^{\ell-1}\dist(p_{i}(y),p_{i+1}(y))

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\rho}2^{h(y)+1}+\sum_{i=h(y)+1}^{\ell-1}O(\rho)2^{i}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{h(y)}+O(\rho)2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}.

∎

The following lemma is a corollary of
Lemma

4.2
.
It shows that the error incurred by portal-respecting distance (i.e.,
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)
) is essentially determined by the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(y)\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
,
where
\pi_{X}(y)
is the closest point to
y
in
X
.
This allows us to utilize the cutting probability (Property
(4)
of
Lemma

4.1
) on
\mathcal{P}
to bound the error.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
x\in X
,
y\in Y
and
0\leq\ell\leq L-1
be the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(y)\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
Then

\dist(x,y)\leq\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,y)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}.

Proof.

If
y\in X
, then the proof is the same as
Lemma

2.6
.
We thus assume
y\in Y\setminus X
.
Recall that
h(y)
is the level where
y
is a leaf node.
By Property
(2)
of
Lemma

4.1
,
\{y,\pi_{X}(y)\}
is cut at level at most
h(y)
.
Consider the following two cases.

If
\ell>h(y)
, then at level
\ell+1
of
\mathcal{P}
, the three points
x,\pi_{X}(y),y
are in the same cluster.
Hence,
\ell
is the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut.
By
Lemma

4.2
,

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}.

(4)

If
\ell\leq h(y)
, then at level
h(y)+1
of
\mathcal{P}
, the three points
x,\pi_{X}(y),y
are in the same cluster.
Hence,
h(y)
is the highest level where
\{x,y\}
is cut.
By
Lemma

4.2
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\rho)2^{h(y)}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,y)+O(\rho)\frac{\dist(y,X)}{\sqrt{\rho}}

By
Lemma

4.1

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,y).

(5)

Combining (
4
) with (
5
) completes the proof.
∎

5
Facility Location with Low-dimensional Centers

The first problem we consider is facility location when the set
Y
has doubling dimension at most
\ddim
.
Our main result in this section is the following.

Theorem 5.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m,\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
, computes a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of facility location in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

5.1
Structural Lemmas

Our algorithm is based on our dimension reduction techniques in
Section

3
.
Specifically, every client
x\in X
will be represented by a proxy set
N_{x}
, which is an
\varepsilon\dist(x,S)
-net on
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
,
where
S\subseteq Y
