will be chosen as a constant approximate solution for facility location.
Moreover, we integrate our dimension reduction with portal-respecting distances.
For a facility set
F\subseteq Y
, we use
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
defined in
Definition

3.4
to replace the metric distance
\dist(x,F)
, where the scaling parameter
\rho
of portals is set to
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.

Recall in
Lemma

3.5
, the error incurred by replacing
\dist(x,F)
with
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
depends on the highest level where the two sets
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
and
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
are cut.
For the first set, we show in
Lemma

5.5
that we can upper bound the cutting level by preprocessing the dataset.
For the second set, we show in
Lemma

5.6
the existence of a good solution
F\subseteq Y
, such that
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut at a bounded level.
Both of these structural results rely on the notion of badly-cut sets and bad points, which are first introduced in
[
18
]
.
We further adapt these notions to our setting.

Definition 5.2

(Badly cut)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a random hierarchical decomposition as in
Lemma

2.5
.
Say a set
T\subseteq Y
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
if
T
is cut at level
\log\diam(T)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
on
\mathcal{H}
.

The next lemma follows immediately from Equation (
2
) of Lemma
2.5
, which bounds the probability of a set being badly cut.

Lemma 5.3
.

For every
T\subseteq Y
,

\displaystyle\Pr[T\text{ is badly cut}]\leq c\cdot\varepsilon,

where
c
is the same constant as in (
2
).

Definition 5.4

(Bad points)
.

Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
and let
\mathcal{H}
be the decomposition for
Y
in
Lemma

2.5
.
Let
F^{*},S\subseteq Y
.

(1)

A point
x\in X
is called a
bad client
(w.r.t.
S
and
\mathcal{H}
) if
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.
Denote the set of bad clients as
\Bad_{X}\subseteq X
.

(2)

A point
y\in S
is called a
bad facility
(w.r.t.
F^{*}
and
\mathcal{H}
) if
B_{Y}(y,10\dist(y,F^{*}))
is badly cut.
Denote the set of bad facilities as
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
.

In our subsequent analysis, we instantiate
F^{*}\subseteq Y
as an optimal solution for facility location and
S\subseteq Y
as a constant approximate solution,
and define bad clients
\Bad_{X}\subseteq X
and bad facilities
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
.

Definition

5.4
is similar to
[
18
, Definition 10]
expect for one major difference.
In
[
18
]
, bad clients are defined w.r.t. a ball around
x
(i.e.,
B(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
).
However, since in our setting
x
does not necessarily lie in the doubling subset, we change the center of the ball to
\pi_{S}(x)
which is in
Y
.
This new definition also integrates well with the proxy set of
x
defined in
Section

3
.

As mentioned earlier, to bound the error
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)-\dist(x,F)
we need to bound the cutting level of
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
for every
x\in X
.
By
Definition

5.4
, this level is at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
if
x
is not a bad client.
It then remains to handle the bad clients, and the idea is to “eliminate” them by moving every
x\in\Bad_{X}
to
\pi_{S}(x)
.
This movement creates a new dataset
X^{\prime}
.
The next lemma shows that such movement changes any clustering cost by at most
\varepsilon\floc(X,S)
.
This is essentially the same as
[
18
, Lemma 12]
.
We provide the proof below for completeness.

Lemma 5.5

(New instance)
.

Given
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
, and a solution
S\subseteq Y
for facility location,
construct a new
(multi-)set of clients
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\cup Y
as
X^{\prime}:=\phi(X)
for

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}x,&\text{if }x\notin\Bad_{X};\\
\pi_{S}(x),&\text{if }x\in\Bad_{X},\end{cases}

namely,
X^{\prime}
is constructed from
X
by moving every bad client
x
to
\pi_{S}(x)
.
Then with probability
0.99
,

\displaystyle\forall F\subseteq Y,\qquad\Big|\floc(X,F)-\floc(X^{\prime},F)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

Proof.

By construction,
for every
x\in X
, either
x
is not a bad client or
x=\pi_{S}(x)
.
To bound the change in clustering cost,
note that

\displaystyle\E\left[\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,\phi(x))\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\E[\dist(x,\phi(x))]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))\cdot\Pr[x\text{ is a bad client}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

5.3

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

By Markov’s inequality, with probability
0.99
,

\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,\phi(x))\leq O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

Conditioning on this, consider an arbitrary
facility set
F\subseteq Y
.
We have

\displaystyle\Big|\floc(X,F)-\floc(X^{\prime},F)\Big|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{x\in X}(\dist(x,F)-\dist(\phi(x),F))\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X}|\dist(x,F)-\dist(\phi(x),F)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,\phi(x))

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

∎

We remark that, if
S
is a constant approximation, then we have

\floc(X^{\prime},F)\in(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\floc(X,F)

for arbitrary solution
F\subseteq Y
with probability
0.99
.

In the following lemma, we show the existence of a facility set
F\subseteq Y
, such that the portal-respecting cost of
X
w.r.t.
F
is bounded by
(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)
.
This allows us to compute the solution with minimum portal-respecting cost in our final algorithm.
The lemma can be viewed as an analogy to
[
18
, Lemmas 13 and 14]
,
with an essential difference that we use the portal-respecting distance defined in
Definition

3.4
, which utilizes our dimension reduction and proxy sets for clients.
The validity of such replacement is mainly based on
Lemma

3.5
.
It also makes the proof more challenging.

Lemma 5.6

(Good portal-respecting solution)
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
be a facility location instance,
S\subseteq Y
be a solution and

\mathcal{H}
be the hierarchical decomposition on
Y
in
Lemma

2.5
with portal parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
be the new client set constructed by
Lemma

5.5
.
For
x\in X^{\prime}
and
F\subseteq Y
, define the portal-respecting connection cost of
x
as

\displaystyle\ccostport(x,F):=\begin{cases}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F),&\text{if }x=\pi_{S}(x);\\
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F),&\text{if }x\neq\pi_{S}(x).\end{cases}

Then with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

(6)

Let us briefly explain the definition of
\ccostport(x,F)
.
The case
x\neq\pi_{S}(x)
implies that
x
is
originally
not a bad client in
X
.
Then the set
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at a bounded level of
\mathcal{H}
.
We can therefore use
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
for approximation, and by
Lemma

3.5
, it suffices to only bound the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut.
However, if
x
is originally a bad client (which corresponds to
x=\pi_{S}(x)
in
Lemma

5.6
), then we can no longer use
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
for approximation, because we now have no control on the highest level where
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut.
Fortunately, in this case
x
and
\pi_{S}(x)
are the same in
X^{\prime}
, so we can directly use the original portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)
for approximation.
By
Lemma

2.6
, we still only need to bound the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut.

Proof
Lemma

5.6
.

Recall that a facility
f\in S
is called a bad facility if
B_{Y}(f,10\dist(f,F^{*}))
is badly cut.
Let
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
be the set of bad facilities in
S
.
Define

F:=F^{*}\cup\Bad_{S}.

We show that the facility set
F
satisfies (
6
).
Recall that
X^{\prime}=\phi(X)
by
Lemma

5.5
, thus we can rewrite the LHS of (
6
) as

\sum_{x\in X}\ccostport(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F).

We first bound for each
x\in X
the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut.
Specifically,
let
\ell(x)
be the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut;
we show that

\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon).

Consider the following cases.

Case 1:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(x)
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, it is in
\Bad_{S}
, and thus in
F
.
Therefore,
\pi_{S}(\phi(x))=\phi(x)=\pi_{F}(\phi(x))
, and
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is never cut.

Case 2:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(x)
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility, the ball

B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))=B_{Y}(\phi(x),10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))

is cut at level at most
\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Note that
\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\in B_{Y}(\phi(x),10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))
, and
\pi_{S}(\phi(x))=\phi(x)
.
Hence,
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut at level at most
\ell(x)\leq\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

Case 3:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand, since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, we have
\pi_{S}(x)\in F
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))\leq 2\dist(x,S),

which implies
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Therefore,
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut at level at most
\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)=\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

Case 4:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
,
and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))
is cut at level at most
\log(10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Consider the following sub-cases.

If
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/(2\varepsilon)
, then

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon.

Hence,
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
. Thus
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut at level at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)=\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

If
\dist(x,F^{*})>\dist(x,S)/(2\varepsilon)
, first note that

\displaystyle\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))=\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+\dist(x,S)\leq\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+2\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*}),

which implies
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\tfrac{1}{1-2\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})\leq 2\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})
.
Then

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})

Since
\dist(x,S)<2\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+2\varepsilon)\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}).

This implies
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))
.
Therefore,
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut at level at most
\log(10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)\leq\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+10\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

In conclusion,
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut at level at most
\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

To further prove (
6
), we first show that
\floc(X,F)\leq\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)
with probability
0.99
.
First note that

\displaystyle\floc(X,F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F^{*})+\ocost(F^{*})+\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f)

\displaystyle=\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f).

Then

\displaystyle 0\leq\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)\leq\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f).

Taking expectation, we have

\displaystyle\E[\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)]

\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in S}\ocost(f)\cdot\Pr[f\text{ is a bad facility}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\sum_{f\in S}\ocost(f)

By
Lemma

5.3

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

Applying Markov’s inequality to the (non-negative) random variable
\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
, with probability
0.99
,
\floc(X,F)\leq\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)
.

Next we bound
\ccostport(\phi(x),F)
for every
x\in X
.
If
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(\phi(x))
, since
\ell(x)
is the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut,
by
Lemma

2.6
we have

\ccostport(\phi(x),F)=\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),F)\leq\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}.

If
\phi(x)\neq\pi_{S}(\phi(x))
, then
x
is not a bad client.
By
Lemma

3.5
, we have

\displaystyle\ccostport(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),F)\leq\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon}.

For
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
we can bound the LHS of (
6
) as

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{x\in X}\ccostport(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(\phi(x))\end{subarray}}(\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
\phi(x)\neq\pi_{S}(\phi(x))\end{subarray}}\left((1+O(\varepsilon))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\floc(\phi(X),F)+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\sum_{x\in X}\left(\frac{2\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon}+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\floc(X^{\prime},F)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X^{\prime},S)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X^{\prime},F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\floc(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\floc(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\text{w.p. }0.99.

We conclude that with probability
0.9
,

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F)\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

Rescaling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

5.2
The Algorithm

Our algorithm is based on the dynamic programming framework proposed by
[
18
]
.
Given an instance
(X,Y)
with
\ddim(Y)
bounded, the algorithm constructs the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
on top of
Y
.
It then modifies the instance according to
Lemma

5.5
.
The dynamic program is run on top of the modified instance, with respect to the portal-respecting connection cost
\ccostport(x,F)
defined in
Lemma

5.6
.
The correctness of the algorithm is proven in
Section

5.2.1
, which is based on
Lemmas

5.5
and
5.6
.
We analyze the time complexity in
Section

5.2.2
.
We describe the algorithm as follows.

Preprocessing stage.

Given as input
(X,Y)
, the algorithm first applies the techniques in
Appendix

A
to computes a
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation solution
S\subseteq Y
for facility location.
At first, this approximation might be too large, as it requires us to rescale the precision parameter
\varepsilon
by a factor of
1/2^{O(\ddim)}
, which may blow up the time complexity of our algorithm.
We ignore this potential issue when describing our algorithm at this point, and discuss how we can fix it in
Section

5.2.1
.

Our algorithm then constructs the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
together with portals on top of
Y
.
The scaling parameter of portals is set to be
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
For every
x\in X
, the algorithm checks if
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut (i.e., if
x
is a bad client).
It moves every bad client
x\in X
to
\pi_{S}(x)
, creating a new instance
(X^{\prime},Y)
.
2
2

2

In fact, this step can be efficiently done by moving every
x
to its
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN in
S
.
This replacement does not affect the correctness of our previous analysis, and only enlarge the approximation ratio by a
(1+\varepsilon)
factor.

For every
f\in S
, the algorithm decides how many copies of
f
exist in
X^{\prime}
; the value is denoted by
w(f)\in\mathbb{N}
.
For
x\in X^{\prime}
, it computes

\displaystyle j(x):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }x=\pi_{S}(x);\\
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)+1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

By
Lemma

5.5
,
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is entirely contained in some cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{j(x)}
.
Such
C
, denoted by
C(x)\in\mathcal{H}_{j(x)}
, is called the cluster where
x
is
revealed
.
(In particular, if
x=\pi_{S}(x)
, then
C(x)=\{x\}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
.)
Roughly speaking, if
j(x)>0
, then the connection cost of client
x
will not be computed at level
0
.
Instead, we will “defer” the computation of
\ccostport(x,F)
to a higher level cluster
C(x)\in\mathcal{H}_{j(x)}
.
Note that every
x
is revealed in exactly one cluster on
\mathcal{H}
.
Compute
C(x)
for every
x\in X^{\prime}
.

The algorithm also computes the proxies
N_{x}
for every
x\in X^{\prime}
.
This can be done by computing
N_{x}=B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)\cap P_{C(x)}
.
For
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
,
N_{x}
is indeed an
\rho 2^{j(x)}\leq\varepsilon\dist(x,S)
-net of
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.

Dynamic Program.

Each table entry of the dynamic program is represented by a cluster
C
on the hierarchical decomposition, together with a configuration

\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},a_{C}^{2},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle,\quad\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},b_{C}^{2},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle.

Roughly speaking, the configuration encodes the positional information of the current facility set
F
.
Specifically, for every portal
p
of cluster
C
,

a_{C}^{p}
encodes the portal-respecting distance from
p
to the closest facility inside
C
;

b_{C}^{p}
encodes the portal-respecting distance from
p
to the closest facility outside
C
.
The value stored in entry
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})

is the minimum
revealed
cost within cluster
C
.
Formally,

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\colon\text{$F$ is consistent}\\
\text{with the configuration}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)\subseteq C}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F\cap C)\right\}.

(7)

Base case.

The base case of the dynamic program corresponds to the leaf nodes of
\mathcal{H}
.
Consider a leaf node
C=\{y\}
and the corresponding configuration
a,b
.
(We can wlog assume
\{x\}
has itself as its only portal.)
It is easy to check consistency for the configuration, since there is either a facility on
y
(then
a=0
) or no facility on
y
(then
a=\infty
).
Furthermore, the opening cost inside
\{y\}
is either
\ocost(y)
or
0
, which can also be easily computed.

The connection cost of
\{y\}
is non-zero iff
y\in X^{\prime}
.
This can be checked easily, since in the preprocessing stage, we have already marked the number of copies of
y
in
X^{\prime}
, denoted by
w(y)
.
For each of these copies, its connection cost can be read from the configuration, specifically,
\ccostport(y,F)=\min\{a,b\}
.
Therefore, the revealed cost in
\{y\}
is

g\left(\{y\},a,b\right)=w(y)\cdot\ccostport(y,F)+\ocost(F\cap\{y\}).

Updating the DP table.

Now consider a higher level cluster
C
and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle
.
To compute the value of the entry
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
, the algorithm first computes the minimum cost inherited from all its child clusters.
Specifically, it enumerates all combinations of configurations for the child clusters.
For each of these combinations

\Big\{\Big(D,\mathbf{a}_{D}=\langle a^{1}_{D},\dots,a^{|P_{D}|}_{D}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{D}=\langle b^{1}_{D},\dots,b^{|P_{D}|}_{D}\rangle\Big)\Big\}_{D\in\child(C)},

(8)

our algorithm checks if it is consistent with
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
.
Following
[
18
]
, we use the following criteria:

(a)

Every portal of
C
connects to a facility inside through a portal of its child cluster.
Formally, for every
p\in P_{C}
, there exists
D\in\child(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, such that
a_{C}^{p}=a^{q}_{D}+\dist(p,q)
.

(b)

Every portal of
D
connects to a facility outside through a portal of either of its parent cluster or its sibling cluster.
Formally, for every
D\in\child(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, either there exists a portal
p\in P_{C}
such that
b^{q}_{D}=b_{C}^{p}+\dist(p,q)
, or there exists a cluster
D^{\prime}\in\child(C)
and a portal
q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
such that
b^{q}_{D}=a^{q^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}+\dist(q,q^{\prime})
.

For every combination of configurations that is consistent, compute the summation of clustering cost over
D\in\child(C)
.
The inherited cost of
C
is the minimum summation, i.e.,

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\child(C)}\\
\text{ is consistent}\end{subarray}}\sum_{D\in\child(C)}g(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D}).

(9)

Besides the inherited cost, the algorithm also computes for
C
the connection cost of
x
for all
x
that is revealed in
C
, which is called the
newly revealed cost
in
C
, i.e.,

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)=C}\ccostport(x,F).

Consider an arbitrary
x\in X^{\prime}
with
C(x)=C
.
Wlog, assume
x\neq\pi_{S}(x)
, since otherwise
x
is already decided in level
0
(i.e., the base case).
Recall the definition of
\ccostport(x,F)
in
Lemmas

5.6
and
3.4
:

\displaystyle\ccostport(x,F):=\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F)=\min_{f\in F}\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,f)=\min_{u\in N_{x}}\dist(x,u)+\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F).

To compute
\ccostport(x,F)
,
it suffices to compute
\dist(x,u)
and
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
for every
u\in N_{x}
.
The former can be easily computed.
For the latter,
by construction
N_{x}=B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)\cap P_{C}
is a subset of
P_{C}
.
Hence
u
is a portal of
C
; therefore
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
can be directly obtained from the configuration of
C
, which is
\min\{a_{u},b_{u}\}
.

We conclude that each entry in the DP table can be updated by

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\child(C)}\\
\text{ is consistent}\end{subarray}}\sum_{D\in\child(C)}g(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})+\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)=C}\ccostport(x,F).

Reducing the number of configurations.

For every
\ell\in[0,\log\Delta]
and a level
\ell
cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, the number of configurations of
C
can be infinite.
To resolve this issue, we use two tricks following
[
18
]
.
First, we restrict
\{a_{C}^{p}\},\{b_{C}^{p}\}
to be multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
.
This discretization incurs a
\pm\rho 2^{\ell}
additive error in the portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(p,F)
for every portal
p
, but the error can be charged to the detour between
p
and
F
.

The second trick is to restrict each
a_{C}^{p},b_{C}^{p}
within a reasonable range
a_{C}^{p},b_{C}^{p}\in[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
.
The restriction for
a_{C}^{p}
is straightforward, since the distance from a portal
p\in P_{C}
to a facility inside
C
is at most
O(2^{\ell})\leq 2^{\ell}/\varepsilon
.
Regarding the restriction for
b_{C}^{p}
, note that if there exists
b_{C}^{p}>2^{\ell}/\varepsilon
, then it falls in either of the following two cases:

(a)

C\cap F\neq\emptyset
.
In this case, the closest facility of each portal
p\in P_{C}
is inside
C
, which means the actual value of
b_{C}^{p}
will never be used for computing connection cost.
Therefore, it is safe to reduce the configuration to
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle\infty,\dots,\infty\rangle
.

(b)

C\cap F=\emptyset
.
In this case, we claim that there is no facility in
B_{Y}(C,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon)
.
Moreover, up to losing a
(1+\varepsilon)
multiplicative factor, we can wlog assume every point
\{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)\subseteq C\}
is assigned to the same facility in
F
.
Therefore, we can treat the whole cluster
C
as a single point with weight
|\{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)\subseteq C\}|
, and decide its connection cost at a higher level.
An extra boolean flag can be used in the configuration to indicate that
C
is such a “compressed” cluster.

In conclusion, up to small error, we can restrict
a_{C}^{p},b_{C}^{p}
to be multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
.
The number of configurations of
C
thus can be bounded by

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}.

5.2.1
Proof of Correctness

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by our algorithm in
Section

5.2
.
Our plan is to show that
\floc(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
.
We first show the following weaker bound for
\floc(X,\widehat{F})
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by the algorithm in
Section

5.2
w.r.t. a solution
S\subseteq Y
.
Then with constant probability,

\displaystyle\floc(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+2\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

Proof.

By the algorithm in
Section

5.2
,

\widehat{F}
minimizes the facility location cost w.r.t.
\ccostport(\cdot,\cdot)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{F}=\argmin_{F\subseteq Y}\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F).

We note that the definition of
X^{\prime}
and
\ccostport
also depend on
S
.

By
Lemma

5.6
,
with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

(10)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\floc(X,\widehat{F})

\displaystyle\leq\floc(X^{\prime},\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

By
Lemma

5.5
, w.p.
0.99

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,\widehat{F})+\ocost(F)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\ocost(F)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

By definition of
\widehat{F}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+2\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\text{By \eqref{eqn:apply_fl_bounded_centers_good_solution}}.

∎

Unfortunately,
Lemma

5.7
does not imply that
\floc(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
.
Recall that in
Section

5.2
, we are only able to obtain a
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximate solution
S
.
Therefore, if we want to obtain the desired bound, we have to rescale
\varepsilon
by a factor of
1/2^{O(\ddim)}
.
Since the time complexity of our algorithm also depends on
\varepsilon
, it could be blow up due to such naive rescaling.

To resolve this issue, we apply a similar bootstrap as in
[
18
, Section 3.3]
.
By
Lemma

5.7
, if
S
is an
\alpha
-approximation of
\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
, then
\widehat{F}
is a
(1+\varepsilon+2\varepsilon\alpha)
-approximation, which improves over
S
by a
2\varepsilon
factor.
Therefore, we can start from an arbitrary
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation
\widehat{F}_{0}
; at each stage
i
run the algorithm in
Section

5.2
with
S=\widehat{F}_{i}

to obtain a better approximate solution
\widehat{F}_{i+1}
.
The procedure halts when
i
reaches
c\cdot\ddim
for some sufficiently large constant
c
.
We return
\widehat{F}_{c\cdot\ddim}
as our final solution.
Since the success probability in
Lemma

5.7
can be boosted to
1-1/\ddim^{2}
by standard amplification,
we can guarantee that with constant probability, all steps of our bootstrap succeed.
Moreover, the bootstrap only introduces a
\poly(\ddim)
overhead in the running time.

Finally, we prove the returned solution
\widehat{F}_{c\cdot\ddim}
satisfies that
\floc(X,\widehat{F}_{c\cdot\ddim})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
(correctness).

For
0\leq i\leq c\cdot\ddim
, assume
\widehat{F}_{i}
is an
\alpha_{i}
-approximate solution, i.e.,
\floc(X,\widehat{F}_{i})\leq\alpha_{i}\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
.
By
Lemma

5.7
, we have

\alpha_{i+1}\leq 1+\varepsilon+2\varepsilon\alpha_{i},\qquad 0\leq i<c\cdot\ddim.

Therefore, for every
s\leq c\cdot\ddim
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{s}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\varepsilon+2\varepsilon\alpha_{s-1}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\varepsilon+2\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)+(2\varepsilon)^{2}\alpha_{s-2}

\displaystyle\leq\dots

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(2\varepsilon)^{j}+(2\varepsilon)^{s}\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}+(2\varepsilon)^{s}2^{O(\ddim)}.

Choosing
s=c\cdot\ddim
, we have
\alpha_{s}\leq 1+O(\varepsilon)
.
Rescaling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

5.2.2
Time Complexity

We prove the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\tilde{O}(n+m)
for
t=O(\ddim\log(\ddim/\varepsilon))
.
By
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
, we can wlog assume that the aspect ratio of
X\cup Y
is
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
(time complexity).

We analyze time complexity for the preprocessing stage and the dynamic program separately.

Preprocessing stage.

In the preprocessing stage, the
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation solution
S\subseteq Y
can be computed in time
2^{O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
by
Appendix

A
.
By
Lemma

2.5
, the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
together with portals for each cluster can be computed in time
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}m\log\Delta=(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}m\log\Delta
.
By
[
18
]
, it can be checked in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(n\log\Delta)
time if
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut for all
x
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

2.7
, the new instance
(X^{\prime},Y)
can be constructed in
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(n)\log\Delta
time by moving all bad points
x\in X
to its
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN in
S
.
The number of copies of each
f\in S
can be computed in a total of
O(n)
time.

For
x\in X^{\prime}
, to determine the cluster
C(x)
where
x
is revealed, it suffices to find the cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{j(x)}
that contains
\pi_{S}(x)
.
This can be done in
O(\log\Delta)
time, thus a total of
O(n\log\Delta)
time.
A list is maintained for every
C
, which contains all points
x
that are revealed in
C
, i.e.,
\{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)=C\}
.

The proxies
N_{x}
of
x
is computed by
N_{x}:=B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)\cap P_{C(x)}
.
This can be done in
O(|P_{C(x)}|)=\rho^{-O(\ddim)}=(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}
time by checking for each
u\in P_{C(x)}
if
\dist(u,\pi_{S}(x))\leq\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon
.
Hence, the time complexity of computing
N_{x}
for all
x\in X^{\prime}
is
(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}O(n)
.

In conclusion, the time complexity of the preprocessing stage is
(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
.

Dynamic program.

Fix a cluster
C
and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle
.
The computation of
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
consists of two parts — the inherited cost and the newly revealed connection cost.

To compute the inherited cost, the algorithm enumerates all combinations of configurations of the child clusters of
C
, in the form of (
8
).
Recall that
a_{C}^{p},b_{C}^{p}\in\{0,\rho 2^{\ell},2\rho 2^{\ell},\dots,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon\}
;
therefore there are
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\sum_{D\in\child(C)}2|P_{D}|}=(\varepsilon\rho)^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}
such combinations.
For each one of these combinations, checking its consistency takes time

\displaystyle|P_{C}|\cdot\sum_{D\in\child(C)}|P_{D}|+\sum_{D\in\child(C)}\sum_{p\in P_{D}}\left(|P_{C}|+\sum_{D^{\prime}\in\child(C)}|P_{D^{\prime}}|\right)=\rho^{-O(\ddim)}.

Computing the summation of
g(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})
takes time
|\child(C)|=2^{O(\ddim)}
.
Therefore, the time complexity of computing the inherited cost of
C
(i.e., (
9
)) is

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\rho)^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\cdot(\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+2^{O(\ddim)})=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}.

To compute the connection cost for all
x
with
C(x)=C
, it suffices to compute
\ccostport(x,F)
for every such
x
.
By the previous analysis, this can be done by computing
\dist(x,u)
and
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(u,F)
for every
u\in N_{x}
, which can be done in
O(|N_{x}|)=\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}
.
Hence, the time complexity of computing the newly revealed connection cost is

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|.

We conclude that the time complexity of computing

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
is

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}+\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|.

The total time complexity of filling the DP table is

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\ell}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}\left[\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}+\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}\sum_{\ell}|\mathcal{H}_{\ell}|+\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}\sum_{\ell}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}O(m+n)\log\Delta.

Combining the analysis above, we conclude that the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\cdot O(n+m)\log\Delta
, for

\displaystyle t=O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

As mentioned in
Section

5.2.1
, the bootstrap only introduces a
\poly(\ddim)
overhead in the running time, which can be charged to the
2^{2^{t}}
factor.

Theorem

5.1
follows with
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.
∎

6
Facility Location with Low-dimensional Clients

In this section, we focus on the setting where clients
X
have bounded doubling dimension
\ddim
, while
Y
is high-dimensional.
Our main result is the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m
,

\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
,
computes a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of facility location in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

6.1
Structural Lemmas

Our algorithm is based on the new hierarchical decomposition in
Section

4
.
Specifically, we construct the new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
for the entire metric
X\cup Y
, with portal scaling parameter
\rho:=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
When calculating the connection cost, we will replace metric distances with portal-respecting distances
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)
introduced in
Section

4.1
.

Lemmas

4.2
and
4.3
are used to bound the error
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)
.
In
Lemma

6.6
we further prove the existence of
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate portal-respecting solution.

In order to control the error
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)-\dist(x,y)
,
we need to bound the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(y)\}
is cut on
\mathcal{P}
.
We need the notion of badly cut and bad points w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
The following definition extends the badly cut notion in
[
18
]
to our new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
.

Definition 6.2

(Badly cut)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be some random hierarchical decomposition as defined in Lemma
4.1
.
Say a set
T\subseteq X
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
, if
T
is cut at level
\log\diam(T)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
in
\mathcal{P}
.

The following lemma is a direct corollary of property
(4)
in
Lemma

4.1
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For every
T\subseteq X
,

\displaystyle\Pr[T\text{ is badly cut w.r.t. }\mathcal{P}]\leq c\cdot\varepsilon,

where
c
is the same constant as in
(4)
.

Next,
we extend the definition of bad points (
Definition

5.4
) to our current setting.

Definition 6.4

(Bad points)
.

Consider a metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist)
and let
\mathcal{P}
be the decomposition of
X\cup Y
in
Lemma

4.1
.
Let
F^{*},S\subseteq Y
.

(1)

A point
x\in X
is called a
bad client
(w.r.t.
S
and
\mathcal{P}
), if
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
Denote the set of bad clients as
\Bad_{X}
.

(2)

A point
y\in S
is called a
bad facility
(w.r.t.
S,F^{*}
and
\mathcal{P}
) if
B_{X}(\pi_{X}(y),100\dist(\pi_{X}(y),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(y),S))
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
Denote the set of bad facilities as
\Bad_{S}
.

In our subsequent analysis, we instantiate
F^{*}\subseteq Y
as an optimal solution for facility location and
S\subseteq Y
as a constant approximate solution,
and define bad clients
\Bad_{X}\subseteq X
and bad facilities
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
.

Let us compare
Definition

6.4
with
Definition

5.4
, which defines bad points in a similar but different way.
In
Definition

6.4
, both bad clients and bad facilities are defined w.r.t. balls in
X
instead of
Y
.
This is because our current notion of badly cut (
Definition

6.2
) is only defined for subsets of the low-dimensional space
X
.
Moreover, the centers of the balls are
x
and
\pi_{X}(y)
respectively, which is symmetric to
Definition

5.4
where the centers are
\pi_{S}(x)
and
y
.
Another difference is that for bad facilities, the radius of the ball depends on both
\dist(\pi_{X}(y),F^{*})
and
\dist(\pi_{X}(y),S)
.
A similar extra
100\dist(\pi_{X}(y),S)
term does not appear in
Definition

5.4
, since
\dist(y,S)=0
for
y\in S
.

The following lemma shows how we can eliminate bad clients in
X
.
Similar to
Lemma

5.5
, we will move every bad client
x
to a nearby point and create a new dataset
X^{\prime}
.
However, since
\pi_{S}(x)
is now in the high-dimensional ambient space
Y
, moving
x
to
\pi_{S}(x)
as
Lemma

5.5
would increase the dimension of our dataset,
which could be problematic for our algorithm design.
Therefore, we slightly alter our strategy by moving
x
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
, i.e., the nearest neighbor of
\pi_{S}(x)
in
X
.
After this modification, our new dataset
X^{\prime}
still lies in
X
and thus has bounded doubling dimension.
We can further prove that the cost of such movement is at most an
\varepsilon
fraction of
\floc(X,S)
.

Lemma 6.5

(New instance)
.

Given
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, and a solution
S\subseteq Y
for facility location, construct a new
(multi-)set of clients
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
as
X^{\prime}:=\phi(X)
for

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}x,&\text{if }x\notin\Bad_{X};\\
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),&\text{if }x\in\Bad_{X},\end{cases}

namely,
X^{\prime}
is constructed from
X
by moving every bad client
x
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Then with probability
0.99
,

\displaystyle\forall F\subseteq Y,\qquad\Big|\floc(X,F)-\floc(X^{\prime},F)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

We remark that, if
S
is a constant approximation, then we have
\floc(X^{\prime},F)\in(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\floc(X,F)
for arbitrary solution
F\subseteq Y
with probability
0.99
.
The proof of
Lemma

6.5
is analogous to
Lemma

5.5
, and thus is omitted.

The following lemma claims the existence of
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate portal-respecting solution.
It is similar to
Lemma

5.6
, with the only difference that we now use the portal-respecting distance
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}
introduced in
Section

4.1
.
It allows us to compute portal-respecting solution in our facility location algorithm.

Lemma 6.6

(Good portal-respecting solution)
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
be a facility location instance,
S\subseteq Y
be a solution and
\mathcal{P}
be the hierarchical decomposition on
X\cup Y
in
Lemma

4.1
with portal parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
be the new client set constructed by
Lemma

6.5
.
Then with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\ocost(F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

(11)

Technically, proving
Lemma

6.6
will be more challenging than
Lemma

5.6
.
Let us first recall the key ideas behind the proof of
Lemma

5.6
.
Our main goal is to upper bound for every
x\in X^{\prime}
the highest level
\ell
where
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut.
For this purpose, consider client
x
and facility
\pi_{S}(x)
; if either
x
is not a bad client or
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility, then we can bound
\ell
by utilizing the fact that either
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
or
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))
is not badly cut.
Otherwise, we directly add
\pi_{S}(x)
to
F
, guaranteeing that
\pi_{S}(x)=\pi_{F}(x)=x
; and thus
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is never cut.

However, this idea does not work in our current setting.
Due to the definition of
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}
, we now need to bound the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
This introduces two main issues.
First,
\pi_{F}(x)
is in the high-dimensional space
Y
, but existing tools such as badly cut (
Lemma

6.3
) and bad points (
Definition

6.4
) are only defined w.r.t. subsets of
X
.
Second, in the case where both
x
is a bad client and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility,
we no longer have the property that
x
and
\pi_{F}(x)
are collocated/never cut in
X^{\prime}
, which makes it difficult to bound the cutting level.

To resolve these issues, we need some new ideas.
For the first issue, instead of directly considering the level where
\{x,\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut, we will consider the level
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut.
Since
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))
is in the low-dimensional space, we can still use the tools developed before.
The correctness of replacing
\pi_{F}(x)
with it nearest neighbor in
X
is guaranteed by
Lemma

4.3
.
The second issue is much more challenging,
because we have completely no information for any meaningful cutting level of
\{x,\pi_{F}(x)\}
in this bad case.
Nevertheless, we can still utilize the level
h(\pi_{S}(x))
, the level where ornament
\pi_{S}(x)
is attached to
\mathcal{P}
(Property
(2)
of
Lemma

4.1
),
and show that
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)\leq O(\dist(x,S))
.
This bound looks useless at first, because
\dist(x,S)
is not directly comparable with
\dist(x,F^{*})
.
However, we can show that bad cases happen with probability
\varepsilon
.
Hence the total error can be bounded by
O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.6
.

Recall that
F^{*}\subseteq Y
is the optimal solution and that
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
is the set of bad facilities in
S
.
We show that with probability
0.97
, solution

F:=F^{*}\cup\Bad_{S}

satisfies (
11
).
By
Lemma

6.5
,
X^{\prime}=\phi(x)
.
We thus rewrite the LHS of Equation (
11
) as

\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F).

To bound the connection cost
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)
for each
x\in X
, we consider the following cases.

Case 1:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, it is in
\Bad_{S}
, and thus in
F
.
If
\pi_{S}(x)\in X
, then
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))=\pi_{S}(x)
, and thus
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)=0
.
If
\pi_{S}(x)\in Y\setminus X
, then by the construction of
\mathcal{P}
, the set
\{\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x)\}
is cut exactly at level
h(\pi_{S}(x))
, the level where
\pi_{S}(x)
is a leaf node on
\mathcal{P}
.
By
Lemma

4.2
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x))+O(\rho)2^{h(\pi_{S}(x))}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+O(\rho)\frac{\dist(\pi_{S}(x),X)}{\sqrt{\rho}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,S).

(12)

Case 2:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Our plan is to upper bound the highest level where
\{\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.

Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility, by
Definitions

6.2
and
6.4
, the ball

\displaystyle B_{X}\Big(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{X}\Big(\phi(x),100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S)\Big)

is cut at level at most

\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Note that

\displaystyle\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x))))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))+\dist(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x))))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})

Since
F^{*}\subseteq F
.

Hence,
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\in B_{X}(\phi(x),100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))
.
Therefore, the highest level where
\{\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\}
is cut can be bounded by

\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

By
Lemma

4.3
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}(\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+\dist(\phi(x),S))

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\varepsilon)(\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+\dist(\phi(x),S)).

(13)

Case 3:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand, since
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}
, we have
\pi_{S}(x)\in F
.
Therefore,

\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,F)\leq 2\dist(x,S).

We have
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Hence, the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut is at most

\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

By
Lemma

4.3
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\frac{\dist(x,S)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(14)

Case 4:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
.
Following the proof in
Section

5
, consider the following two sub-cases:

If
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/(2\varepsilon)
,
first observe that
the ball
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand,

\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,F)\leq 2\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\frac{\dist(x,S)}{\varepsilon}.

Hence
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Therefore, the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut can be bounded by

\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Similar to Case 3, we can show that

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(15)

If
\dist(x,F^{*})>\dist(x,S)/(2\varepsilon)
, we utilize the fact that the ball

B_{X}\Big(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S)\Big)

is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
, by showing
both
x
and
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))
are contained in the ball.

First observe that

\displaystyle\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}).

Hence,
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\frac{1}{1-4\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})\leq 3\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})
.

Furthermore, note that

\displaystyle\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,S)<4\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})\leq 12\varepsilon\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})\leq 10\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}),

and

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)),\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))+\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,F)+2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,F^{*})+4\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle=(2+4\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 3(2+4\varepsilon)\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 10\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}).

We conclude that
x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))
.
Thus the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut can be bounded by

\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Similar to Case 2, we can show that

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\Big(\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(16)

Combining (
15
) with (
16
), we conclude that in this case,

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(17)

Combining above four cases (i.e., (
12
), (
13
), (
14
) and (
17
)), we have

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\notin\Bad_{X}\text{ or}\\
\pi_{S}(x)\notin\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}\Big((1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(\phi(x),S)\Big)

(18)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)+(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\floc(X^{\prime},F)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X^{\prime},F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X^{\prime},S)

(19)

To bound the RHS of (
19
), first note that by
Lemma

6.5
,

\displaystyle\text{with probability }0.99,\quad

\displaystyle\floc(X^{\prime},F)\leq\floc(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S),

\displaystyle\floc(X^{\prime},F^{*})\leq\floc(X,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\floc(X^{\prime},S)\leq\floc(X,S)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)\leq O(1)\floc(X,S)

(20)

hold simultaneously.

Next, we bound
\floc(X,F)
by
\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)
.
Write

\displaystyle\floc(X,F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F^{*})+\ocost(F^{*})+\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f)

\displaystyle=\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f).

Then

\displaystyle 0\leq\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)\leq\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f).

Taking expectation, we have

\displaystyle\E[\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)]

\displaystyle\leq\E\left[\sum_{f\in\Bad_{S}}\ocost(f)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in S}\ocost(f)\cdot\Pr[f\text{ is a bad facility}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\sum_{f\in S}\ocost(f)

By
Lemma

6.3

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

Applying Markov’s inequality to the (non-negative) random variable
\floc(X,F)-\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
,

\text{with probability }0.99,\qquad\floc(X,F)\leq\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

(21)

Finally, we bound
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)
. Observe that

\displaystyle\E\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}2\dist(x,S)\cdot\Pr[x\in\Bad_{X},\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{x\in X}2\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

6.3

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\cdot\floc(X,S).

Applying Markov’s inequality, we have

\text{with probability }0.99,\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)\leq O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

(22)

Combining (
20
), (
21
) and (
22
) with (
19
), we conclude that with probability
0.97

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F)

\displaystyle\leq+(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\floc(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\floc(X,S).

Rescaling
\varepsilon
concludes the proof.

∎

6.2
The Algorithm

Our algorithm is based on the dynamic programming framework proposed by
[
18
]
.
Given as input
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, the algorithm constructs the new hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{P}
on top of
X\cup Y
(
Algorithm

2
).
The dataset
X
is subsequently transformed to
X^{\prime}
according to
Lemma

6.5
.
We run a dynamic program for
X^{\prime}
on top of
\mathcal{P}
, with respect to the portal-respecting distance measure
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}
.
Although the DP framework is similar to that of
[
18
]
, our algorithm handles facilities in the ambient space, which is essentially different from the setting of
[
18
]
.
This new feature makes our algorithm more technical challenging.
We describe the algorithm as follows.

Preprocessing stage.

The algorithm first applies the techniques in
Appendix

A
to compute a
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation solution
S\subseteq Y
for facility location.
At first, this approximation might be too large, as it requires us to rescale the precision parameter
\varepsilon
by a factor of
1/2^{O(\ddim)}
, which may blow up the time complexity of our algorithm.
We ignore this potential issue when describing our algorithm at this point, and discuss how we can fix it in
Section

6.2.1
.

The algorithm then uses
Algorithm

2
to construct the new hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{P}
,
where the scaling parameter of portals is set to
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
For every
x\in X
, the algorithm checks if
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
(i.e., if
x
is a bad client).
It moves every bad client
x
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
,
3
3

3

In fact, this step can be efficiently done by computing
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN instead of the exact
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
This replacement does not affect the correctness of our previous analysis, and only enlarges the final approximation ratio by a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
factor.

creating the new instance
X^{\prime}
.

Dynamic program.

Each table entry of the dynamic program is represented by a cluster
C
on the modified decomposition
\mathcal{P}
, together with a configuration

\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},a_{C}^{2},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle,\qquad\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},b_{C}^{2},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle.

Roughly speaking, the configuration encodes the positional information of the current facility set
F
.
Specifically, each
a_{C}^{p}
encodes the distance from portal
p
to the closest facility inside
C
,
and
b_{C}^{p}
encodes the distance from portal
p
to the closest facility outside
C
.
The value stored in entry
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
is the minimum cost of
C
among all potential facility sets which are consistent with the configuration.
Formally,

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\colon\text{$F$ is consistent}\\
\text{with the configuration}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\cap C}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\ocost(F\cap C)\right\}.

Base case.

The base case of the dynamic program corresponds to the leaf nodes of
\mathcal{P}
which are in
X^{\prime}
.
Consider
x\in X^{\prime}
and its corresponding node
\{x\}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, together with a configuration
(a,b)
.
(Wlog, assume that
x
itself is the only portal of the node
\{x\}
.)
It is easy to check if the configuration is valid.
Recall that
a
encodes the distance from
x
to the closest facility in
\{x\}
.
Therefore, if
x\in Y\cap X^{\prime}
, then
a
is either
0
or
\infty
;
if
x\in X^{\prime}\setminus Y
, then
a
must be
\infty
.
We set
g(\{x\},a,b)=\infty
if the configuration is invalid.

If the configuration is valid, we then compute the cost inside
\{x\}
.
If
a=0
, then
x
is a facility in the solution, we thus have
\ocost(F\cap\{x\})=\ocost(x)
.
Otherwise,
\ocost(F\cap\{x\})=0
.
Furthermore, the connection cost of
x
is
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)=\min\{a,b\}
multiplied by
w(x)
, the number of copies of
x
in
X^{\prime}
.
Hence,

g(\{x\},a,b)=w(x)\cdot\min\{a,b\}+\ocost(F\cap\{x\}).

Updating the DP table.

Consider a higher level cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 1
, together with its configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle
.
Recall that
\Schild(C)
is the set of
C
’s children which are ornaments,
and
\Nchild(C)
is the set of non-ornament children of
C
.

To compute
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
, the algorithm enumerates all combinations of configurations for clusters
D\in\Nchild(C)
.
For each of these combinations

\Big\{\Big(D,\mathbf{a}_{D}=\langle a^{1}_{D},\dots,a^{|P_{D}|}_{D}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{D}=\langle b^{1}_{D},\dots,b^{|P_{D}|}_{D}\rangle\Big)\Big\}_{D\in\Nchild(C)},

(23)

we discuss in the following how to compute its cost
\Gamma=\Gamma(\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\Nchild(C)})
.
We will first need the following definition of weighted set cover problem.

Definition 6.7

(Weighted set cover problem)
.

Given a universe
U
with
m
elements and
n
subsets
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{n}\subseteq U
with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}=U
, together with weights
w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n}
,
compute a set of indices
I\subseteq[n]
that minimizes
\sum_{i\in I}w_{i}
, subject to
\bigcup_{i\in I}R_{i}=U
.

We compute the cost
\Gamma=\Gamma(\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\Nchild(C)})
by the following steps.

•

Step 0: Initialize.

Maintain a universe
U
, which is set to
\emptyset
initially.
For every ornament
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
, maintain a set
R_{y}
, which is set to
\emptyset
initially.

Roughly speaking,
U
is the set of portals that need to be served by an ornament in
\Schild(C)
,
and
R_{y}
contains the portals which can be served by
y
.

•

Step 1: Check consistency for
p\in P_{C}
.

For every
p\in P_{C}
, the algorithm finds
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, such that
a_{C}^{p}=a_{D}^{q}+\dist(p,q)
.
If such
D
and
q
exist, then check for the next
p\in P_{C}
.

Otherwise we add the pair
(C,p)
to
U
, indicating portal
p
needs to be served by a facility in
\Schild(C)
.
Our algorithm then finds all ornaments
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
that satisfy
a_{C}^{p}=\dist(p,y)
, and adds the pair
(C,p)
to
R_{y}
, indicating
p
can be served by
y
.
If no such
y
exists, we claim that the combination (
23
) is inconsistent, and return
\Gamma=\infty
.

•

Step 2: Check consistency for
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
.

For every
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, our algorithm first tries to find
p\in P_{C}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=b_{C}^{p}+\dist(p,q)
.
If such
p
exists, it means
q
connects to a facility outside
D
first via
p
, then to a facility outside
C
.
We can then check for the next
D
and
q
.

If such
p
does not exist, our algorithm tries to find a non-ornament cluster
D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C),D^{\prime}\neq D
and a portal
q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=a^{q^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}+\dist(q,q^{\prime})
.
If such
D^{\prime},q^{\prime}
exist, it means
q
connects to a facility outside
D
first via
q^{\prime}
, then to a facility inside
D^{\prime}
.
We can then check for the next
D
and
q
.

Assume there exist neither
p\in P_{C}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=b_{C}^{p}+\dist(p,q)
, nor
D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C),D^{\prime}\neq D
and
q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=a^{q^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}+\dist(q,q^{\prime})
.
We add the pair
(D,q)
to the universe
U
, indicating that portal
q\in P_{D}
needs to be served by a facility in
\Schild(C)
.
Our algorithm then finds all ornaments
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
that satisfy
b^{q}_{D}=\dist(q,y)
, and adds
(D,q)
to
R_{y}
, indicating
q
can be served by
y
.
If no such
y
exist, we claim that the combination (
23
) is inconsistent, and return
\Gamma=\infty
.

•

Step 3: Compute the opening cost for
\Schild(C)
.

At this stage,
the universe
U
is a collection of (cluster, portal) pairs:

U\subseteq\{C\}\times P_{C}\cup\bigcup_{D\in\Nchild(C)}\{D\}\times P_{D}.

(24)

For every
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
,
R_{y}
is a subset of
U
, and
\bigcup_{y\in\Schild(C)}R_{y}=U
.
We solve the weighted set cover problem (
Definition

6.7
) for universe
U
and set system
\{R_{y}\colon\{y\}\in\Schild(C)\}
, where the weight of each set
R_{y}
is
\ocost(y)
.
The opening cost for
\Schild(C)
is the optimal cost for this set cover instance, denoted by
\Gamma_{\text{open}}
.

•

Step 4: compute the total cost
\Gamma
.

If the combination (
23
) passes all the consistency checks, its cost is then computed by

\Gamma=\sum_{D\in\Nchild(C)}g(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})+\Gamma_{\text{open}}.

The value of
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
is the minimum
\Gamma
over all possible combinations of child configurations:

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})=\min_{\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\Nchild(C)}}\Gamma\Big(\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D})\}_{D\in\Nchild(C)}\Big).

Reducing the number of configurations.

Similar to
Section

5.2
, for every cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, we can restrict
a_{p},b_{p}
to multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in the range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
.
This reduces the number of configurations to
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}=2^{2^{t}}
for
t=O(\ddim\log(\ddim/\varepsilon))
.
The reason is exactly the same as
Section

5.2
, and thus is omitted here.

6.2.1
Proof of Correctness

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by our algorithm in
Section

5.2
.
Similar to
Section

5.2.1
,
we first show the following weaker bound for
\floc(X,\widehat{F})
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by the algorithm in
Section

6.2
w.r.t. a solution
S\subseteq Y
.
Then with constant probability,

\displaystyle\floc(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+2\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

Proof.

By
Section

6.2
,
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
minimizes the facility location cost w.r.t.
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, i.e.,

\widehat{F}=\argmin_{F\subseteq Y}\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\ocost(F).

We note that the definition of
X^{\prime}
depends on
S
.

By
Lemma

6.6
,
with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\ocost(F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S).

(25)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\floc(X,\widehat{F})

\displaystyle\leq\floc(X^{\prime},\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

By
Lemma

6.5
, w.p.
0.99

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,\widehat{F})+\ocost(F)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\ocost(F)+\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

By definition of
\widehat{F}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)+2\varepsilon\floc(X,S)

\displaystyle\text{By \eqref{eqn:apply_fl_bounded_clients_good_solution}}.

∎

We use the same bootstrap as in
[
18
, Section 3.3]
and
Section

5.2.1
.
Concretely, we can start from an arbitrary
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation
\widehat{F}_{0}
; at each stage
i
run the algorithm in
Section

6.2
with
S=\widehat{F}_{i}

to obtain a better approximate solution
\widehat{F}_{i+1}
.
The procedure halts when
i
reaches
c\cdot\ddim
for some sufficiently large constant
c
.
We return
\widehat{F}_{c\cdot\ddim}
as our final solution.
Since the success probability in
Lemma

6.8
can be boosted to
1-1/\ddim^{2}
by standard amplification,
we can guarantee that with constant probability, all steps of our bootstrap succeed.
Moreover, the bootstrap only introduces a
\poly(\ddim)
overhead in the running time.

The proof of
\floc(X,\widehat{F}_{c\cdot\ddim})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{\mathrm{fl}}(X,Y)
is the same as
Section

5.2.1
, and we omit it here.

6.2.2
Time Complexity.

Recall in
Section

6.2
, we need to solve a weighted set cover problem to determine the opening cost for every
\Schild(C)
.
It is well known that weighted set cover is NP-hard in general.
Nonetheless, we always have
m=|U|=O(1)
in our setting.
This allows us to compute weighted set cover exactly in
O(n)
time.

Lemma 6.9
.

There exists an algorithm that computes the weighted set cover problem (
Definition

6.7
) exactly in
O(2^{m}n)
time.

Proof.

Consider the following dynamic program.
For
i\in[n]
and
T\subseteq U
, let
h(i,T)
be the minimum weight of covering
T
by
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{i}
.
Specifically, the optimal value for the original problem is
h(n,U)
.
Define
h(0,\emptyset)=0
and
h(0,T)=\infty
for
T\neq\emptyset
, and observe that

h(i,T)=\min\{h(i-1,T),h(i-1,T\setminus R_{i})+w_{i}\}.

The value of all
h(i,T)
can be computed in time
O(2^{m}n)
.
∎

We are ready to prove
Theorem

6.1
for the time complexity.
By
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
, we can wlog assume that the aspect ratio of
X\cup Y
is
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
(time complexity).

We analyze time complexity for the preprocessing stage and the dynamic program separately.

Preprocessing stage.

The
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximate solution
S\subseteq Y
can be computed in
2^{O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
time by
Appendix

A
.
By
Lemma

4.1
, the new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
can be computed in time
\rho^{-O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta=(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}O(n+m)\log\Delta
.
By
[
18
]
,
the algorithm can find all bad client
x\in\Bad_{X}
(i.e.,
x\in X
s.t.
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is badly cut) in time
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}O(n\log\Delta)
.
Finally, to construct the new dataset
X^{\prime}
, the algorithm first uses the
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN data structure in
Lemma

2.9
to compute
\pi_{S}(x)
, then uses the
(1+\varepsilon)
-ANN data structure in
Lemma

2.7
to further compute
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
, and moves
x
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
The total time complexity is
\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log\Delta
.

In conclusion, the time complexity of the preprocessing stage is
(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log\Delta
.

Dynamic program.

Fix a non-ornament cluster
C
and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a_{C}^{1},\dots,a_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle
,
\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b_{C}^{1},\dots,b_{C}^{|P_{C}|}\rangle
.
When computing
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
, the algorithm enumerates all combinations of configurations for
D\in\Nchild(C)
in the form of (
23
), and computes the cost
\Gamma
for each combination.
We start by analyzing the time complexity of computing
\Gamma
.

In step 0, the algorithm initializes
R_{y}\leftarrow\emptyset
for
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
.
This takes
O(|\Schild(C)|)
time.

In step 1, checking consistency for
p\in P_{C}
requires going over all
D\in\Nchild(C),q\in P_{D}
and
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
.
It thus takes time

|P_{C}|\cdot\left(\sum_{D\in\Nchild(C)}|P_{D}|+|\Schild(C)|\right)=\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+\rho^{-O(\ddim)}|\Schild(C)|.

In step 2, checking consistency for
D\in\Nchild(C),q\in P_{D}
requires going over all
p\in P_{C}
,
D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C),q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
and
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
.
It thus takes time

\sum_{D\in\Nchild(C)}\sum_{q\in P_{D}}\left(|P_{C}|+\sum_{D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C)}|P_{D^{\prime}}|+|\Schild(C)|\right)=\rho^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot(1+|\Schild(C)|).

In step 3, the algorithm solves a weighted set cover problem for universe
U
given in (
24
), and set system
\{R_{y}\colon y\in\Schild(C)\}
.
By
Lemma

6.9
, this can be done in
O(2^{|U|}|\Schild(C)|)
time.
Since
|U|\leq\rho^{-O(\ddim)}
, the time complexity of step 3 is

2^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}|\Schild(C)|.

We conclude that the time complexity of computing the cost
\Gamma
for a single combination (
23
) is

\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+2^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}|\Schild(C)|.

Since there are a total of
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}
such combinations,
the time complexity of computing
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C})
is

(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\cdot\left(\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+2^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}|\Schild(C)|\right)\leq(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\cdot(1+|\Schild(C)|).

The total time complexity of filling the DP table is

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\\
\text{$C$ is non-ornament}\end{subarray}}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}\cdot(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\cdot(1+|\Schild(C)|)

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\\
\text{$C$ is non-ornament}\end{subarray}}(1+|\Schild(C)|)

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\Big(|\{C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\text{$C$ is non-ornament}\}|+|\{y\in Y\colon h(y)=\ell\}|\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}O(n+m)\log\Delta.

Combining the analysis above, we conclude that the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)\log\Delta
, for

\displaystyle t=O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

As mentioned in
Section

6.2.1
, the bootstrap only introduces a
\poly(\ddim)
overhead in the running time, which can be charged to the
2^{2^{t}}
factor.

Theorem

6.1
follows with
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.
∎

7
k
-Median with Low-dimensional Centers

In this section, we extend
Theorem

5.1
to
k
-median problem in the setting where the candidate center set has low doubling dimension.
Our main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 7.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m,\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
, computes a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of
k
-median in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

7.1
Structural Lemmas

Let
F^{*}\subseteq Y,|F^{*}|\leq k
be the optimal solution for
k
-median, and

S\subseteq Y,|S|\leq k
be a constant approximate solution for
k
-median.
We define bad clients
\Bad_{X}\subseteq X
and bad facilities
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
w.r.t.
F^{*}
,
S
and
\mathcal{H}
.
Analogous to
Lemma

5.5
, we have the following lemma that eliminates bad clients by moving them to the nearest facility in
S
.
The proof of
Lemma

7.2
is the same as
Lemma

5.5
, and thus is omitted.

Lemma 7.2

(New instance)
.

Given
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
, and a constant approximate solution
S\subseteq Y
for
k
-median,
construct a new
(multi-)set of clients
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\cup Y
as
X^{\prime}:=\phi(X)
for

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}x,&\text{if }x\notin\Bad_{X};\\
\pi_{S}(x),&\text{if }x\in\Bad_{X},\end{cases}

namely,
X^{\prime}
is constructed from
X
by moving every bad client
x
to
\pi_{S}(x)
.
Then with probability
0.99
,

\displaystyle\forall F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k,\qquad\Big|\cost_{k}(X,F)-\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y).

The following lemma guarantees the existence of
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate portal-respecting solution.
It is the same as
Lemma

5.6
, with an extra requirement that we now need
F
to have bounded size
F\leq k
.

Lemma 7.3

(Good portal-respecting solution)
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
be a
k
-median instance,
S\subseteq Y
be a constant approximate solution and
\mathcal{H}
be the hierarchical decomposition on
Y
in
Lemma

2.5
with portal parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
be the new client set constructed by
Lemma

7.2
.
For
x\in X^{\prime}
and
F\subseteq Y
, define the portal-respecting connection cost of
x
as

\displaystyle\ccostport(x,F):=\begin{cases}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F),&\text{if }x=\pi_{S}(x);\\
\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(x,F),&\text{if }x\neq\pi_{S}(x).\end{cases}

Then with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

(26)

To prove
Lemma

7.3
, we first show in the following lemma that there exists a facility set of size at most
k
containing
\Bad_{S}
.

Lemma

7.4
is essentially a combination of
[
18
, Claims 20 and 21]
.
We provide the proof in
Section

C.1
for completeness.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(Y)\leq\ddim
be a
k
-median instance, and
S\subseteq Y
be a constant approximate solution.
Then there exists a set of facilities
F\subseteq Y
, which satisfies the following properties.

(a)

\Bad_{S}\subseteq F
.

(b)

For every
x\in X\cup Y
,
\dist(x,F)\leq 6\dist(x,F^{*})+5\dist(x,S)
.

(c)

With probability
0.99
,
|F|\leq k
and
\cost_{k}(X,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)
.

Based on
Lemma

7.4
, we are ready to prove
Lemma

7.3
.
In fact, we show the facility set in
Lemma

7.4
satisfies the conditions in
Lemma

7.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.3
.

We show that the facility set in
Lemma

7.4
satisfies (
26
).
Rewrite the LHS of (
26
) as

\sum_{x\in X}\ccostport(\phi(x),F)+\ocost(F).

For every
x\in X
, we first bound the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut.
Specifically, let
\ell(x)
be the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}

is cut; we show that

\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon).

Consider the following cases:

Case 1:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(x)
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, it is in
\Bad_{S}
, and thus in
F
.
Therefore,
\pi_{S}(\phi(x))=\phi(x)=\pi_{F}(\phi(x))
;
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is never cut.

Case 2:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(x)
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility, the ball

B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))=B_{Y}(\phi(x),10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))

is cut at level at most
\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
By
Lemma

7.4
,

\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\leq 6\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+5\dist(\phi(x),S)=6\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}).

Then
\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\in B_{Y}(\phi(x),10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))
.
Hence, the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut can be bounded by
\ell(x)\leq\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

Case 3:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand, since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, we have
\pi_{S}(x)\in F
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))\leq 2\dist(x,S),

which implies
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Therefore,
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut at level at most
\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)=\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

Case 4:
x
is not a bad client in
X
, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
,
and the ball
B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))
is cut at level at most
\log(10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Consider the following sub-cases.

If
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/(10\varepsilon)
, then

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+6\dist(x,F^{*})+5\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

7.4

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon

\displaystyle\text{Since $\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/(10\varepsilon)$}.

Hence,
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
, and thus the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut is at most
\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)=\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

If
\dist(x,F^{*})>\dist(x,S)/(10\varepsilon)
, first note that

\displaystyle\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))=\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+\dist(x,S)\leq\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})+10\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*}),

which implies
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\tfrac{1}{1-10\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})
.
Then

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+\dist(x,\pi_{F}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+6\dist(x,F^{*})+5\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

7.4

\displaystyle\leq 6(1+10\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})

Since
\dist(x,S)<10\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(1+10\varepsilon)}{1-10\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})

\displaystyle\text{for }\varepsilon\leq 1/100.

This implies
\pi_{F}(x)\in B_{Y}(\pi_{S}(x),10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))
.
Therefore, the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{F}(x)\}
is cut is at most
\log(10\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)\leq\log(10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+10\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

In conclusion, we bound the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut by
\ell(x)\leq\log(\dist(\phi(x),S)/\varepsilon+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*}))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

We are now ready to prove (
26
).
Fix
x\in X
.
If
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(\phi(x))
, since
\ell(x)
is the highest level where
\{\pi_{S}(\phi(x)),\pi_{F}(\phi(x))\}
is cut,
by
Lemma

2.6
we have

\ccostport(\phi(x),F)=\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),F)\leq\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}.

If
\phi(x)\neq\pi_{S}(\phi(x))
, then
x
is not a bad client.
By
Lemma

3.5
, we have

\displaystyle\ccostport(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),F)\leq\widehat{\dist}_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon}.

For
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
we can bound the LHS of (
26
) as

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{x\in X}\ccostport(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
\phi(x)=\pi_{S}(\phi(x))\end{subarray}}(\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
\phi(x)\neq\pi_{S}(\phi(x))\end{subarray}}\left((1+O(\varepsilon))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\rho)2^{\ell(x)}+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\floc(\phi(X),F)+O(\rho)\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\sum_{x\in X}\left(\frac{2\dist(\phi(x),S)}{\varepsilon}+10\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},S)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F^{*})
