\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X,S)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)

Lemma

7.2

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)

Lemma

7.4

We conclude that with probability
0.9
,

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

∎

7.2
The Algorithm

Our
k
-median algorithm is a modification of the facility location algorithm in
Section

5.2
.

Preprocessing stage.

By
Appendix

A
, we can compute an
O(1)
-approximate solution
S\subseteq Y
for
k
-median in near-linear time, when
\ddim(Y)
is bounded.
The rest of the preprocessing stage is exactly the same as
Section

5.2
,
where we
1) construct the hierarchical decomposition
\mathcal{H}
with parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
,
2) construct the new instance
(X^{\prime},Y)
,
3) compute the revealed cluster
C(x)
for every
x\in X^{\prime}
and
4) construct the proxy set
N_{x}
for every
x\in X^{\prime}
.

Dynamic program.

The most significant difference of
k
-median from facility location is that the number of facilities is always bounded by
k
, which makes the dynamic program more complicated.
Analogous to the facility location algorithm, each entry of the DP table is represented by a cluster
C
on
\mathcal{H}
, together with a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},a^{2}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},b^{2}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
, which encodes the minimum (portal-respecting) distance from each portal to facilities inside and outside cluster
C
, respectively.
The entry is additionally encoded by a value
\Gamma_{C}\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
, which corresponds to the total revealed cost inside cluster
C
.

Given an entry encoded by
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
, the value stored in it is the minimum number of facilities required to be placed in
C
, such that the revealed cost inside
C
is at most
\Gamma_{C}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C}):=\min\Bigg\{K\colon\exists F\subset Y\text{ that is consistent with }\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\text{ s.t. }|F\cap C|=K

\displaystyle\text{ and }\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)\subseteq C}\ccostport(x,F)\leq\Gamma_{C}\Bigg\}.

(27)

Note that once we have the table entries corresponding to the root node
X^{\prime}
, it suffices to output the minimum
\Gamma_{X^{\prime}}
with
g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a}_{X^{\prime}},\mathbf{b}_{X^{\prime}},\Gamma_{X^{\prime}})\leq k
as the
k
-median value.

As in
Section

5.2
, for every level
\ell
cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, we discretize the configuration
a_{i},b_{i}
to be multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in the range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
.
Besides,
\Gamma
is discretized to be powers of
(1+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta})
in the range
[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.

Base case.

Consider a leaf node
C=\{y\}
and the corresponding configuration
a,b
and
\Gamma
.
(We can wlog assume that the only portal of
\{y\}
is
y
itself.)
It is easy to check consistency for
a,b
, since there is either a facility on
y
(then
a=0
) or no facility on
y
(then
a=\infty
).

The revealed cost in
\{y\}
can be easily computed.
Recall in the preprocessing stage, we have already marked the number of copies of
y
in
X^{\prime}
, denoted by
w(y)
.
For each of these copies, its connection cost can be read from the configuration, specifically,
\ccostport(y,F)=\min\{a,b\}
.
Therefore, the revealed cost in
\{y\}
is
w(y)\cdot\ccostport(y,F)
.

Finally, we compare the revealed cost
w(y)\cdot\ccostport(y,F)
with
\Gamma
.
If
w(y)\cdot\ccostport(y,F)>\Gamma
, then we set
g(\{y\},a,b,\Gamma)=\infty
, indicating this is not a consistent configuration.
If
w(y)\cdot\ccostport(y,F)\leq\Gamma
, then we set
g(\{y\},a,b,\Gamma)
to be
0
or
1
, depending on
a=\infty
or
0
(i.e., whether there is a facility on
y
).

Next, we show how to compute
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
for a higher level cluster
C
.
We first give an intuitive but inefficient algorithm.
Then following the idea of
[
18
]
, we show how to accelerate the algorithm using an auxiliary DP.

Updating the DP table – an inefficient solution.

Consider a higher level cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
, together with value
\Gamma_{C}
.
Following
Section

5.2
, the newly revealed cost in
C
can be computed from
\mathbf{a}_{C}
and
\mathbf{b}_{C}
.
Let

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}=\Gamma_{C}-\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)=C}\ccostport(x,F).

Note that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}
is the maximal cost that
C
is allowed to inherit from its child clusters.

To compute
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma)
, our algorithm enumerates all possible combinations of configurations for the child clusters of
C
.
For each of these combinations
\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D},\Gamma_{D})\}_{D\in\child(C)}
, the consistency of
\mathbf{a}_{D}
and
\mathbf{b}_{D}
with
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
can be checked in the same way as
Section

5.2
(i.e., criteria
(a)
and
(b)
).
We additionally require that
\sum_{D\in\child(C)}\Gamma_{D}\leq\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}
.
The DP table is then updated by

\displaystyle\qquad g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D},\Gamma_{D})\}_{D\in\child(C)}\\
\text{ is consistent}\text{ and }\sum_{D}\Gamma_{D}\leq\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}\end{subarray}}\sum_{D\in\child(C)}g(D,\mathbf{a}_{D},\mathbf{b}_{D},\Gamma_{D}).

(28)

One issue of (
28
) is that there are too many combinations of configurations to enumerate.
To be specific, the total number of combinations for each cluster
C
is

\displaystyle\prod_{D\in\child(C)}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D}|}\log^{2}\Delta=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}\log^{2^{O(\ddim)}}\Delta,

which will introduce an
O(\log^{2^{O(\ddim)}}\Delta)
factor in the
total running time.

Acceleration by an auxiliary DP.

To reduce this dependency of
\log^{2^{O(\ddim)}}\Delta
, we follow
[
18
]
to use an auxiliary DP to accelerate the computation.
In this auxiliary DP, children of
C
are given an arbitrary order
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{|\child(C)|}
.
Every DP table entry is encoded by a cluster
C
, one of its child cluster
D_{i}
, a set of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2},\mathbf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}
, and a value
\Gamma
.
The value in that table entry equals to the minimum number of facilities needed to be placed in
D_{i+1}\cup D_{i+2}\cup\dots\cup D_{|\child(C)|}
, such that the cost in
D_{i+1}\cup D_{i+2}\cup\dots\cup D_{|\child(C)|}
is at most
\Gamma
, given the configurations of
C,D_{1},\dots,D_{i}
.

The auxiliary DP table
h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)
can be updated by enumerating the configuration of the
next
child cluster, namely,
D_{i+1}
.

\displaystyle h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)=

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1}}\Big\{g(D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1})

\displaystyle+h(C,D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma-\Gamma_{i+1})\Big\}

(29)

\displaystyle h(C,D_{|\child(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\child(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\child(C)|},\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if configuration is consistent;}\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(30)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\min_{\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}}\Big\{g(D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1})+h(C,D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}-\Gamma_{1})\Big\}.

(31)

7.2.1
Proof of Correctness

Denote by
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
the solution returned by the dynamic program.
We note that
\widehat{F}
does not necessarily minimize

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)
, and it is even non-trivial to see if it gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for the minimum cost.
This potential misalignment is mainly due to the discretization tricks used in our dynamic program.
Recall that we only consider powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
for the value
\Gamma
.
Therefore, when we enumerate
\{\Gamma_{D}\}
with
\sum_{D}\Gamma_{D}\leq\Gamma
, every
\Gamma_{D}
is also a power of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
.
This discretization leads to the loss of other possible combinations of
\{\Gamma_{D}\}
,
which could result in a
\pm O(1)
additive error in the computation of
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
.
The error accumulates with levels and could become as large as
\poly(n)
on the root cluster
X^{\prime}
.
Nevertheless, we show the following lemma, whose proof is provided in
Section

C.2
for completeness.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by the algorithm in
Section

7.2
.
Then

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,\widehat{F})\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\min_{F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k}\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F).

Based on
Lemma

7.5
, we are ready to prove the correctness of our algorithm.

Proof of
Theorem

7.1
(correctness).

We will show that with constant probability, the facility set
\widehat{F}
computed by our algorithm satisfies
\cost_{k}(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)
.

By
Lemma

7.3
, with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k
, such that
\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X,\widehat{F})

\displaystyle\leq\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

By
Lemma

7.2
, w.p.
0.99

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

By
Lemma

7.5

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\text{By \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:kmedian_bounded_centers_good_solution}}.

Rescaling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

7.2.2
Time Complexity

We prove the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\tilde{O}(n+m)
for
t=O(\ddim\log(\ddim/\varepsilon))
.
By
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
, we can wlog assume that the aspect ratio of
X\cup Y
is
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.1
(time complexity).

The time complexity of the preprocessing stage is the same as the facility location algorithm, which is
(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log\Delta
.

We focus on the time complexity of filling the DP table.
Fix a cluster
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
.
We calculate the complexity of filling all table entries regarding
C
.

and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
, and a value
\Gamma_{C}\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.

The computation of
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
includes first computing

\Gamma^{\prime}_{C}=\Gamma_{C}-\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\colon C(x)=C}\ccostport(x,F).

By
Section

5.2.2
, this can be done in time

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|.

Then, the algorithm enumerates
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}
.
Recall that
a_{1}^{p},b_{1}^{p}
are multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in the range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
, and
\Gamma_{1}
is a power of
(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta})
in the range
[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.
Hence, there are
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
such combinations.
For each of these combinations, the value of
g(\cdot)+h(\cdot)
can be calculated in
O(1)
time, thus a total of
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
time.

Analogously, by (
29
), each entry in the auxiliary DP

h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)

can be computed in time
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
.

Therefore, the time complexity of the computation regarding a single cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is

\displaystyle\qquad(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}\log^{2}\Delta\cdot\Big(\varepsilon^{-O(\ddim)}\cdot|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|+(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{|\child(C)|-1}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|-2\sum_{j=1}^{i}|P_{D_{j}}|}\log^{2}\Delta\cdot(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{i+1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta

\displaystyle\leq(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\cdot(1+|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|).

The total complexity of filling the DP table is

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\cdot(1+|\{x\colon C(x)=C\}|)

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\Big(|\mathcal{H}_{\ell}|+|\{x\in X\colon x\text{ is revealed at level }\ell\}|\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log^{4}\Delta.

Finally, the algorithm finds the smallest
\Gamma_{X^{\prime}}
such that there exists
\mathbf{a}_{X^{\prime}},\mathbf{b}_{X^{\prime}}
satisfying
g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a}_{X^{\prime}},\mathbf{b}_{X^{\prime}},\Gamma_{X^{\prime}})\leq k
.
This takes extra
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
time.

Combining the analysis above, we conclude that the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)\log^{4}\Delta
, for

\displaystyle t=O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

Theorem

7.1
follows with
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.
∎

8
k
-Median with Low-dimensional Clients

In this section, we extend our results in
Section

6
to the
k
-median problem.
Our main result is the following.

Theorem 8.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm that, given as input
\varepsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
,
n,m,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
|X|=n,|Y|=m,\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, computes a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of
k
-median in time

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)

with constant success probability, where

t\in O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

8.1
Structural Lemmas

Let
F^{*}\subseteq Y,|F^{*}|\leq k
be the optimal
k
-median solution, and
Let
S\subseteq Y,|S|\leq k
be a constant approximate solution.
We define bad clients
\Bad_{X}\subseteq X
and bad facilities
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
w.r.t.
F^{*}
,
S
and
\mathcal{P}
.
We will use the same definition of bad clients and bad facilities in
Definition

6.4
,
w.r.t.
F^{*}
and
S
.
We first state the following lemma which eliminate bad clients in
X
.

Lemma 8.2

(New instance)
.

Given
(X\cup Y,\dist)
, with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
, and a constant approximate solution
S\subseteq Y
for
k
-median,
construct a new
(multi-)set of clients
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
as
X^{\prime}:=\phi(X)
for

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}x,&\text{if }x\notin\Bad_{X};\\
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),&\text{if }x\in\Bad_{X},\end{cases}

namely,
X^{\prime}
is constructed from
X
by moving every bad client
x
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Then with probability
0.99
,

\displaystyle\forall F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k,\qquad\Big|\cost_{k}(X,F)-\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y).

The proof of
Lemma

8.2
is analogous to
Lemma

5.5
, and thus is omitted.
The following lemma shows the existence of
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate portal-respecting solution.
It is similar to
Lemma

6.6
, with the only extra requirement that
|F|\leq k
.

Lemma 8.3

(Good portal-respecting solution)
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
be a
k
-median instance,
S\subseteq Y
be a constant approximate solution and
\mathcal{P}
be the hierarchical decomposition on
X\cup Y
in
Lemma

4.1
with portal parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
.
Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
be the new client set constructed by
Lemma

8.2
.
Then with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k
, such that

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

(32)

To prove
Lemma

8.3
, we use the following lemma, which claims the existence of a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution that contains all bad facilities in
S
.

Lemma

8.4
is essentially a combination of
[
18
, Claims 20 and 21]
.
We provide the proof in
Section

D.1
for completeness.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
be a
k
-median instance, and
S\subseteq Y
be a constant approximate solution.
Then there exists a set of facilities
F\subseteq Y
, which satisfies the following properties.

(a)

\Bad_{S}\subseteq F
.

(b)

For every
x\in X
,
\dist(x,F)\leq 6\dist(x,F^{*})+5\dist(x,S)
.

(c)

With probability
0.99
,
|F|\leq k
and
\cost_{k}(X,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)
.

We prove
Lemma

8.3
based on
Lemma

8.4
.
In fact, we show that the solution
F
in
Lemma

8.4
satisfies (
32
).
The proof is very similar to
Lemma

6.6
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.3
.

Recall that
F^{*}\subseteq Y
is the optimal solution and that
\Bad_{S}\subseteq S
is the set of bad facilities in
S
.
We show that with probability
0.9
, the solution
F
in
Lemma

8.4
satisfies (
32
).
By
Lemma

8.2
,
X^{\prime}=\phi(x)
.
We thus rewrite the LHS of (
32
) as

\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F).

To bound the connection cost
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)
for each
x\in X
, we consider the following cases.

Case 1:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility, it is in
\Bad_{S}
, and thus in
F
.
If
\pi_{S}(x)\in X
, then
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))=\pi_{S}(x)
, and thus
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)=0
.
If
\pi_{S}(x)\in Y\setminus X
, then by the construction of
\mathcal{P}
, the set
\{\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x)\}
is cut exactly at level
h(\pi_{S}(x))
, the level where
\pi_{S}(x)
is a leaf node on
\mathcal{P}
.
By
Lemma

4.2
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),\pi_{S}(x))+O(\rho)2^{h(\pi_{S}(x))}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))+O(\rho)\frac{\dist(\pi_{S}(x),X)}{\sqrt{\rho}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,\pi_{S}(x))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,S).

(33)

Case 2:
x
is a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

By the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
\phi(x)=\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.
Our plan is to upper bound the highest level where
\{\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\}
is cut w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.

Since
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility, by
Definitions

6.2
and
6.4
, the ball

\displaystyle B_{X}\Big(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{X}\Big(\phi(x),100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S)\Big)

is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Note that

\displaystyle\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x))))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(\phi(x),\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))+\dist(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x))))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq 12\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+10\dist(\phi(x),S)

By
Lemma

8.4
.

Hence,
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\in B_{X}(\phi(x),100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))
.
Therefore, the highest level where
\{\phi(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(\phi(x)))\}
is cut is at most

\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+100\dist(\phi(x),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
By
Lemma

4.3
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}(\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+\dist(\phi(x),S))

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{\rho})\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\varepsilon)(\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+\dist(\phi(x),S)).

(34)

Case 3:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
, and the ball
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand, since
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}
, we have
\pi_{S}(x)\in F
by
Lemma

8.4
.
Therefore,

\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,F)\leq 2\dist(x,S).

We have
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Hence, the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut is at most

\ell\leq\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.

By
Lemma

4.3
,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle=\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\sqrt{\rho})\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\frac{\dist(x,S)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(35)

Case 4:
x
is not a bad client, and
\pi_{S}(x)
is not a bad facility.

In this case
\phi(x)=x
.
Following the proof in
Sections

5
,
6
and
7
, consider the following two sub-cases:

If
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\dist(x,S)/(20\varepsilon)
,
first observe that
the ball
B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
is cut at level at most
\ell\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand,

\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,F)\leq 12\dist(x,F^{*})+10\dist(x,S)\leq\frac{\dist(x,S)}{\varepsilon}.

Hence
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(x,\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)
.
Therefore, the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut is at most

\ell\log(\dist(x,S)/\varepsilon)+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Using a similar argument as Case 3, we can show that

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(36)

If
\dist(x,F^{*})>\dist(x,S)/(20\varepsilon)
, we plan to utilize the fact that

B_{X}(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))

is cut at level at most
\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
, by showing
both
x
and
\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))
is contained in this ball.

First observe that

\displaystyle\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,S)+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 40\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}).

Hence,
\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\frac{1}{1-40\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})
.

Furthermore, note that

\displaystyle\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))\leq 2\dist(x,S)<40\varepsilon\dist(x,F^{*})\leq\frac{40\varepsilon}{1-40\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})\leq 100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}),

and that

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)),\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x)))+\dist(x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq 2\dist(x,F)+2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle\leq 12\dist(x,F^{*})+12\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

8.4

\displaystyle\leq 12(1+20\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12(1+20\varepsilon)}{1-40\varepsilon}\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*}),

for
\varepsilon\leq 1/100
.
We conclude that
x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\in B_{X}(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))
.
Thus the highest level where
\{x,\pi_{X}(\pi_{F}(x))\}
is cut is at most

\ell\leq\log(100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+100\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S))+\log(\ddim/\varepsilon)
.
Similar to Case 2, we can show that

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\Big(\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),F^{*})+\dist(\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x)),S)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(37)

Combining (
36
) with (
37
), we conclude that in this case,

\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)\leq(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(x,F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(x,S).

(38)

Combining above four cases (i.e., (
33
), (
34
), (
35
) and (
38
)), we have

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\notin\Bad_{X}\text{ or}\\
\pi_{S}(x)\notin\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}\Big((1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\dist(\phi(x),F)+O(\varepsilon)\dist(\phi(x),F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\dist(\phi(x),S)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)+(1+O(\sqrt{\rho}))\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},S)

(39)

By
Lemma

8.2
,

\displaystyle\text{with probability }0.99,\quad

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F)\leq\cost_{k}(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y),

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},F^{*})\leq\cost_{k}(X,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)=(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},S)\leq\cost_{k}(X,S)+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)\leq O(1)\opt_{k}(X,Y)

(40)

hold simultaneously.

By
Lemma

8.4
,

\text{with probability }0.99,\qquad|F|\leq k\text{ and }\cost_{k}(X,F)\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y).

(41)

Finally, observe that

\displaystyle\E\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}2\dist(x,S)\cdot\Pr[x\in\Bad_{X},\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{x\in X}2\dist(x,S)

By
Lemma

6.3

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\cdot\cost_{k}(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\cdot\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Applying Markov’s inequality, we have

\text{with probability }0.99,\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Bad_{X},\\
\pi_{S}(x)\in\Bad_{S}\end{subarray}}2\dist(x,S)\leq O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

(42)

Combining (
40
), (
41
) and (
42
) with (
39
), we conclude that with probability
0.97
,
|F|\leq k
and

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\phi(x),F)

\displaystyle\leq O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)+(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X,F)+O(\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Rescaling
\varepsilon
concludes the proof.

∎

8.2
The Algorithm

Our
k
-median algorithm is a dynamic program on top of the new decomposition
\mathcal{P}
.
The framework is similar to the algorithm in
Section

7.2
, with the complication of handling ornaments on
\mathcal{P}
.

Preprocessing stage.

By
Appendix

A
, we can compute an
O(1)
-approximate solution
S\subseteq Y
for
k
-median in near-linear time, when
\ddim(X)
is bounded.
The rest of the preprocessing stage is exactly the same as
Section

6.2
, where we
1) use
Algorithm

2
to compute the decomposition
\mathcal{P}
on top of
X\cup Y
with scaling parameter
\rho=\varepsilon^{10}/\ddim^{2}
,
and 2) construct the new dataset
X^{\prime}
by moving every bad client
x\in X
to
\pi_{X}(\pi_{S}(x))
.

Dynamic program.

Analogous to
Section

7.2
, our algorithm consists of a main DP
g(\cdot)
and an auxiliary DP
h(\cdot)
.

In the main DP, each entry in the DP table is encoded by
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
.
The configurations
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},a^{2}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\ \mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},b^{2}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
encode the minimum (portal-respecting) distance from each portal to facilities inside and outside cluster
C
, respectively.
The real value
\Gamma_{C}\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
corresponds to the total connection cost inside
C
.
The DP value
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
equals to the minimum number of facilities required to be placed in
C
, such that the connection cost inside
C
is at most
\Gamma_{C}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C}):=\min\Bigg\{K\colon\exists F\subseteq Y

\displaystyle\text{ that is consistent with }\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }|F\cap C|=K\text{ and }\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}\cap C}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)\leq\Gamma_{C}\Bigg\}.

(43)

Note that once we have the table entries corresponding to the root node
X^{\prime}
, it suffices to output the minimum
\Gamma
with
g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma)\leq k
as the
k
-median value.

To define the auxiliary DP, as in
Section

7.2
, we need an order for the non-ornament child clusters of
C
.
Recall that
\Nchild(C)
is the set of non-ornament child clusters of
C
and
\Schild(C)
is the set of ornament child clusters of
C
.
Consider an arbitrary order of
\Nchild(C)
as
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{|\Nchild(C)|}
.
Each table entry of the auxiliary DP
h
is encoded by a cluster
C
, its
i
-th non-ornament child cluster
D_{i}
, a set of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}
, and a value
\Gamma
.
The value in that table entry

h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)

equals to the minimum number of facilities needed to be placed in

C\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}D_{j}=\bigcup_{j=i+1}^{|\Nchild(C)|}D_{j}\cup\bigcup_{\{y\}\in\Schild(C)}\{y\},

such that the cost in
C\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}D_{j}
is at most
\Gamma
, given the configurations of
C,D_{1},\dots,D_{i}
.

For a level
\ell
cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, we discretize the configurations
a_{p},b_{p}
to be multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in the range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
.
Besides,
\Gamma
is discretized to be powers of
(1+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta})
in the range
[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.

Base case.

The base case of the dynamic program corresponds to the leaf nodes of
\mathcal{P}
which are in
X^{\prime}
.
Consider
x\in X^{\prime}
and its corresponding node
\{x\}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, together with a configuration
(a,b,\Gamma)
.
(We can wlog assume
\{x\}
has itself as its only portal.)
It is easy to check if
(a,b)
is valid.
Recall that
a
encodes the distance from
x
to the closest facility in
\{x\}
.
Therefore, if
x\in Y\cap X^{\prime}
, then
a
is either
0
or
\infty
;
if
x\in X^{\prime}\setminus Y
, then
a
must be
\infty
.
We set
g(\{x\},a,b,\Gamma)=\infty
if
(a,b)
is invalid.

To further check if
\Gamma
is consistent with
(a,b)
, we can compare the connection cost of
x
with
\Gamma
.
Specifically, the connection cost of
x
is
\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)=\min\{a,b\}
multiplied by
w(x)
, the number of copies of
x
in
X^{\prime}
.
If
\Gamma<w(x)\cdot\min\{a,b\}
, i.e., the connection cost inside
\{x\}
already exceeds
\Gamma
,
then
\Gamma
is inconsistent with
(a,b)
.
We set
g(\{x\},a,b,\Gamma)=\infty

If
(a,b,\Gamma)
is consistent, we set
g(a,b,\Gamma)
to be the number of facilities in
\{x\}
, which is either
0
or
1
, depending on whether
a
is
\infty
or
0
.

Updating the DP table.

Consider a higher level cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
with
\ell\geq 1
, together with its configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
and value
\Gamma_{C}
.
To compute
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
, the algorithm enumerates the configuration of its first non-ornament child
D_{1}
; the number of facilities inside
C
equals to the number of facilities in
D_{1}
plus the number of facilities in
C\setminus D_{1}
.
Formally,

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C}):=\min_{\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}}\Big\{g(D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1})+h(C,D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{C}-\Gamma_{1})\Big\}.

(44)

For every
1\leq i\leq|\Nchild(C)|-1
, to compute the auxiliary DP entry
h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)
, our algorithm enumerates the configuration of
C
’s
(i+1)
-th non-ornament child cluster
D_{i+1}
.
The number of facilities inside
C\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}D_{j}
equals to the number of facilities in
D_{i+1}
plus the number of facilities in
C\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i+1}D_{j}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)=

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1}}\Big\{g(D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1})

\displaystyle+h(C,D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma-\Gamma_{i+1})\Big\}.

(45)

Finally, we discuss how to compute

h(C,D_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\Gamma).

This corresponds to the number of facilities in
\bigcup_{\{y\}\in\Schild(C)}\{y\}
.
We use the (almost) same 4-step procedure as
Section

6.2
.

•

Step 0: Initialize.

Maintain a universe
U
, which is set to
\emptyset
initially.
For every ornament
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
, maintain a set
R_{y}
, which is set to
\emptyset
initially.

Roughly speaking,
U
is the set of portals that need to be served by a facility in
\Schild(C)
,
and
R_{y}
contains the portals which can be served by
y
.

•

Step 1: Check consistency for
p\in P_{C}
.

For every
p\in P_{C}
, the algorithm finds
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, such that
a_{C}^{p}=a_{D}^{q}+\dist(p,q)
.
If such
D
and
q
exists, then check for the next
p\in P_{C}
.

Otherwise, such
D
and
q
do not exist.
We add the pair
(C,p)
to
U
, indicating portal
p
needs to be served by a facility in
\Schild(C)
.
Our algorithm then finds all ornaments
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
that satisfy
a_{C}^{p}=\dist(p,y)
, and adds the pair
(C,p)
to
R_{y}
, indicating
p
can be served by
y
.
If no such
y
exists, we claim that the configuration

(\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|})

is inconsistent, and return
h(C,D_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\Gamma)=\infty
.

•

Step 2: Check consistency for
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
.

For every
D\in\Nchild(C)
and
q\in P_{D}
, our algorithm first tries to find
p\in P_{C}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=b_{C}^{p}+\dist(p,q)
.
If such
p
exists, it means
q
connects to a facility outside
D
first via
p
, then to a facility outside
C
.
We can then check for the next
D
and
q
.

If such
p
does not exist, our algorithm tries to find a non-ornament cluster
D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C),D^{\prime}\neq D
and a portal
q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=a^{q^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}+\dist(q,q^{\prime})
.
If such
D^{\prime},q^{\prime}
exists, it means
q
connects to a facility outside
D
first via
q^{\prime}
, then to a facility inside
D^{\prime}
.
We can then check for the next
D
and
q
.

Assume there exist neither
p\in P_{C}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=b_{C}^{p}+\dist(p,q)
, nor
D^{\prime}\in\Nchild(C),D^{\prime}\neq D
and
q^{\prime}\in P_{D^{\prime}}
, such that
b^{q}_{D}=a^{q^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}+\dist(q,q^{\prime})
.
We add the pair
(D,q)
to the universe
U
, indicating that portal
q\in P_{D}
needs to be served by a facility in
\Schild(C)
.
Our algorithm then finds all ornaments
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
that satisfy
b_{D}^{q}=\dist(q,y)
, and adds
(D,q)
to
R_{y}
, indicating
q
can be served by
y
.
If no such
y
exists, we claim that the configuration

(\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|})

is inconsistent,
and return
h(C,D_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\Gamma)=\infty
.

•

Step 3: Compute the number of facilities in
\Schild(C)
.

At this stage,
the universe
U
is a collection of (cluster, portal) pairs:

U\subseteq\{C\}\times P_{C}\cup\bigcup_{D\in\Nchild(C)}\{D\}\times P_{D}.

(46)

For every
\{y\}\in\Schild(C)
,
R_{y}
is a subset of
U
, and
\bigcup_{y\in\Schild(C)}R_{y}=U
.
We solve the unweighted set cover problem for universe
U
and set system
\{R_{y}\colon\{y\}\in\Schild(C)\}
.

h(C,D_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\Gamma)

is set to
the optimal cost for this set cover instance, i.e., the minimum number of sets required to cover the universe
U
.

8.2.1
Proof of Correctness

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by the algorithm.
Similar to
Lemma

7.5
, we can show the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to
Section

D.2
:

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\widehat{F}\subseteq Y
be the set of facilities returned by the algorithm in
Section

8.2
.
Then

\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,\widehat{F})\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\min_{F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k}\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F).

Based on
Lemma

8.5
, we prove the correctness of our algorithm.

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
(correctness).

We will show that with constant probability, the facility set
\widehat{F}
computed by our algorithm satisfies
\cost_{k}(X,\widehat{F})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)
.

By
Lemma

8.3
, with probability
0.9
, there exists a solution
F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k
, such that
\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X,\widehat{F})

\displaystyle\leq\cost_{k}(X^{\prime},\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

By
Lemma

8.2
, w.p.
0.99

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,\widehat{F})+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\dist_{\mathrm{port}}^{\mathcal{P}}(x,F)+\varepsilon\opt_{k}(X,Y)

By
Lemma

8.5

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\text{By \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:kmedian_bounded_clients_good_solution}}.

Rescaling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

8.2.2
Time Complexity

We prove the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\tilde{O}(n+m)
for
t=O(\ddim\log(\ddim/\varepsilon))
.
By
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
, we can wlog assume that the aspect ratio of
X\cup Y
is
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
(time complexity).

The preprocessing stage is the same as
Section

6.2
, and thus has time complexity
(\ddim/\varepsilon)^{O(\ddim)}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log\Delta
.

We focus on the time complexity of filling the DP table.
Fix a cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
and a configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C}=\langle a^{1}_{C},\dots,a^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle,\mathbf{b}_{C}=\langle b^{1}_{C},\dots,b^{|P_{C}|}_{C}\rangle
, and a value
\Gamma_{C}\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.

The computation of
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
includes an enumeration of
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}
.
Recall that
a_{p}^{C},b_{p}^{C}
are multiplications of
\rho 2^{\ell}
in the range
[0,2^{\ell}/\varepsilon]
, and
\Gamma_{C}
is a power of
(1+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta})
in the range
[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
.
Hence, there are
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
such combinations.
For each of these combinations, the value of
g(\cdot)+h(\cdot)
can be calculated in
O(1)
time, thus a total of
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
time.

For
1\leq i\leq|\Nchild(C)|-1
, the computation of
h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma)
is analogous, thus
(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{2}\Delta
time in total.

The computation of
h(C,D_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\mathbf{b}_{|\Nchild(C)|},\Gamma)

is more involved.
By
Section

6.2.2
, it can be computed in time

\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+2^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}|\Schild(C)|.

Therefore, the time complexity of the computation regarding a single cluster
C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is

\displaystyle\qquad(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|}\log^{2}\Delta\cdot(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{|\Nchild(C)|-1}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|-2\sum_{j=1}^{i}|P_{D_{j}}|}\log^{2}\Delta\cdot(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{D_{i+1}}|}\log^{2}\Delta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(\varepsilon\rho)^{-2|P_{C}|-2\sum_{j=1}^{|\Nchild(C)|}|P_{D_{j}}|}\log^{2}\Delta(\rho^{-O(\ddim)}+2^{\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}|\Schild(C)|)

\displaystyle\leq(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\cdot(1+|\Schild(C)|).

The total complexity of filling the DP table is

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\\
\text{$C$ is non-ornament}\end{subarray}}(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\cdot(1+|\Schild(C)|)

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon\rho)^{-\rho^{-O(\ddim)}}\log^{4}\Delta\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\Big(|\{C\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\text{$C$ is non-ornament}\}|+|\{y\in Y\colon h(y)=\ell\}|\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\left(\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right)^{O(\ddim)}}\tilde{O}(n+m)\log^{4}\Delta.

Combining the analysis above, we conclude that the time complexity of our algorithm is
2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n+m)\log^{4}\Delta
, for

\displaystyle t=O\left(\ddim\log\frac{\ddim}{\varepsilon}\right).

Theorem

8.1
follows with
\Delta=\poly(n,m)
.
∎

9
(k,\ell)
-Median Clustering of Polygonal Curves under Discrete Fréchet Distance

In this section, we apply our method to obtain new results for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem of polygonal curves under the discrete Fréchet distance. This is a standard variant of the
k
-median problem, where the centers are constrained to have
\ell
vertices. We first establish the general result and then specialize to the case

d=1
, in which we achieve an improved running time by means of a novel complexity-reduction technique that may be of independent interest.

The following statement about the doubling dimension of the discrete Fréchet distance is probably folklore, but we include a proof for completeness.

Proposition 9.1
.

The metric space defined on the equivalence classes of polygonal curves in
\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
that have pairwise discrete Fréchet distance
0
,
equipped with the discrete Fréchet distance, has doubling dimension
\Theta(dz)
.

Proof.

We first show that the doubling dimension is at least
dz
. For each
i\in[d]
, let
e_{i}
be the
i
-th vector of the standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
e_{i}
has
1
appearing in the
i
-th position and
0
in all other positions.
Consider a polygonal curve
\pi=\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{z}\rangle
where each
x_{i}=(3\cdot i,0\ldots,0)
for each
i\in[z]
. Let
B
be the discrete Fréchet ball of radius
1
centered at
\pi
, i.e.,
B=\{\tau\in\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)\leq 1\}
. All polygonal curves defined by sequences in
\prod_{i=1}^{z}\bigcup_{j=1}^{d}\{x_{i}-e_{j},x_{i}+e_{j}\}
are in
B
, but no two of them can be covered by the same ball of radius
1/2
. Hence, we need at least
2^{dz}
balls of radius
1/2
to cover
B
, implying that the doubling dimension is at least
dz
.

For the upper bound, consider any polygonal curve
\pi=\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{z}\rangle
and any radius
r>0
. Let
B_{r}
be the discrete Fréchet ball of radius
r
centered at
\pi
. For each
i\in[z]
, let
S_{i}
be a minimal
r/2
-covering set of the Euclidean ball of radius
r
centered at
x_{i}
, i.e., for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\|p-x_{i}\|_{2}\leq r
, there exists a
p^{\prime}\in S_{i}
such that
\|p-p^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq r/2
. It is known (see e.g.
[
28
]
) that
|S_{i}|=2^{O(d)}
.
Let
T^{\ast}
be an optimal traversal between
\pi
and an arbitrary polygonal curve
\tau=\langle q_{1},\ldots,q_{z}\rangle\in B_{r}
. For each
j\in[z]
, let
i_{j}
be the first index (pointing to the
i_{j}
-th vertex of
\pi
) paired with the
j
-th vertex of
\tau
in
T^{\ast}
. It must hold that there is a
y_{j}\in S_{i_{j}}
such that
\|q_{j}-y_{j}\|_{2}\leq r/2
.
Hence, the sequence
\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{z}\rangle
defines a polygonal curve with discrete Fréchet distance within
r/2
from
\tau
. By taking into account all traversals and for each traversal all relevant combinations of points of
S_{i}
, we cover the entire
B_{r}
with balls of radius
r/2
. By
[
24
, Lemma 4]
,
the number of traversals is at most
4^{z}
, and for each traversal, we consider at most
2^{O(dz)}
polygonal curves as centers of the balls of radius
r/2
, implying an upper bound of
O(dz)
on the doubling dimension.
∎

We begin with an auxiliary lemma on discretizing the set of polygonal curves that have complexity
\ell
and are within distance
r
from an arbitrary polygonal curve of complexity
z
. This was essentially proven and used by Filtser et al.
[
24
]
in the context of approximate nearest neighbor data structures. Since there is no standalone lemma with the exact same statement in
[
24
]
, we include a proof for completeness.

Lemma 9.2
.

Given a polygonal curve
\pi\in\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
, threshold
r>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we can compute a set of polygonal curves
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r,\varepsilon}
, in time
O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}+O(dz\log z)
, such that
|\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r,\varepsilon}|\in O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}
and for all
\tau\in\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
, if
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)\leq r
then there exists
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{{\pi},r,\varepsilon}
such that
\dist_{dF}(\tau^{\prime},\tau)\leq\varepsilon r
.

Proof.

We first compute a polygonal curve
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
such that
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq 2r
or determine that there is no curve in
\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
within distance
r
from
\pi
, using
[
24
, Lemma 10]
, in time
O(dz\log z)
. If there is no curve in
\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
within distance
r
from
\pi
then we return
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r,\varepsilon}\leftarrow\emptyset
. Otherwise, we proceed as follows.
Let
\tau=\langle q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
,
\pi^{\prime}=\langle\tilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{x}_{\ell}\rangle
and
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon r}
be the regular grid in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with cell width
\varepsilon rd^{-1/2}
. We define
\tilde{I}_{j}:=\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid\|p-\tilde{x}_{j}\|_{2}\leq(3+\varepsilon)r\}\cap\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon r}
, for any
j\in[\ell]
.
For each traversal
T
of two polygonal curves of complexity
\ell

we compute a set of polygonal curves
\mathcal{S}_{T}
as follows: for each
j\in[\ell]
let
i_{j}
be the index of the first vertex matched to the vertex at index
j
in the traversal, i.e.,
i_{j}=\min\{i:\penalty\ (i,j)\in T\}
. We compute
\mathcal{S}_{T}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\tilde{I}_{i_{j}}
and we output
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r,\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{T}\mathcal{S}_{T}
.

Each
\tilde{I}_{j}
contains at most
O(1/\varepsilon)^{d}
points. Hence, for a fixed traversal
T
, there are
O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}
combinations of vertices defining the polygonal curves included in
\mathcal{S}_{T}
. By
[
24
, Lemma 4]
, the number of different traversals is at most
4^{\ell}
. Hence, the running time of computing the set
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r,\varepsilon}
is
O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}+O(dz\log z)
and its size is upper bounded by
O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}
.

To show correctness, we first observe that if
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)\leq r
, then by the triangle inequality
\dist_{dF}(\pi^{\prime},\tau)\leq\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\dist_{dF}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\leq 3r
. Hence, there is an optimal traversal
T^{\ast}
matching the vertices of
\tau
with vertices of
\pi^{\prime}
with cost at most
3r
, which implies that there exists a polygonal curve in
\mathcal{S}_{T^{\ast}}
with vertices
q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{\ell}^{\prime}
such that for any
j\in[\ell]
,
\|q_{j}^{\prime}-q_{j}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon r
. Therefore,
\dist_{dF}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon r
.

∎

We require the following definition of simplifications.

Definition 9.3

(minimum-error
\ell
-simplification)
.

For a curve
x\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{z}
a curve
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{\ell}
is a minimum-error
\ell
-simplification of
x
if for any curve
y\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{\ell}
it holds that
\dist_{dF}(x,\tilde{x})\leq\dist_{dF}(x,y)
.

The minimum-error
\ell
-simplification
\tilde{x}
of some curve
x\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{z}
can be computed in time
O(2^{O(d)}\ell z\log(z)\log(z/\ell))

[
6
]
.

Now we present a simple constant factor approximation algorithm, which was previously used and analyzed in
[
22
]
in a slightly different setting. We give a proof for the sake of completeness.

1

Input:

\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}

2

Let
\tilde{P}\leftarrow\{\tilde{x}_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
, where
\tilde{x}_{i}
is a
minimum-error
\ell
-simplification of
x_{i}

3

Let
C
be the solution computed by a constant factor approximation algorithm for
k
-median on
(\tilde{P},\dist_{dF})

4

return

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},C)+\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i})

5

Algorithm 3

(k,\ell)
-MedianConstantApprox

Lemma 9.4
.

Given
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\in\mathbb{X}^{d}_{z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Algorithm
3
computes in time
O(2^{O(d\ell)}d\ell^{2}nz(\log(z)\log(z/\ell)+\log^{9}n))
a constant factor approximation for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem.

Proof.

We start by showing that the computed value is indeed a constant approximation to the optimal cost.
Let
C^{*}
be an optimal solution for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem with cost
\delta^{*}
and let
c^{*}_{i}\in C^{*}
be the closest center to
x_{i}
.
Additionally let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
denote the approximation factor of
C
.
By triangle inequality we get the following lower bound.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},C)+\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(x_{i},C)

\displaystyle\geq\delta^{*}

Next we can upper bound the returned value as follows, by utilizing the definition of a minimum-error
\ell
-simplification

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x_{i}},C)+\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x_{i}},x_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x_{i}},C)+\delta^{*}.

Then, again by using triangle inequality and the definition of minimum-error
\ell
-simplification we get

\displaystyle\delta^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(x_{i},c^{*}_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},c^{*}_{i})-\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},c^{*}_{i})-\delta^{*}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x}_{i},C)-\delta^{*},

which yields
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x_{i}},C)+\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x_{i}},x_{i})\leq(2\lambda+1)\delta^{*}
.

Next we analyze the running time of Algorithm
3
. Calculating the set
\tilde{P}
takes
O(n2^{O(d)}\ell z\log(z)\log(z/\ell))
time
[
6
]
. Computing
C
takes
O(d\ell^{2}2^{O(d\ell)}n\log 9n)
time by using the constant approximation algorithm in
[
18
]
, Proposition
9.1
and the fact that computing the discrete Fréchet distance between to curves in
\mathbb{X}^{d}_{\ell}
takes
O(d\ell^{2})
time using a straightforward dynamic programming algorithm
[
2
]
.
The total running time is therefore
O(2^{O(d\ell)}d\ell^{2}nz(\log(z)\log(z/\ell)+\log^{9}n))

∎

We now proceed by computing a set of candidate centers, i.e., a set of polygonal curves with complexity
\ell
, from which the
k
centers can be chosen, while only sacrificing an arbitrarily small approximation factor from the cost of the optimal solution.

Lemma 9.5
.

Given a set of
n
polygonal curves
\Pi\subset\mathbb{X}_{z}^{d}
and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we can compute in time
O(2^{O(d\ell)}d\ell^{2}nz(\log(z)\log(z/\ell)+\log^{9}n))+O(n\log n)\cdot O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}
a set
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
of size
O(n\log n)\cdot O(1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}
such that there exists a set
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{S}
,
|\mathcal{C}|=k
, that satisfies

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}}\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)\leq(1+3\varepsilon)\opt_{k,\ell},

where
\opt_{k,\ell}
is the optimal
(k,\ell)
-median cost.

Proof.

We first compute
\delta
such that
\opt_{k,\ell}\leq\delta\leq\alpha\cdot\opt_{k,\ell}
using Algorithm
3
, where
\alpha
is some constant. For each
i\in\{0,\dots,\lceil\log(\alpha n)\rceil\}
, let
r_{i}=\frac{2^{i}\cdot\delta}{\alpha n}
. For each
\pi\in\Pi
, we compute
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r_{i},\varepsilon}
using
Lemma

9.2
. We output
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{\pi\in\Pi}\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lceil\log(\alpha n)\rceil}\mathcal{S}_{\pi,r_{i},\varepsilon}
.
The time needed to compute
\delta
is in
O(2^{O(d\ell)}d\ell^{2}nz(\log(z)\log(z/\ell)+\log^{9}n))

9.4
.
By
Lemma

9.2
, the running time to create
\mathcal{S}
is in
O(n\log n)\cdot O\left((1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}+dz\log z\right)
since we consider
O(\log n)
different parameters
r_{i}
, and its size is in
O(n\log n)\cdot O\left((1/\varepsilon)^{d\ell}\right)
.

To show correctness, consider any polygonal curve
\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{\ast}
is an optimal solution for
(k,\ell)
-median. For any
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
that has
\tau
as its closest center from
\mathcal{C}^{*}
it holds that
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)\leq\opt_{k,\ell}
.
Now let
\pi_{\tau}
be the polygonal curve in
\Pi
which has the smallest discrete Fréchet distance to
\tau
(ties are broken arbitrarily) and let
i^{\ast}
be the smallest value
i
such that

\dist_{dF}(\pi_{\tau},\tau)\leq r_{i}
. By
Lemma

9.2
,
\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{\tau},r_{i^{\ast}},\varepsilon}
contains a polygonal curve
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\ell}^{d}
such that
\dist_{dF}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon r_{i^{\ast}}
.
If
i^{\ast}=0
, then
\dist_{dF}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\leq\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n}
and by the triangle inequality, for any
\pi\in\Pi
,
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau^{\prime})\leq\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n}
.
If
i^{\ast}>0
, then by the triangle inequality, for any
\pi\in\Pi
that has
\tau
as its closest center from
\mathcal{C}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\dist_{dF}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\varepsilon\cdot r_{i^{\ast}}

\displaystyle\leq\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+2\varepsilon\cdot\dist_{dF}(\pi_{\tau},\tau)

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau).

Hence, for any
\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}
there is a
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
such that for any
\pi\in\Pi
that has
\tau
as its closest center,
\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau^{\prime})\leq\max((1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau),\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n})
.
Now, let
\mathcal{C}
be a set containing one such
\tau^{\prime}
for each
\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}
. The cost of this solution is

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}}\max\left((1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau),\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}}\left((1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\ast}}\dist_{dF}(\pi,\tau)+n\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\opt_{k,\ell}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq(1+3\varepsilon)\opt_{k,\ell}.

∎

We can now prove our main result for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem. Note that the discrete Fréchet distance between two curves with ambient dimension
d
of complexities
z
and
\ell
can be computed in
O(dz\ell)
time
[
2
]
.

See
1.5

Proof.

The result follows directly by using the set
\mathcal{S}
computed as in
Lemma

9.5
, as a set of center candidates in the algorithm of
Theorem

7.1
.
∎

In the next subsections, we focus on the case
d=1
and show an alternative approach for solving the problem. For the case
d=1
we develop a novel complexity-reduction technique that may be of independent interest. We give an additional application of this technique in the context of coresets for
(k,\ell)
-median.

9.1
Complexity Reduction for Time Series

In this section, we will establish a complexity-reduction technique for time series. Time series of complexity
z\in\mathbb{N}
are polygonal curves of
\mathbb{X}_{z}^{1}
and can be simply seen as
z
-dimensional real vectors, i.e., members of
\mathbb{R}^{z}
.
In a first step, we will show that one can quantize the entries of a time series from
\mathbb{R}^{z}

to
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
distinct values, while guaranteeing that the distance to any time series of complexity
\ell
is preserved up to a factor of
(1\pm\varepsilon)
.

1

Input:

x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

2

Let
\tilde{x}
be a minimum-error
\ell
-simplification of
x

3

\delta\leftarrow\dist_{dF}(x,\tilde{x})

4

Obtain
x^{\prime}

from
x
by rounding up each entry of
x
to its next multiple of
\varepsilon\delta

5

return

x^{\prime}

6

Algorithm 4

ReduceValueDomain

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\geq\varepsilon>0
. Algorithm
4
computes in time
O(\ell z\log(z)\log(z/\ell))
a time series
x^{\prime}\in X^{z}
with
X\subset\mathbb{R}
and
|X|\in O(\ell/\varepsilon)
s.t. for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,

(1-\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)\leq\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y).

Proof.

The minimum-error
\ell
-simplification
\tilde{x}
of
x
can be computed in
O(\ell z\log(z)\log(z/\ell))
time using an algorithm from

[
6
]

and the distance
\dist_{dF}(x,\tilde{x})
can be computed in
O(\ell z)
time
[
2
]
.
The remaining steps are linear in
z
and hence the running time follows.
Now let
\tilde{x}
be the computed minimum-error
\ell
-simplification of
x
and let
\delta=\dist_{dF}(x,\tilde{x})
.
For
1\leq j\leq\ell
we define
S_{j}=[\tilde{x}_{j}-\delta,\tilde{x}_{j}+(1+\varepsilon)\delta]
. Then it holds for all
1\leq i\leq z
that there is
1\leq j\leq\ell
s.t.
x_{i}\in S_{j}
. It follows that the number of distinct values of
x^{\prime}

is
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
.
By the triangle inequality we have

|\dist_{dF}(x,y)-\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y)|\leq\dist_{dF}(x,x^{\prime})\leq\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon\delta\leq\varepsilon\dist_{dF}(x,y)

for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.
∎

In our complexity reduction we are interested in maintaining
the discrete Fréchet distance of a time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}

to every time series of length at most
\ell
. Thus, we can use the previous lemma to reduce the number of distinct values of every fixed time series to
O(\ell/\varepsilon)
. We will
therefore focus in the remainder of this section on such time series and exploit this property for our dimension reduction.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{z})
, define

\mathrm{set}(x)=\{x_{i}:1\leq i\leq z\}
.

Definition 9.7
.

(traversal sectors)
Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
T
be a traversal between
x
and
y
.
For
j\in[\ell]

we define
S^{(x,T)}_{j}:=\{x_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in[z]\text{ and }(i,j)\in T\}.

We call the sequence
(S^{(x,T)}_{1},\dots,S^{(x,T)}_{\ell})
the traversal sectors of
x
and
T
.

Furthermore,
(S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})
are called traversal sectors of
x
, if there exists a traversal
T
with traversal sectors
(S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})
.
We observe that for a given
traversal
T
we get

\max_{(i,j)\in T}|x_{i}-y_{j}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\max_{a\in S^{(x,T)}_{i}}|a-y_{i}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\max\{|\min(S^{(x,T)}_{i})-y_{i}|,|\max(S^{(x,T)}_{i})-y_{i}|\}.

Thus, to determine the discrete Fréchet distance between time series
x
and
y
it suffices to consider the minimum and maximum value in each traversal sector. This will be used in the following definition.

Definition 9.8

(
\ell
-profile)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
.
For an arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
a traversal
T
between
x
and
y
we call the sequence

\big(\min(S^{(x,T)}_{1}),\max(S^{(x,T)}_{1})\big),\dots,\big(\min(S^{(x,T)}_{\ell}),\max(S^{(x,T)}_{\ell})\big)

the
\ell
-profile of
(x,T)
.

See Figure
2

for an example of traversal sectors and
\ell
-profile.

Figure 2:
Depicted are two time series
x
and
y
and the matched vertices through some traversal
T
. The traversal sectors of
(x,T)
are
S^{(x,T)}_{1}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}\},S^{(x,T)}_{2}=\{x_{6},x_{7},x_{8}\}
and
S^{(x,T)}_{3}=\{x_{9},x_{10},x_{11}\}
. The orange highlighted vertices are the extrema values located in each traversal sector, which define the profile, i.e. the sequence
((x_{2},x_{4}),(x_{6},x_{8}),(x_{10},x_{11}))
is the
3
-profile of
(x,T)
.

Let
D_{(x,\ell)}
denote the set of all
\ell
-profiles of a time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}

over all traversals in
\mathcal{T}_{z,\ell}
.

Since the information stored in an
\ell
-profile of some time series
x
and a traversal
T
preserves
\max_{(i,j)\in T}|x_{i}-y_{j}|
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
we can argue that two time series
x
and
x^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{set}(x)=\mathrm{set}(x^{\prime})

that have the same set of
\ell
-profiles are interchangeable w.r.t. the discrete Fréchet distance. This is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
z,z^{\prime},\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}}
be two time series with the same set of
\ell
-profiles, i.e.

D_{(x,\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}
.
Then for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
we have
\dist_{dF}(x,y)=\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y).

Proof.

Since
D_{(x,\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}
, for any traversal
T\in\mathcal{T}_{(z,\ell)}
there is a traversal
T^{\prime}=\in\mathcal{T}_{(z^{\prime},\ell)}
s.t. for all
i\in[\ell]
,
\max(S^{(x,T)}_{i})=\max(S^{(x^{\prime},T^{\prime})}_{i})
and
\min(S_{i}^{(x,T)})=\min(S^{(x^{\prime},T^{\prime})}_{i})
and vice versa.

Consider some arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and let
T\in\mathcal{T}_{z,\ell)}
be s.t.
\max_{(i,j)\in T}|x_{i}-y_{j}|=\dist_{dF}(x,y)
.
Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{(z^{\prime},\ell)}
s.t.
(x^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has the same
\ell
-profile as
(x,T)
.

Then we get

\displaystyle\dist_{dF}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\min_{(i,j)\in T}|x_{i}-y_{j}|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[\ell]}\max\{|\min(S^{(x,T)}_{i})-y_{i}|,|\max(S^{(x,T)}_{i})-y_{i}|\}

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[\ell]}\max\{|\min(S^{(x^{\prime},T^{\prime})}_{i})-y_{i}|,|\max(S^{(x^{\prime},T^{\prime})}_{i})-y_{i}|\}

\displaystyle=\max_{(i,j)\in T^{\prime}}|x^{\prime}_{i}-y_{j}|

\displaystyle\geq\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y).

The other direction holds by a symmetric argument, which concludes the proof.
∎

In the following we show how to construct for any time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
a time series
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}}
s.t.
D_{(x,\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}
and
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2\ell+2}})
. To do so we introduce the concept of
prefix profiles
. For some time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
t\in[z]
we write
x(t):=(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})^{T}
to denote the prefix of
x
of complexity
t
.

Definition 9.10

(prefix profile)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
t\in[z]
. For arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and some
traversal
T
between
x
and
y
let
r\in[\ell]
be the largest value s.t.
S_{r}^{(x(t),T)}\neq\emptyset
. Then we call the sequence

\big(\min(S^{(x(t),T)}_{1}),\max(S^{(x(t),T)}_{1})\big),\dots,\big(\min(S^{(x(t),T)}_{r}),\max(S^{(x(t),T)}_{r})\big)

the
prefix profile
of
(x(t),T)
with length
r
.

Observe that for a time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
there are
O(|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2r})
many possible prefix profiles with length
r
. This implies that the number of distinct prefix profiles over all lengths
r\in[\ell]
is

\sum^{\ell}_{r=1}O(|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2r})\in O(|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2\ell+2}).

Now let
D_{x(t)}
be the set of all prefix profiles for
(x(t),T)
over all traversals
T\in\mathcal{T}_{z,\ell}
and let
D_{x}=\bigcup_{t=1}^{z}D_{x(t)}
. Then there is a unique function
\delta_{x}:2^{D_{x}}\times\mathrm{set}(x)\rightarrow 2^{D_{x}}
that defines how a set of prefix profiles can evolve to a potentially different set of prefix profiles by including a further vertex of
x
. To see this, consider some arbitrary prefix profile of length
r
for
x(t)
and a traversal
T
. Then either
(t+1,r)\in T
and
x_{t+1}\in S_{r}^{(x(t+1),T)}
or
(t+1,r+1)\in T
and
S^{(x(t+1),T)}_{r+1}=\{x_{t+1}\}
.
Both possibilities correspond to different traversals in
\mathcal{T}_{m,\ell}
and therefore potentially different prefix profiles in
D_{x}
.

We are now ready to show the following lemma.

Lemma 9.11
.

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a time series
x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{set}(x)^{z^{\prime}}
with
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{(2\ell+2)}})
s.t.

D_{(x,\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}
.

Proof.

Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{set}(x)^{z^{\prime}}
be the time series with smallest complexity
z^{\prime}
s.t.
D_{(x,\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}
and observe that the number of subsets of the prefix profiles of
x^{\prime}
is bounded by
|2^{D_{x^{\prime}}}|\in O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{(2\ell+2)}}
). Assume there exist
1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq z^{\prime}
with
D_{x^{\prime}(t_{1})}=D_{x^{\prime}(t_{2})}
, then it holds that
\delta_{x^{\prime}}(D_{x^{\prime}(t_{1})},x^{\prime}_{t_{2}+1})=\delta_{x^{\prime}}(D_{x^{\prime}(t_{2})},x^{\prime}_{t_{2}+1})
. Recursively applying
\delta_{x^{\prime}}
with the remaining vertices of
x^{\prime}
implies that the time series
x^{\prime\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{1},\dots x^{\prime}_{t_{1}},x^{\prime}_{t_{2}+1},\dots x^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}})^{T}
has the same set of
\ell
-profiles
as
x^{\prime}
and therefore
x
, i.e.
D_{(x^{\prime\prime},\ell)}=D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}=D_{(x,\ell)}
. This is a contradiction to the assumption that
x^{\prime}
is the time series of smallest complexity with
D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}=D_{(x,\ell)}
. Therefore for all
1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq z^{\prime}
it holds that
D_{x^{\prime}(t_{1})}\neq D_{x^{\prime}(t_{2})}
and since the number of distinct prefix profiles of
x^{\prime}
is
O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2\ell+\ell}})
we get that
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{2\ell+\ell}})
.

∎

The following corollary results from combining Lemma
9.6
and Lemma
9.11
.

Corollary 9.12
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N},z\in\mathbb{N}
and every time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
there exists a
time series
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}}
with
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})
such that for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
we have

(1-\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)\leq\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y).

9.1.1
Algorithm

In this section we turn the previously discussed existential result into an algorithmic one.

Given some time series
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
the solution will be to iterate over all increasing complexities
t
and all time series
in
\mathrm{set}(x)^{t}
until we find one that has the same set of
\ell
-profiles as
x
.
Our previous results guarantee that this algorithm terminates with

t\in O(2^{|\mathrm{set}(x)|^{(2\ell+2)}})
. By Lemma
9.11
we have that
\dist_{dF}(x,q)=\dist_{dF}(y,q)
, for all
q\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.
The challenge is to efficiently compute the set of
\ell
-profiles for a given time series, which we will discuss in the remaining part of the section.

To compute the set
D_{(x,\ell)}
, for some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
, we iterate over all sequences
p\in({\mathrm{set}(x)^{2}})^{\ell}
of potential
\ell
-profiles and decide if there exists a traversal
T
s.t.
p
is an
\ell
-profile of
(x,T)
. To do so it is sufficient to decide the existence of the corresponding traversal sectors
S^{(x,T)}_{1},\dots,S^{(x,T)}_{\ell}
.

Consider a sequence
p=((\min_{1},\max_{1}),\dots,(\min_{\ell},\max_{\ell}))\in({\mathrm{set}(x)^{2}})^{\ell}
. Note that
\min_{i}
and
\max_{i}
are supposed to be the minimum and maximum element according to the considered
\ell
-profile. The objective is to decide if there exist traversal sectors

S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}
of
x
that are consistent with
p
, i.e. for all
i\in[\ell]
,
\min_{i},\max_{i}\in S_{i}
and
S_{i}\subseteq[\min_{i},\max_{i}]
. This is done in a recursive way and stated as dynamic program in the form of Algorithm
5
.

The idea is as follows. For some
t\in[z]
and
h\in[\ell]
we would like to know if there are traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
that are compatible with
p
.
However, in order to set up a recursion, we also need to know whether the minimum and/or maximum value of
S_{h}

has already appeared in
x_{1},\dots,x_{t}
.
For this purpose, we introduce two Boolean variables
a
and
b
.

Concretely, Algorithm
5
is a dynamic programm that checks for the existence of partial solutions of the following form.

Definition 9.13

(compatible traversal sectors)
.

Given a time series
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
, sequence
p=\big((\min_{1},\max_{1}),\dots,(\min_{\ell},\max_{\ell})\big)\in(\mathrm{set}(x)^{2})^{\ell}
,
h\in[\ell]
,
t\in[z]
.
Traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
are compatible with
p
if they satisfy:

1.

\min_{i},\max_{i}\in S_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq h-1

2.

S_{i}\subseteq[\min_{i},\max_{i}]
for
1\leq i\leq h

Note that the last sector
S_{h}
is not required to fulfill the conditions of the profile, in that we don’t require
\min_{h}
and
\max_{h}
to be contained in
S_{h}
. This is needed as they may not have appeared in the sequence at that time of the algorithm, yet.
Lemma
9.15
below shows correctness of Algorithm
5
. The crucial observation is that partial solutions can be combined as follows.

Observation 9.14
.

For any
h\in[\ell],t\in[z]
, let
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
be compatible traversal sectors of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
with
\min_{h}\in S_{h}
and let
S^{\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime}_{h}
be compatible traversal sectors of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
with
\max_{h}\in S^{\prime}_{h}
, then either
S_{h}\subseteq S^{\prime}_{h}
and therefore also
\min_{h}\in S^{\prime}_{h}
, or
S^{\prime}_{h}\subseteq S_{h}
and therefore also
\max_{h}\in S_{h}
. This follows because the last sector in each case consist of the elements of a prefix of the same sequence.

Input:

(x_{1},\dots,x_{z})
,
((\min_{1},\max_{1}),\dots,(\min_{\ell},\max_{\ell}))

1

\feas\leftarrow\{\text{False}\}^{(\ell+1)\times(z+1)}
,
\hmin\leftarrow\{\text{False}\}^{(\ell+1)\times(z+1)}
,
\hmax\leftarrow\{\text{False}\}^{(\ell+1)\times(z+1)}

2

\feas[0,0]\leftarrow\text{True}
,
\hmin[0,0]\leftarrow\text{True}
,
\hmax[0,0]\leftarrow\text{True}

3

for

h=1\textbf{ to }\ell

do

4

for

t=1\textbf{ to }z

do

5

if

x_{t}\in[\min_{h},\max_{h}]

then

6

if

\feas[h,t-1]

then

7

\feas[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

8

if

\hmin[h,t-1]\lor x_{t}=\min_{h}

then

9

\hmin[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

if

\hmax[h,t-1]\lor x_{t}=\max_{h}

then

10

\hmax[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

else

11

if

\feas[h-1,t-1]\land\hmin[h-1,t-1]\land\hmax[h-1,t-1]

then

12

\feas[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

13

if

x_{t}=\min_{h}

then

14

\hmin[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

if

x_{t}=\max_{h}

then

15

\hmax[h,t]\leftarrow\text{True}

return

\feas[\ell,z]\land\hmin[\ell,z]\land\hmax[\ell,z]

16

Algorithm 5

DecideProfile

Lemma 9.15
.

Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
p\in{(\mathrm{set}(x)^{2})}^{\ell}
.
Let
\feas,\hmin
and
\hmax
be the tables constructed during Algorithm
5
with input
(x,p)
.
For every
h\in[\ell],t\in[z]
, after the corresponding iteration of the for-loop, we have
\feas[h,t]=True

if and only if there exist
compatible traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
. In addition, we have that
\hmin[h,t]=True
(resp.
\hmax[h,t]=True
) if and only if there exists such a compatible solution with
\min_{h}\in S_{h}
(resp.
\max_{h}\in S_{h}
).

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on the iterations of the inner for-loop. For the base case consider the first iteration with
h=1
and
t=1
. Assume
x_{1}\in[\min_{1},\max_{1}]
. In this case, the clause in Line
5
evaluates to True, but the clause in Line
5
evaluates to False. However, the clause in Line
5
evaluates to True, because these Booleans were set in Line
5
to True to initialize the algorithm. In this case,
\feas[1,1]
is set to True, which is correct since
S_{1}=\{x_{1}\}
is a compatible traversal sector. Furthermore,
\hmin[1,1]
and
\hmax[1,1]
are set correctly. Otherwise, if
x_{1}\notin[\min_{1},\max_{1}]
, then there exists no compatible traversal sectors and
\feas[1,1],\hmin[1,1],
as well as
\hmax[1,1]
remain set to False.

For the induction step consider any
h,t\geq 1
with
h>1
or
t>1
. Assume
x_{t}\in[\min_{h},\max_{h}]
.
If
\feas[h,t-1]
is True, then by induction there exist compatible traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t-1})
. Therefore there exist compatible traversal sectors of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
by adding
x_{t}
to
S_{h}
. Furthermore,
\hmin[h,t]
and
\hmax[h,t]
are set correctly.

Otherwise, if
\feas[h,t-1]
is False, then we check in Line
5
, if

\hmin[h-1,t-1]
and
\hmax[h-1,t-1]
are True. By induction and Observation
9.14
this is the case if and only if there exist compatible traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h-1}
for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t-1})
and it holds that
\min_{h-1}\in S_{h-1}
as well as
\max_{h-1}\in S_{h-1}
.
As such, there exist compatible traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{h}
with
S_{h}=\{x_{t}\}
for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
. Furthermore,
\hmin[h,t]
and
\hmax[h,t]
are set correctly with respect to
S_{h}
.

Now, assume
x_{t}\notin[\min_{h},\max_{h}]
. In this case, there exists no compatible traversal sectors and
\feas[h,t],\hmin[h,t],
as well as
\hmax[h,t]
remain set to False.
∎

Lemma 9.16
.

Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
and
p=\big((min_{1},max_{1}),\dots,(min_{\ell},max_{\ell})\big)\in{(\mathrm{set}(x)^{2})}^{\ell}
. Then Algorithm
5
with input
(x,p)
takes
O(z\ell)
time and returns True iff there exists a traversal
T
s.t.
p
is an
\ell
-profile for
(x,T)
.

Proof.

Let
\feas,\hmin
and
\hmax
be the tables constructed by Algorithm
5
and assume
the algorithm returns True. Then, it must be that
\hmin[\ell,z]
and
\hmax[\ell,z]
, as well as
\feas[\ell,z]
are all set to True.
By Lemma
9.15
and Observation
9.14
, there exist traversal sectors
S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}
of
x
that are compatible with
p
and it holds that
\min_{\ell}\in S_{\ell}
and
\min_{\ell}\in S_{\ell}
. By definition there exists a traversal
T
s.t.
S^{(x,T)}_{i}=S_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq\ell
implying that
p
is an
\ell
-profile of
(x,T)
.

Next assume that there exists a traversal
T
s.t.
p
is an
\ell
-profile for
(x,T)
. Then
S^{(x,T)}_{1},\dots,S^{(x,T)}_{\ell}
are traversal sectors for
x
that are compatible with
p
and it holds that
\min_{\ell}\in S^{(x,T)}
and
\max_{\ell}\in S^{(x,T)}
. By Lemma
9.15
,

\hmin[\ell,z]
and
\hmax[\ell,z]
, as well as
\feas[\ell,z]
are correctly set to True.

The initialization of
\feas
takes
O(\ell z)
time and the algorithm takes
O(\ell z)
many iterations of the inner for-loop, which require constant time each.
∎

After establishing how to compute the set of
\ell
-profiles we are ready to state the complete procedure, which is given by Algorithm
6
and analyzed in Theorem
1.7
.

Input:

x\in\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

1

\tilde{x}\leftarrow\text{ReduceValueDomain}(x,\ell,\varepsilon)

2

Let
z^{\prime}
be smallest value s.t. it exists
x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})^{z^{\prime}}
with
D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}=D_{(\tilde{x},\ell)}

3

return

x^{\prime}

Algorithm 6

ComplexityReduction

See
1.7

Proof.

Let
\tilde{x}
be the time series returned by ReduceValueDomain
(x,\ell,\varepsilon)
. Then by Lemma
9.6
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

(1-\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)\leq\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x},y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)

with
|\mathrm{set}(x^{\prime})|\in O(\ell/\varepsilon)
.
By Lemma
9.11
there exists a time series
x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})^{z^{\prime}}
, with
D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}=D_{(\tilde{x},\ell)}

which further implies by Lemma
9.9
that for arbitrary
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
we have
\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y)=\dist_{dF}(\tilde{x},y)
. It follows that

(1-\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y)\leq\dist_{dF}(x^{\prime},y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\dist_{dF}(x,y).

By
Lemma

9.6
, the time needed to compute
\tilde{x}
is in
O(\ell z\log^{2}z)
.
Then, by
Lemma

9.16
, computing
D_{(\tilde{x},\ell)}
takes
O(\ell/{\varepsilon})^{2\ell}\cdot z
time by enumerating all
\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})^{2\ell}
candidate
\ell
-profiles and checking if they are valid
\ell
-profiles for
\tilde{x}
using
Algorithm

5
.
To find the time series
x^{\prime}
with smallest complexity with its vertices in
\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})
such that
D_{(x^{\prime},\ell)}=D_{(\tilde{x},\ell)}
, we enumerate all vectors over

\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})
in increasing length until we find a vector with the same set of
\ell
-profiles as
\tilde{x}
. For each increasing value of
i=1,2,\ldots,z^{\prime}
, for each vector
y\in\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})^{i}
we compute its set of
\ell
-profiles, by enumerating all
\mathrm{set}(\tilde{x})^{2\ell}
candidate
\ell
-profiles and checking if they are valid
\ell
-profiles for
x^{\prime}
using
Algorithm

5
. By definition, we have
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})
.
The overall running time of this step is in
O({z^{\prime}}^{2})\cdot O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{z^{\prime}}\cdot O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{2\ell}\subseteq O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{2z^{\prime}}
.
∎

9.2
Near Linear Time Approximation Scheme for
(k,\ell)
-Median with
d=1

First, we reduce the complexity
z
of the input time series to a complexity that only depends on
\ell,\varepsilon
using
Algorithm

6
, to obtain a set of clients
X
with constant complexity. Then,
we compute a set of candidate centers
Y
using
Lemma

9.5
, which has a size near linear in
n
, and independent of
z
.

Strictly speaking, the discrete Fréchet distance is a pseudo-metric as distance between distinct curves can be
0
. Therefore, one has to consider a metric space defined on the equivalence classes of curves with pairwise distance
0
. Each equivalence class has a corresponding representative time series and, for some given time series
x
its representative can be computed by removing all duplicates of neighboring values in
x
.

Input:

P\subset\mathbb{R}^{z}
,
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

1

X\leftarrow
for each
x\in P
, run
Algorithm

6
with input
x,\ell,\varepsilon/16
and store output in
X

2

Y\leftarrow
output of the algorithm of
Lemma

9.5
with input
X,k,\ell
and
\varepsilon/12
.

3

Sol\leftarrow
output of the algorithm of
Theorem

7.1
with input
X,Y,k,\varepsilon/4

4

return

Sol

5

Algorithm 7

NLTAS

Theorem 9.17
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be constants. Given a set
P
of
n
real-valued time series of complexity
z
, and parameter
k
Algorithm
7
computes in time

\tilde{O}(nz)

and with success probability at least
1-\varepsilon
a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to the
(k,\ell)
-median problem under discrete Fréchet distance.

Proof.

By
Theorem

1.7
,
Algorithm

6
runs in
O(z\ell\log^{2}z)+O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{2z^{\prime}}
time and outputs
a time series
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}}
, where
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})
. Hence, computing
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{z^{\prime}}
costs

O(nz\ell\log^{2}z)+n\cdot O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{2z^{\prime}}

time.
The algorithm of
Lemma

9.5
runs in time
O(2^{O(\ell)}\ell^{2}nz^{\prime}(\log(z^{\prime})\log(z^{\prime}/\ell)+\log^{9}n))+O(n\log n)\cdot O(1/\varepsilon)^{\ell}
and outputs a set
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
of size
