O(n\log n)\cdot O(1/\varepsilon)^{\ell}
.
Finally, we run the algorithm of
Theorem

7.1
on
X,Y
, where
|X\cup Y|\in O(n\log n)\cdot O(1/\varepsilon)^{\ell}
.
By
Proposition

9.1
, the doubling dimension of the ambient space of
Y
is in
O(\ell)
. By
Theorem

7.1
and assuming constant time distance evaluations, the running time of this step is

2^{2^{t}}\cdot\tilde{O}(n)
, where
t=O\left(\ell\log\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon}\right).

Since
\varepsilon,\ell
and
z^{\prime}
are constants, distances can be computed in constant time and furthermore we can simplify the running time to
\tilde{O}(n).

To show correctness, first observe that by
Theorem

1.7
, the cost of any solution for
P
is preserved up to a factor of
(1\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{16})
after reducing the complexity of each
x\in P
resulting in
X
. The optimal solution for
X
corresponds to a solution for
P
which is at most
\frac{1+\varepsilon/16}{1-\varepsilon/16}\leq 1+\varepsilon/4
from the optimal.
Now by
Lemma

9.5
, there will be a solution consisting of medians from
Y
which has cost at most
(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4})
times that of the optimal solution for
X
. Therefore, the execution of the algorithm of
Theorem

7.1
on
X,Y
will find a solution consisting of
k
time series from
Y
that has a cost of at most
(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{2}
times that of the optimal solution for
X
. This solution has a cost of at most
(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{3}\leq 1+\varepsilon
times that of the optimal solution for
P
.
∎

9.3
Coreset for
(k,\ell)
-Median with
d=1

In this section, we discuss an additional implication of our complexity reduction result in the context of constructing coresets for
(k,\ell)
-median. Given a set
P
of
n
time series, an
\varepsilon
-coreset of
P
for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem, is a weighted set
S\subseteq P
such that for any
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
|C|=k
,

(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{x\in P}\min_{c\in C}\dist_{dF}(x,c)\leq\sum_{x\in S}w_{x}\cdot\min_{c\in C}\dist_{dF}(x,c)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{x\in P}\min_{c\in C}d_{dF}(x,c),

where
w_{x}
is the weight associated with
x
.

A recent result of Cohen-Addad et al.
[
17
, Corollary 7.2]
implies coresets for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem under the discrete Fréchet distance of size
\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}k\ell\log(z))
. By combining this result with
Theorem

1.7
, we obtain coresets of size
\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}k\ell\log(z^{\prime}))
, i.e., completely independent of the size of the input since
z^{\prime}
is a function of
\ell,\varepsilon
. The result is formally stated as follows.

Corollary 9.18
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. For any set
P
of time series there exists an
\varepsilon
-coreset for the
(k,\ell)
-median problem of size
\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}k\ell\log(z^{\prime}))
, where
z^{\prime}\in O(2^{O(\ell/\varepsilon)^{(2\ell+2)}})
.

Appendix A
Constant Approximation Algorithms

It has been shown in
[
18
]
that one can obtain near linear time algorithms to compute a constant approximation to the
k
-median problem as well as the facility location problem when the input points come from a doubling space. In detail, the running time for the
k
-median problem is
O(2^{O(\ddim)}n\log^{9}n)
(where
n=|X\cup Y|)
and combines the spanner construction (with parameter
\varepsilon=1/2
from
[
31
]
) with a graph-based algorithm by Thorup
[
45
]
.
For the facility location problem one can obtain a
O(n\log n)
time
2^{O(\ddim)}
-approximation algorithm, where
n=|X\cup Y|
is the size of the instance
[
18
, Section A.3]
.

We would like to extend their result to our setting when only one of the two sets has bounded doubling dimension. Assume first that this is the set
Y
of candidate centers. In this case, we first compute for every
x\in X
its
2
-approximate nearest neighbor in
Y
using the algorithm in
Lemma

2.7
which runs in time
O(2^{O(\ddim)}\cdot n\log\Delta)
. Then we replace every client by its approximate nearest neighbor and solve the resulting problem using one of the algorithms described above. We claim that the resulting solution is also a constant factor approximation for the original problem.

Indeed, moving a client by a distance
D
while maintaining the assignment to the same cluster center/facility will change the objective function by a value of at most
D
. Since the distance of
x\in X

to its closest candidate facility is a lower bound on its connection cost, this implies that our construction changes the cost of any fixed solution by at most
2\opt
. Thus, the cost of the optimal solution will become at most
3\opt
and the solution of a
c
-approximation algorithm
on the new instance will have cost at most
3c\opt
. Going back to the original data set changes the cost of this solution by at most
2\opt
, so it is a
(3c+2)
-approximation.
The above argumentation holds for both the
k
-median and the facility location problem.

It remains to deal with the case that
X
has bounded doubling dimension and
Y
not.
In this case, we define the following metric embedding as in
[
30
]
.
For
y\in Y
let
\bar{y}
denote a
2
-approximate nearest neighbor of
y
in
X
, otherwise if
y\in X
define
\bar{y}=y
.
We define a new metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime})
where

\dist^{\prime}(x,y)=|\dist(x,\bar{x})-\dist(y,\bar{y})|+\dist(\bar{x},\bar{y})
for
x,y\in X\cup Y
. Then, by Lemma 2.4 in
[
30
]
and the fact that
\dist(y,\bar{y})\leq 2\dist(y,X)
for all
y\in X\cup Y
we get the following Lemma.

Lemma A.1
.

For the metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime})
the following holds.

1.

For
x,y\in X
,
\dist^{\prime}(x,y)=\dist(x,y)

2.

For
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
\dist(x,y)\leq\dist^{\prime}(x,y)\leq 6\dist(x,y)

3.

The doubling dimension is
O(\ddim(X))

Computing
\bar{y}
for some
y\in Y
can be done in
O(2^{O(\ddim(X))}n\log\Delta)
time using the data structure of Lemma
2.7
.

We have designed an embedding of a metric space into a doubling metric in such a way that pairwise distances between
X
and
Y
and within
X
are maintained upto a constant factor. Since only these distances appear in the objective function, we obtain that any constant factor solution w.r.t.
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime})

is also a constant factor approximation in
(X\cup Y,\dist)
.
Since we can evaluate
\dist^{\prime}(p,q)
for arbitrary
p,q\in X\cup Y
in constant time per query (assuming constant time access to
\dist(p,q)
) we can use the previously known algorithms and get the same running times.

Appendix B
Removing Dependency on Aspect Ratio

In the following we will show how to transform our input instance in near linear time to some instance that has aspect ratio poly-logarithmic in
n
and
m
, while only incurring a factor
(1+\varepsilon)
on the pairwise distances. Our construction is independent of the case wether
X
or
Y
is doubling. We will formulate it with respect to
X
being doubling but the construction for the other case is symmetric.

We start by stating a data-structure to compute an
O(n)
-ANN in
X
for a point
y
that has construction and query time independent of the aspect ratio. We will further utilize this to compute a
O(n)
-approximation for the facility location or
k
-median problem, which will be useful for our instance transformation.

We show that the data-structure of
[
31
, Lemma 4.2]
works for partially doubling metrics.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(X\cup Y,\dist)
be a metric space with
|X|=n
and
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
. Then there is an algorithm that builds in
2^{O(\ddim)}O(n\log n)
time a data-structure, that given a query point
y\in Y
, returns a
2n
-ANN of
y\in X
. The query time is
2^{O(\ddim)}O(\log n)
.

The following proof is identical to that of Lemma 4.2 from
[
31
]
. We give our own formulation to keep the paper self-contained.

Proof.

By applying the data-structure of Lemma 4.2 in
[
31
]
on
X
we get a binary search tree
S
, in which each vertex of the tree
v
is associated with a point
x_{v}\in X
and some radius
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Each vertex
v
in
S
satisfies that

\frac{n}{2\ddim^{3}}\leq|B_{X}(x_{v},r_{v})|\leq(1-\frac{1}{2\ddim^{3}})n

and
B_{X}(x_{v},(1+\frac{1}{2n}r_{v}))\setminus B_{X}(x_{v},(1-\frac{1}{2n}r_{v}))=\emptyset
. The left subtree of
v
is associated with points in
B_{X}(x_{v},r_{v})
and the right subtree with points in
X\setminus B_{X}(x_{v},r_{v})
. The depth of
S
is
O(\ddim^{3}\log n)
.

Given some
y\in Y
we can find a
2n
-ANN in
X
in time
O(\ddim^{3}\log n)
as follows. Let
u
be the root of
S
, then if
\dist(y,x_{u})\leq r_{u}
recurse of the left subtree and otherwise on the right. Return the nearest point to
y
among all
x_{v}
, where
v
is a vertex in
S
that was traversed this way.

Let
y^{*}
be the nearest neighbor of
y
in
X
and let
y^{\prime}
be the output of the described procedure. Let
w
be the lowest common ancestor of the vertices in
S
that correspond to
y
and
y^{\prime}
. Assume that
y^{\prime}
is contained in the left subtree and
y^{*}
in the right subtree of
w
. Then, by the properties of the tree
\dist(y,x_{w})\leq r_{w}
and
\dist(y,y^{*})\geq r_{w}/2n
implying that
x_{w}
is already a
2n
-ANN.
Now consider the other case where
y^{*}
is contained in the left subtree and
y^{\prime}
in the right subtree of
w
. Then
\dist(y,y^{\prime})\leq\dist(y,x_{w})
and by the property of the tree it holds
\dist(y,y^{*})\geq r_{w}/2n+(\dist(x_{w},y)-r_{w})
and the ratio of these two terms is at most
2n
and
x_{w}
is therefore a
2n
-ANN of
y
in
X
.
∎

Now let
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
be the metric spaced introduced in section
A
with the exception that
\bar{x}
is an
O(n)
-ANN for
x\in Y
.
Then Lemma
A.1
implies the following Corollary.

Corollary B.2
.

For the metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
the following holds.

1.

For
x,y\in X
,
\dist^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\dist(x,y)

2.

For
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
\dist(x,y)\leq\dist^{\prime\prime}(x,y)\leq O(n)\dist(x,y)

3.

The doubling dimension is
O(\ddim(X))

The embedding can be computed in
O(2^{O(\ddim(X))}n\log n)
time using the data structure of Lemma
B.1
.
One can now combine the embedding to metric
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
and constant factor algorithms for
k
-median or facility location problem
[
18
]
to get an
O(n)
-approximation for the respective clustering problem in near linear time.

Next we give the transformation to an input instance that has bounded aspect ratio. The idea is to first apply the metric embedding of Lemma
B.2
to
(X\cup Y,\dist)
to get the metric space
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
.
and then apply the transformation given by Lemma 32 in
[
18
]
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
P
denote the facility location problem or the
k
-Median problem.
Given
(X\cup Y,\dist)
with
\ddim(X)\leq\ddim
and
|X\cup Y|=n
,
\varepsilon>0
. Then one can compute in time
\tilde{O}(n+m)
a set of instances
(X_{1}\cup Y_{1},\dist_{1}),\dots,(X_{\ell}\cup Y_{\ell},\dist_{\ell})
s.t.

1.

X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}X_{i}
and
Y=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}Y_{i}

2.

For
i\in[\ell]
, the instance
(X_{i}\cup Y_{i},\dist_{i})
has aspect ratio
O(n^{6}/\varepsilon)

3.

Let
(X\cup Y,\bar{\dist})
be the metric where
\bar{\dist}(x,y)=\dist_{i}(x,y)
for
x,y\in X_{i}\cup Y_{i}
and
\bar{\dist}=\infty
otherwise. Let
\opt
be the optimal value for problem
P
. Then it holds

•

There exists a solution on
(X\cup Y,\bar{\dist})
with cost
(1+\varepsilon/n)\opt

•

Every solution on
(X\cup Y,\bar{\dist})
for problem
P
with cost
A
induces a solution with cost at most
A+\varepsilon\opt/n
for
(X\cup Y,\dist)

Proof.

We first compute the metric embedding
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
, which preserves all distances
\dist(x,y)
with
x\in X
and
y\in X\cup Y
within factor
O(n)
.
We will further equip the embedding
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
with the following mechanism. Let
\gamma
be the cost of an constant approximation for a problem
P
.
Whenever we evaluate a distance
\dist^{\prime\prime}(x,y)<\varepsilon\gamma/n^{3}
we set the distance
\dist^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\varepsilon\gamma/n^{3}
. By doing so we ensure that the smallest distance that is queried on
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
is at least
\varepsilon\gamma/n^{3}
. Note that this satisfies the triangle inequality. Doing so induces an additive error of
O(\varepsilon\opt/n^{3})
.
We then apply the construction given in
[
18
]
on
(X\cup Y,\dist^{\prime\prime})
and partition
(X\cup Y,\dist)
accordingly. By using
\dist^{\prime\prime}
we incur an additional factor
O(n)
to the aspect ratio.
∎

The following Lemma corresponds to Lemma 33 in
[
18
]
and the proof is analogous. It will allow us to trade the logarithmic dependency on
\Delta
for additional factors that are only poly-logarithmic in the size of the input instance.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
P
denote the facility location problem or the
k
-Median problem. Given a set of instances
(X\cup Y,\dist)
and
(X_{1}\cup Y_{1},\dist_{1}),\dots,(X_{\ell}\cup Y_{\ell},\dist_{\ell})
as in Lemma
B.3
and an algorithm with running time
O(n_{i}(\log n_{i})^{c}f(\Delta))
to solve
P
on instances with
n_{i}
points and aspect ratio
\Delta
. Then there exists an algorithm that has running time
O(n(\log n)^{c+2}f(O(n^{6}/\varepsilon)))
to solve
P
on
(X\cup Y,\dist)
.

Appendix C
Missing Proofs in
Section

7

C.1
Proof of
Lemma

7.4

See
7.4

Proof of
Lemma

7.4
.

We first state how to construct
F
.
Let
F^{*}\subseteq Y
be the optimal
k
-median solution.
Wlog, assume
|F^{*}|=|S|=k
, since otherwise we can add arbitrary facilities to them.
For
f\in F^{*}
, recall that
\pi_{S}(f)
is the point in
S
closest to
f
.
For
s\in S
, denote
S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)=\{f\in F^{*}\colon\pi_{S}(f)=s\}
.
Denote
S_{0}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|=0\}
,

S_{1}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|=1\}

and
S_{\geq 2}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|\geq 2\}
.
Denote
S_{\geq 1}:=S_{1}\cup S_{\geq 2}
.
For
s\in S_{\geq 1}
, denote
f_{s}:=\argmin_{f\in S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)}\dist(f,s)
, i.e.,
f_{s}
is the closest point to
s
among
S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)
, breaking ties arbitrarily.
Denote
F_{S}:=\{f\in F^{*}\colon\exists s\in S,\text{ s.t. }f=f_{s}\}
.

Following
[
18
]
, the center set
F
is constructed as follows:

•

Step 1:
Among the centers in
F^{*}\setminus F_{S}
, remove from
F^{*}
a subset of size
1000c\varepsilon|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|
that yields the minimum cost increase after removal, where
c
is the same constant in
Lemma

5.3
.
4
4

4

This step requires us to assume
\varepsilon\leq 1/(1000c)
. Since
1000c
is a constant, there is no loss of generality to make such assumption.

The resulting center set is denoted as
\overline{F}^{*}
.
Let
F=\overline{F}^{*}
.

•

Step 2:
For
s\in S_{0}
, if
s
is a bad center, then add
s
to
F
.

•

Step 3:
For

, if
s
is a bad center, then add
s
to
F
, and remove
f_{s}
from
F
.

Formally,
F:=\overline{F}^{*}\setminus\{f_{s}\colon s\in\Bad_{S}\cap S_{\geq 1}\}\cup\Bad_{S}
.
We note that
F
contains all bad facilities in
S
.
This proves property (a).

Property (b).

We first show that
\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})\leq 3\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(x,S)
.
For
x\in X\cup Y
, if
\pi_{F^{*}}(x)
is not removed, then
\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})=\dist(x,F^{*})
.
If
\pi_{F^{*}}(x)
is removed, then
\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\notin F_{S}
.
Denote
s=\pi_{S}(\pi_{F^{*}}(x))
.
Recall that
f_{s}
is the closest point to
s
in
S_{F^{*}}^{-1}(s)
, and
f_{s}\in\overline{F}^{*}
.
We have

\displaystyle\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,f_{s})

Since
f_{s}\in\overline{F}^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,\pi_{F^{*}}(x))+\dist(\pi_{F^{*}}(x),s)+\dist(s,f_{s})

By triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(\pi_{F^{*}}(x),s)

By definition of
f_{s}

\displaystyle=\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(\pi_{F^{*}}(x),S)

By definition of
s

\displaystyle\leq 3\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(x,S)

\displaystyle\text{By triangle inequality}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})\leq\begin{cases}\dist(x,F^{*}),&\text{if }\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\in\overline{F}^{*};\\
3\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(x,S).&\text{if }\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\notin\overline{F}^{*}.\end{cases}

(47)

Next, we bound
\dist(x,F)
.
For
x\in X\cup Y
, if
\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)\in F
, then
\dist(x,F)\leq\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})
.
If
\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)\notin F
, then there exists
s\in\Bad_{S}\cap S_{\geq 1}
, such that
\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)=f_{s}
.
Moreover,
s\in F
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\dist(x,F)\leq\dist(x,s)\leq\dist(x,f_{s})+\dist(f_{s},s)=\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})+\dist(\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x),S)\leq 2\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})+\dist(x,S).

Hence,

\displaystyle\dist(x,F)\leq\begin{cases}\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*}),&\text{if }\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)\in F;\\
2\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})+\dist(x,S),&\text{if }\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)\notin F.\end{cases}

(48)

Combining (
48
) with (
47
), we have
\dist(x,F)\leq 6\dist(x,F^{*})+5\dist(x,S)
.

Property (c): size of
F
.

By step 2, every bad center in
S_{0}
increases the size of
F
by
1
.
By step 3, bad centers in
S_{\geq 1}
do not increase the size of
F
.
Therefore,

|F|=|F^{*}|-1000c\varepsilon|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|+|\Bad_{S}\cap S_{0}|.

By
Lemma

5.3
,

\displaystyle\E[|\Bad_{S}\cap S_{0}|]=\sum_{s\in S_{0}}\Pr[s\in\Bad_{S}]\leq c\varepsilon\cdot|S_{0}|.

By Markov’s inequality, with probability
0.999
,

|\Bad_{S}\cap S_{0}|\leq 1000c\varepsilon|S_{0}|
.
Observe that
|S_{0}|=|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|
.
Therefore, with probability
0.999
,

|F|\leq|F^{*}|-1000c\varepsilon|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|+1000c\varepsilon|S_{0}|=|F^{*}|\leq k.

Property (c): cost of
F
.

We first show that
\cost_{k}(X,\overline{F}^{*})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\opt_{k}(X,Y)
by an averaging argument:
Consider removing from
F^{*}
a
random
subset of size
\varepsilon|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|
and denote the resulting (random) set to be
\widetilde{F}^{*}
.
We can obtain a similar bound as (
47
).

\displaystyle\dist(x,\widetilde{F}^{*})\leq\begin{cases}\dist(x,F^{*}),&\text{if }\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\in\widetilde{F}^{*};\\
3\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(x,S).&\text{if }\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\notin\widetilde{F}^{*}.\end{cases}

Moreover,
\pi_{F^{*}}(x)\notin\widetilde{F}^{*}
happens with probability at most
\varepsilon
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\E[\cost_{k}(X,\widetilde{F}^{*})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\E[\dist(x,\widetilde{F}^{*})]\leq\sum_{x\in X}\dist(x,F^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\cdot(3\dist(x,F^{*})+2\dist(x,S))

\displaystyle=(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Since
\overline{F}^{*}
minimizes the cost increase after removal, we have

\displaystyle\cost_{k}(X,\overline{F}^{*})\leq\E[\cost_{k}(X,\widetilde{F}^{*})]\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y).

We next show that
\cost_{k}(X,F)\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X,F^{*})
with probability
0.999
.
Note that in (
48
),
Moreover,
\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x)\notin F
only if
\pi_{S}(\pi_{\overline{F}^{*}}(x))\in\Bad_{S}
, which happens with probability
c\varepsilon
by
Lemma

5.3
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\E[\cost_{k}(X,F)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}\E[\dist(x,F)]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in X}\left[\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})+O(\varepsilon)(2\dist(x,\overline{F}^{*})+\dist(x,S))\right]

\displaystyle=(1+O(\varepsilon))\cost_{k}(X,\overline{F}^{*})+O(\varepsilon)\cost_{k}(X,S)

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y).

Applying Markov’s inequality over (non-negative) random variable
\cost_{k}(X,F)-\opt_{k}(X,Y)
, we have

\cost_{k}(X,F)\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\opt_{k}(X,Y)
holds with probability
0.999
.
Rescaling
\varepsilon
concludes the proof.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

7.5
: Cost of the Returned Solution

See
7.5

Consider an entry
(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
in the DP table.
Let
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
be the value computed by the dynamic program (
Equations

30
,
29
and
31
).
Let
G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
be the true value of that entry, i.e., the
minimum number of facilities required to be placed in
C
, such that the revealed cost inside
C
is at most
\Gamma_{C}
, which is formally defined in (
27
).
Recall that
G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
can be different from
g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})
, mainly because of the discretization of
\Gamma_{C}
into powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta}
.
We show in the following lemma that
G
can be two-sided bounded by
g
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
0\leq\ell\leq L
and
C\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
be a level
\ell
cluster.
Then for every configuration
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
, and
\Gamma_{C}\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
being powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
, it holds

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{\ell}\Gamma_{C})\leq G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})\leq g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C}),

for
\alpha_{\ell}=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\ell\cdot 2^{O(\ddim)}}
.

We use
Lemma

C.1
to prove
Lemma

7.5
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.5
.

Applying
Lemma

C.1
to
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{L}
and noting
\alpha_{L}=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2^{O(\ddim)}\log\Delta}\leq 1+\varepsilon
,
we have that for every configuration
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
and
\Gamma\in[\cost_{k}(X,S)/n,2\cost_{k}(X,S)]
,

g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},(1+\varepsilon)\Gamma)\leq G(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma)\leq g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma).

Recall our algorithm outputs the smallest
\Gamma
, such that there exists
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
with
g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma)\leq k
, and
\widehat{F}
is the solution corresponding to this
\Gamma
.
Therefore,
G(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma)\leq k
.
By the definition of
G
, this implies the optimal
k
-median value of
X^{\prime}
(under portal-respecting distance) is at most
\Gamma
.

By the minimality of
\Gamma
, for every
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
, we have
g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma/(1+\varepsilon^{\prime}))>k
.
Then
G(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\frac{\Gamma}{(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})(1+\varepsilon)})\geq g(X^{\prime},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\Gamma/(1+\varepsilon^{\prime}))>k
.
This implies the optimal
k
-median value of
X^{\prime}
(under portal-respecting distance) is greater than
\frac{\Gamma}{(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})(1+\varepsilon)}
.

We thus conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,\widehat{F})\leq\Gamma<(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})(1+\varepsilon)\min_{F\subseteq Y,|F|\leq k}\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}\ccostport(x,F)

Rescaling
\varepsilon
concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we prove
Lemma

C.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

C.1
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
\ell
.
Throughout, denote by
\tau:=|\child(C)|
for simplicity.

When
\ell=0
, it corresponds to the base cases of the DP, where the computation of
g
is accurate.
Hence,

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})\geq G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{0}\Gamma_{C})=g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{0}\Gamma_{C}).

Assume the inequality holds for level
\ell-1
, and consider any level
\ell
cluster
C
.
By the dynamic program (
Equations

29
,
30
and
31
),
there exists a sequence of values
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\dots,\Gamma_{\tau}
which are powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
and satisfy
\sum_{i}\Gamma_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}
, together with a sequence of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau}
, such that

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i}).

(49)

Since configurations
\{\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}\}
are consistent with
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
, and
\sum_{i}\Gamma_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})

By definition of
G

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})

Induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle\text{By \eqref{eqn:g_C_w_sum}}.

For the other direction, by the definition of
G
, there exists a sequence of values
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\dots,\Phi_{\tau}
which satisfy
\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}
, together with a sequence of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau}
, such that

G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Phi_{i}).

(50)

Note that
\Phi_{i}
’s are no longer necessarily powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
.
Let
R
be the operator that rounds every value
v
to the smallest power of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
greater than
v
, i.e.,
R(v)=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\lceil\log_{(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})}v\rceil}
.
Define
\Gamma_{i}:=R(\Phi_{i})
for every
i\in[\tau]
.
We have

\displaystyle G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Phi_{i})

By (
50
)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})

Since
\Gamma_{i}\geq\Phi_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i})

\displaystyle\text{Induction hypothesis}.

(51)

Denote
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}:=\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i}
for short.
Consider the following sequence of values, which are powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tau-1}=\Gamma_{\tau}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}=R(\Psi_{i+1}+\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}),\quad 0\leq i\leq\tau-2.

Note that
\{\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}\}
are consistent with
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
, and that
\Psi_{i+1}+\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}\leq\Psi_{i}
, we thus have

\displaystyle h(C,D_{\tau-1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau-1},\mathbf{b}_{\tau-1},\Psi_{\tau-1})\leq g(D_{\tau},\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau},\Gamma_{\tau}^{\prime});

\displaystyle h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Psi_{i})\leq h(C,D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Psi_{i+1})

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt+g(D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq\tau-2;

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Psi_{0}+(\Gamma_{C}-\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}))\leq h(C,D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Psi_{1})+g(D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}).

Summing over
0\leq i\leq\tau-1
, we have

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Psi_{0}+(\Gamma_{C}-\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

By (
51
).

(52)

Finally, note that

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=R(\Psi_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Psi_{1}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}

By the definition of
R

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}\Psi_{2}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\dots

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau-1}\Psi_{\tau-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{\tau-1}(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{i}\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{\tau-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau-1}\alpha_{\ell-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Gamma_{i}

Recall
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Phi_{i}

Since
\Gamma_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Phi_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{Since }\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{C}-(\Gamma_{C}-\Gamma_{C}^{\prime}).

We thus have

\Psi_{0}+(\Gamma_{C}-\Gamma_{C}^{\prime})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{C}\leq\alpha_{\ell}\Gamma_{C}.

Combining with (
52
), we have
G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})\geq g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{\ell}\Gamma_{C})
, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Missing Proofs in
Section

8

D.1
Proof of
Lemma

8.4

See
8.4

The proof is essentially the same as
Lemma

7.4
, so we only provide a sketch here.

Proof of
Lemma

6.6
(sketch).

Let
F^{*}\subseteq Y
be the optimal
k
-median solution.
Wlog, assume
|F^{*}|=|S|=k
, since otherwise we can add arbitrary facilities to them.
For
f\in F^{*}
, recall that
\pi_{S}(f)
is the point in
S
closest to
f
.
For
s\in S
, denote
S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)=\{f\in F^{*}\colon\pi_{S}(f)=s\}
.
Denote
S_{0}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|=0\}
,

S_{1}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|=1\}

and
S_{\geq 2}=\{s\in S\colon|S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)|\geq 2\}
.
Denote
S_{\geq 1}:=S_{1}\cup S_{\geq 2}
.
For
s\in S_{\geq 1}
, denote
f_{s}:=\argmin_{f\in S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)}\dist(f,s)
, i.e.,
f_{s}
is the closest point to
s
among
S^{-1}_{F^{*}}(s)
, breaking ties arbitrarily.
Denote
F_{S}:=\{f\in F^{*}\colon\exists s\in S,\text{ s.t. }f=f_{s}\}
.

Following
[
18
]
, the center set
F
is constructed as follows:

•

Step 1:
Among the centers in
F^{*}\setminus F_{S}
, remove from
F^{*}
a subset of size
1000c\varepsilon|F^{*}\setminus F_{S}|
that yields the minimum cost increase after removal, where
c
is the same constant in
Lemma

6.3
.
5
5

5

This step requires us to assume
\varepsilon\leq 1/(1000c)
. Since
1000c
is a constant, there is no loss of generality to make such assumption.

The resulting center set is denoted as
\overline{F}^{*}
.
Let
F=\overline{F}^{*}
.

•

Step 2:
For
s\in S_{0}
, if
s
is a bad center, then add
s
to
F
.

•

Step 3:
For
s\in S_{\geq 1}
, if
s
is a bad center, then add
s
to
F
, and remove
f_{s}
from
F
.

Formally,
F:=\overline{F}^{*}\setminus\{f_{s}\colon s\in\Bad_{S}\cap S_{\geq 1}\}\cup\Bad_{S}
.

The proofs of properties (a), (b) and (c) are the same as
Lemma

7.4
.
∎

Next, we prove
Lemma

D.1
.
The proof is almost the same as
Lemma

C.1
, except now we need to deal with ornaments in
\Schild(C)
.

Proof of
Lemma

D.1
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
\ell
.
Throughout, denote by
\tau:=|\child(C)|
for simplicity.

When
\ell=0
, it corresponds to the base cases of the DP, where the computation of
g
is accurate.
Hence,

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})\geq G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{0}\Gamma_{C})=g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{0}\Gamma_{C}).

Assume the inequality holds for level
\ell-1
, and consider any level
\ell
cluster
C
.
By the dynamic program (
44
) and (
45
),
there exists a sequence of values
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\dots,\Gamma_{\tau}
which are powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
and satisfy
\sum_{i}\Gamma_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}
, a sequence of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau}
,
and an integer
K=K(\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau})
which corresponds to the number of facilities in
\Schild(C)
, such that

g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})+K.

(53)

It is important to note that
K
only depends on the configurations
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau}
and does not depend on
\Gamma_{C}
and
\{\Gamma_{i}\}
.

Since configurations
\{\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}\}
and
K
are consistent with
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
, and
\sum_{i}\Gamma_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}
, we have

\displaystyle G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})+K

By definition of
G

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})+K

Induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle\text{By \eqref{eqn:g_C_w_sum_clients}}.

For the other direction, by the definition of
G
, there exists a sequence of values
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\dots,\Phi_{\tau}
which satisfy
\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}
,
a sequence of configurations
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau}
,
and an integer
K=K(\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau})
which corresponds to the number of facilities in
\Schild(C)
,
such that

G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Phi_{i})+K.

(54)

Note that
\Phi_{i}
’s are no longer necessarily powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
.
Let
R
be the operator that rounds every value
v
to the smallest power of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
greater than
v
, i.e.,
R(v)=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\lceil\log_{(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})}v\rceil}
.
Define
\Gamma_{i}:=R(\Phi_{i})
for every
i\in[\tau]
.
We have

\displaystyle G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Phi_{i})+K

By (
53
)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}G(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i})+K

Since
\Gamma_{i}\geq\Phi_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i})+K

\displaystyle\text{Induction hypothesis}.

(55)

Denote
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}:=\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i}
for short.
Consider the following sequence of values, which are powers of
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tau}=0,

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}=R(\Psi_{i+1}+\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}),\quad 0\leq i\leq\tau-1.

Note that
\{\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}\}
and
K
are consistent with
\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C}
, and that
\Psi_{i+1}+\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}\leq\Psi_{i}
, we thus have

\displaystyle h(C,D_{\tau},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{\tau},\mathbf{b}_{\tau},\Psi_{\tau})=K;

\displaystyle h(C,D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Psi_{i})\leq h(C,D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Psi_{i+1})

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt+g(D_{i+1},\mathbf{a}_{i+1},\mathbf{b}_{i+1},\Gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq\tau-1;

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Psi_{0})\leq h(C,D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Psi_{1})+g(D_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}).

Summing over
0\leq i\leq\tau
, we have

\displaystyle g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Psi_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}g(D_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i},\Gamma_{i}^{\prime})+K

\displaystyle\leq G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})

By (
55
).

(56)

Finally, note that

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=R(\Psi_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Psi_{1}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}

By the definition of
R

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}\Psi_{2}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\dots

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau-1}\Psi_{\tau-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{\tau-1}(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{i}\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{\tau-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau-1}\alpha_{\ell-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Gamma_{i}

Recall
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\Phi_{i}

Since
\Gamma_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\Phi_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{C}

\displaystyle\text{Since }\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}\leq\Gamma_{C}.

We thus have

\Psi_{0}\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\tau}\alpha_{\ell-1}\Gamma_{C}\leq\alpha_{\ell}\Gamma_{C}.

Combining with (
56
), we have
G(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\Gamma_{C})\geq g(C,\mathbf{a}_{C},\mathbf{b}_{C},\alpha_{\ell}\Gamma_{C})
, completing the proof.
∎
